IELTS: все 4 навыка

Reading — 100 текстов (800 вопросов) с новыми словами и заданиями · Listening — 50 аудиозаписей по 10 вопросов · Writing — актуальные Task 1 и Task 2 · Speaking — актуальные Part 1, 2, 3 (сентябрь–декабрь 2026). Составлено 15 ноября 2026.

Как пользоваться. Reading: просмотри «Новые слова», прочитай текст, ответь на вопросы и нажми «Проверить» — верные ответы подсветятся зелёным, ошибки красным с правильным вариантом. Listening: включи запись, отвечай во время прослушивания, транскрипт открывай только после проверки. Тексты и записи составлены специально для этого набора в формате IELTS; они короче экзаменационных (на экзамене текст Reading — 700–900 слов и 13–14 вопросов, секция Listening — 5–7 минут), поэтому на один текст закладывай 8–10 минут.

Reading — 100 passages

Типы заданий: True / False / Not Given, Yes / No / Not Given, Multiple choice, Sentence completion, Summary completion, Short answers, Matching information, Matching headings.

Passage 1. The Decline of the Honeybee Science & Nature 242 words

Новые слова
  • nectar — нектар
  • pollination — опыление
  • vanish — исчезать
  • hive — улей
  • parasite — паразит
  • pesticide — пестицид
  • navigate — ориентироваться, находить путь
  • meadow — луг

A Honeybees are among the most valuable insects on Earth. As they travel from flower to flower collecting nectar, they carry pollen with them, and this process of pollination allows plants to produce fruit and seeds. Roughly a third of the food that humans eat depends, directly or indirectly, on pollinators, and honeybees do a large share of that work.

B In the winter of 2006, beekeepers in the United States began reporting something strange. Worker bees were vanishing from their hives, leaving behind the queen, plenty of food and a few young bees. Very few dead bees were found nearby. Scientists named the phenomenon Colony Collapse Disorder, and similar losses were soon recorded in parts of Europe.

C No single cause has been identified. Most researchers now believe that several pressures act together. A tiny parasite, the varroa mite, feeds on bees and spreads viruses. Certain pesticides, even in small doses, appear to damage the bees' ability to navigate back to the hive. In addition, modern farming has replaced wild meadows with huge fields of a single crop, so bees have a poorer and less varied diet.

D There is some reason for optimism. Several countries have restricted the most harmful pesticides, and farmers are being paid to plant strips of wild flowers along the edges of their fields. City residents are helping too: urban hives on rooftops often do surprisingly well, because gardens and parks offer a wide range of flowers throughout the year.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Honeybees are responsible for all of the pollination that food crops require. ✔ FALSE
2 When colonies collapsed, large numbers of dead bees were usually found close to the hive. ✔ FALSE
3 Colony Collapse Disorder was first noticed in Europe. ✔ FALSE
4 Varroa mites were accidentally brought to the United States from Asia. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

5 According to the passage, modern farming harms bees mainly because it
  • A uses too much water.
  • B reduces the variety of their food.
  • C destroys their hives directly.
  • D attracts more parasites.
 ✔ B
6 Urban hives are often successful because
  • A cities are warmer than the countryside.
  • B there are no pesticides in cities.
  • C city plants flower across the whole year.
  • D city beekeepers are better trained.
 ✔ C

Complete the sentences. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

7 Some pesticides seem to harm the bees' ability to home. ✔ navigate
8 Farmers receive money for planting at the borders of fields. ✔ wild flowers

Passage 2. Coral Reefs Under Pressure Science & Nature 248 words

Новые слова
  • marine — морской
  • skeleton — скелет
  • accumulate — накапливаться
  • algae — водоросли
  • shallow — мелкий, неглубокий
  • fragile — хрупкий
  • expel — выбрасывать, изгонять
  • bleaching — обесцвечивание

A Coral reefs cover less than one per cent of the ocean floor, yet they are home to around a quarter of all marine species. For this reason they are often called the rainforests of the sea. A reef may look like rock, but it is built by millions of tiny animals called polyps, which produce a hard skeleton of limestone. Over thousands of years these skeletons accumulate into enormous structures.

B Corals get their colour, and most of their food, from microscopic algae that live inside their tissue. The relationship benefits both sides: the algae receive shelter, and in return they supply the coral with energy made from sunlight. This is why reefs grow only in clear, shallow water where light can reach them.

C The partnership is fragile, however. When sea temperatures rise by just one or two degrees for several weeks, the stressed coral expels its algae and turns white, an event known as bleaching. A bleached coral is not dead, and it can recover if the water cools quickly. If the heat continues, though, it slowly starves.

D Reefs matter to people as well as to fish. They protect coastlines by breaking the force of waves, and hundreds of millions of people rely on them for food or for income from tourism. Scientists are now experimenting with growing heat-resistant corals in nurseries and planting them on damaged reefs. Such projects are promising, but most experts agree that they cannot succeed unless the warming of the oceans is slowed.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Coral reefs occupy a very small proportion of the sea bed. ✔ TRUE
2 Polyps are a type of plant. ✔ FALSE
3 Bleached corals always die within a few weeks. ✔ FALSE
4 The largest reef in the world is located near Australia. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Complete the summary. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

5 Corals obtain most of their food from tiny living in their tissue. ✔ algae
6 In exchange for energy, the coral gives them . ✔ shelter
7 Because the process needs , reefs are found only in clear, shallow water. ✔ sunlight/light

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

8 What is the writer's view of coral nurseries?
  • A They are a waste of money.
  • B They will solve the problem on their own.
  • C They are useful but not sufficient.
  • D They damage healthy reefs.
 ✔ C

Passage 3. The Intelligence of the Octopus Science & Nature 243 words

Новые слова
  • relative — родственник
  • secure — надёжный, безопасный
  • neuron — нейрон
  • squeeze — протискиваться
  • beak — клюв
  • disguise — маскировка
  • texture — текстура, фактура
  • remarkable — поразительный

A The octopus is one of the strangest clever animals on the planet. Its closest relatives are slow creatures such as snails and clams, yet it can solve puzzles, open jars and escape from tanks that were thought to be secure. Aquarium staff around the world tell stories of octopuses that climbed out at night, ate fish in a neighbouring tank and returned before morning.

B Its body is as unusual as its behaviour. An octopus has three hearts and blue blood, and about two thirds of its neurons are located not in its brain but in its eight arms. Each arm can taste what it touches and can carry out some movements without instructions from the brain. Because the animal has no bones, it can squeeze through any gap larger than its hard beak.

C Octopuses are also masters of disguise. Special cells in the skin allow them to change colour and even texture in less than a second, so that they resemble sand, rock or seaweed. Curiously, experiments suggest that they are colour-blind, and scientists still do not fully understand how they match their surroundings so precisely.

D Perhaps the most surprising fact is how short their lives are. Most species live for only one or two years, and they grow up without any contact with their parents. Unlike apes or dolphins, therefore, an octopus cannot learn from older animals. Everything it knows, it has discovered alone, which makes its intelligence all the more remarkable.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Octopuses are closely related to snails. ✔ TRUE
2 Most of an octopus's neurons are found in its brain. ✔ FALSE
3 An octopus's arms are able to sense taste. ✔ TRUE
4 Octopuses in aquariums are fed only at night. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Answer the questions. Write NO MORE THAN THREE WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

5 What is the only hard part of an octopus's body mentioned in the passage? ✔ beak/its beak/hard beak
6 How long do most octopus species live? ✔ one or two years/1 or 2 years/two years

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

  • A How quickly the skin changes.
  • B How the animal copies colours it apparently cannot see.
  • C Why it copies seaweed.
  • D Why only some species can do it.
 ✔ B
8 The writer finds octopus intelligence especially impressive because octopuses
  • A have larger brains than dolphins.
  • B are taught by their parents very quickly.
  • C learn everything by themselves.
  • D live for a long time.
 ✔ C

Passage 4. How Birds Find Their Way Science & Nature 249 words

Новые слова
  • breeding grounds — места гнездования
  • astonishing — поразительный
  • steer — держать курс, управлять
  • orient — ориентироваться
  • detect — обнаруживать, улавливать
  • landmark — ориентир
  • predator — хищник
  • wetland — водно-болотное угодье

A Every autumn, billions of birds leave their breeding grounds and fly to warmer regions, returning in spring. Some of these journeys are astonishing. The Arctic tern travels from the Arctic to the Antarctic and back each year, a round trip of more than 70,000 kilometres, while the bar-tailed godwit can fly for over a week across the Pacific Ocean without landing once.

B How birds navigate over such distances puzzled naturalists for centuries. It is now clear that they use several methods at the same time. During the day many species use the position of the sun, adjusting for the time with an internal clock. At night they steer by the stars. In a famous experiment in the 1960s, young birds raised in a planetarium learned to orient themselves by the pattern of the artificial night sky.

C Birds can also detect the Earth's magnetic field, although exactly how they do so is still debated. One theory suggests that light-sensitive molecules in the eye allow a bird literally to see the field. Closer to home, birds switch to landmarks such as rivers, coastlines and mountain ranges, and some seabirds even appear to use smell.

D Migration is risky. Storms, hunger and predators kill many birds on the way, and human activity has added new dangers. Brightly lit cities confuse birds that fly at night, and the wetlands where they stop to rest and feed are disappearing. Protecting these stopover sites, researchers say, is as important as protecting the places where birds nest.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 The bar-tailed godwit rests on the water during its Pacific crossing. ✔ FALSE
2 Birds rely on a single method of navigation. ✔ FALSE
3 The planetarium experiment showed that birds can learn star patterns. ✔ TRUE
4 Scientists agree on how birds sense the magnetic field. ✔ FALSE

Which paragraph contains the following information? Choose the correct letter.

5 a description of dangers created by people ✔ D
6 examples of extremely long flights ✔ A
7 a reference to research carried out indoors ✔ B

Complete the sentences. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

8 Near the end of their journey, birds find their way using such as rivers and coastlines. ✔ landmarks

Passage 5. Living with Volcanoes Science & Nature 244 words

Новые слова
  • crust — кора (земная)
  • molten — расплавленный
  • erupt — извергаться
  • catastrophic — катастрофический
  • bury — погребать, засыпать
  • fertile — плодородный
  • slope — склон
  • prediction — прогноз, предсказание

A Volcanoes are openings in the Earth's crust through which molten rock, ash and gas escape. Most are found along the edges of the tectonic plates that make up the planet's surface, especially around the Pacific Ocean in a zone known as the Ring of Fire. There are about 1,350 potentially active volcanoes on land, and around fifty of them erupt in a typical year.

B Eruptions can be catastrophic. In AD 79 Mount Vesuvius buried the Roman town of Pompeii under several metres of ash. In 1815 the eruption of Tambora in Indonesia threw so much dust into the atmosphere that temperatures fell around the world, and 1816 became known in Europe and North America as the year without a summer. Crops failed and many people went hungry.

C Given such dangers, it may seem surprising that hundreds of millions of people choose to live close to volcanoes. The explanation lies largely in the soil. Volcanic ash is rich in minerals, and over time it breaks down into some of the most fertile farmland on Earth. The slopes of volcanoes in Indonesia, Italy and Central America have been intensively farmed for centuries. Volcanic regions also provide geothermal energy and attract tourists.

D Today scientists can often tell when an eruption is approaching. They measure small earthquakes, changes in the shape of the mountain and the gases escaping from it. Prediction is still far from exact, but timely warnings have saved many thousands of lives in recent decades.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Most volcanoes are located in the middle of tectonic plates. ✔ FALSE
2 About fifty volcanoes erupt on land in an average year. ✔ TRUE
3 The eruption of Tambora affected the weather in other continents. ✔ TRUE
4 More people died at Pompeii than at Tambora. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

  • A cheap housing.
  • B productive soil.
  • C lack of space elsewhere.
  • D the mild climate.
 ✔ B
6 What does the writer say about predicting eruptions?
  • A It is now completely reliable.
  • B It is impossible.
  • C It is imperfect but valuable.
  • D It relies only on measuring gases.
 ✔ C

Complete the sentences. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

Passage 6. The Deep Sea: Earth's Last Frontier Science & Nature 250 words

Новые слова
  • fade — тускнеть, угасать
  • dim — тусклый
  • crush — раздавить
  • assume — предполагать
  • hot spring — горячий источник
  • prey — добыча
  • scarce — скудный, редкий
  • trench — впадина, жёлоб

A More than half of our planet is covered by water that is over 3,000 metres deep, yet we have better maps of the surface of Mars than of our own ocean floor. Sunlight fades quickly in the sea: below about 200 metres there is only a dim blue glow, and beneath 1,000 metres the darkness is total. The water there is close to freezing, and the pressure would crush an unprotected human instantly.

B For a long time scientists assumed that almost nothing could survive in such conditions. That view changed in 1977, when researchers in a small submarine near the Galápagos Islands discovered hot springs on the seabed, known as hydrothermal vents. Around them lived dense communities of giant tube worms, clams and crabs. These animals depend not on sunlight but on bacteria that obtain energy from chemicals in the hot water.

C Many deep-sea creatures produce their own light, an ability called bioluminescence. They use it to attract prey, to find a mate or to confuse an attacker. Food is scarce in the depths, so animals there tend to move slowly and live for a long time; some deep-sea fish are thought to reach an age of more than a hundred years.

D The deep ocean is no longer beyond human reach. Plastic waste has been found in its deepest trenches, and companies now hope to mine the seabed for metals used in batteries. Many biologists argue that this should not begin until we know far more about what lives there.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 The ocean floor has been mapped in greater detail than Mars. ✔ FALSE
2 There is no light at all below 1,000 metres. ✔ TRUE
3 The hydrothermal vents were discovered by an unmanned robot. ✔ FALSE
4 Tube worms are the largest animals living near the vents. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Complete the summary. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

5 Animals at hydrothermal vents rely on rather than sunlight. ✔ bacteria
6 These organisms get their energy from in the hot water. ✔ chemicals

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

7 Why do deep-sea animals tend to move slowly?
  • A The water is too cold.
  • B There is little to eat.
  • C The pressure is too great.
  • D They cannot see.
 ✔ B
8 What do many biologists think about deep-sea mining?
  • A It should be banned for ever.
  • B It is harmless.
  • C It should wait until more research is done.
  • D It will reduce plastic pollution.
 ✔ C

Passage 7. Why We Sleep Science & Nature 250 words

Новые слова
  • intense — интенсивный
  • vivid — яркий, живой
  • tissue — ткань (организма)
  • release — выделять, высвобождать
  • fluid — жидкость
  • storage — хранение, хранилище
  • delay — задерживать
  • mood — настроение

A Humans spend about a third of their lives asleep, and for a long time this seemed like wasted time. Modern research suggests the opposite: sleep is a period of intense activity for the brain and body, and going without it is quickly harmful.

B A night's sleep is made up of cycles lasting roughly ninety minutes. Each cycle includes light sleep, deep sleep and a stage known as REM, during which the eyes move rapidly and most vivid dreams occur. In deep sleep the body repairs tissue and releases growth hormone. During the night the brain also appears to wash itself: fluid flows through it more freely and carries away waste products that build up during the day.

C Sleep is essential for memory as well. Studies show that people who sleep after learning something remember it far better than those who stay awake. The sleeping brain replays the day's experiences and transfers important information into long-term storage, while weaker memories are allowed to fade.

D Most adults need between seven and nine hours a night, although teenagers need more. Yet in many countries people are sleeping less than they did fifty years ago. Artificial light is partly responsible. The blue light from phone and computer screens delays the release of melatonin, the hormone that makes us feel sleepy. Regular lack of sleep has been linked to weight gain, heart disease and low mood. Doctors therefore advise keeping the bedroom dark and cool, and switching off screens at least an hour before bed.

Do the following statements agree with the views of the writer? Choose YES, NO or NOT GIVEN.

1 Sleeping is an unproductive use of time. ✔ NO
2 The brain removes waste more effectively at night. ✔ YES
3 Dreams help people to solve practical problems. ✔ NOT GIVEN
4 Screens should be avoided shortly before going to bed. ✔ YES

Complete the sentences. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

5 One complete cycle of sleep lasts about minutes. ✔ ninety/90
6 The hormone that causes sleepiness is called . ✔ melatonin

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

7 According to the passage, what happens to memories during sleep?
  • A All memories are stored permanently.
  • B Important ones are stored and weaker ones fade.
  • C Memories from the day are erased.
  • D Only dreams are remembered.
 ✔ B
8 Which group is said to need more sleep than adults?
  • A babies
  • B the elderly
  • C teenagers
  • D athletes
 ✔ C

Passage 8. The Moon and the Tides Science & Nature 265 words

Новые слова
  • creep — медленно подкрадываться, ползти
  • retreat — отступать
  • gravity — гравитация, сила тяжести
  • bulge — выпуклость; выпячиваться
  • rotate — вращаться
  • funnel — направлять (как в воронку)
  • grind — молоть
  • predictable — предсказуемый

A Anyone who has spent a day at the seaside has seen the water creep up the beach and then retreat again. Along most coasts this happens twice a day, and the cause lies 384,000 kilometres away. The gravity of the Moon pulls on the Earth, and because water moves easily, the oceans bulge slightly towards the Moon. A second bulge forms on the opposite side of the planet. As the Earth rotates, a coastline passes through both bulges, producing two high tides and two low tides roughly every twenty-five hours.

B The Sun also influences the tides, though its effect is less than half that of the Moon because it is so much farther away. When the Sun, Earth and Moon are in a line, at full moon and new moon, their forces combine to create especially large tides called spring tides. The name has nothing to do with the season. When the Sun and Moon pull at right angles, the result is the weaker neap tide.

C The shape of the coast makes an enormous difference. In the open ocean the water rises by less than a metre, but in narrow bays it can be funnelled to great heights. In the Bay of Fundy in Canada the difference between high and low tide can exceed fifteen metres.

D Tides have long been useful to people. Fishermen and sailors plan their work around them, and tidal mills were grinding grain in Europe over a thousand years ago. Today engineers are building turbines that generate electricity from tidal currents, a source of energy that, unlike wind, is entirely predictable.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Most coasts experience two high tides in about twenty-five hours. ✔ TRUE
2 The Sun has a stronger effect on tides than the Moon. ✔ FALSE
3 Spring tides occur only in the spring. ✔ FALSE
4 The Bay of Fundy has the highest tides in the world. ✔ NOT GIVEN

  • vi. Measuring the distance to the Moon

Passage 9. Bamboo: The Giant Grass Science & Nature 268 words

Новые слова
  • species — вид (биол.)
  • stem — стебель
  • hollow — полый
  • joint — узел, соединение
  • scaffolding — строительные леса
  • disastrous — катастрофический, губительный
  • harvest — собирать урожай
  • timber — древесина

A Bamboo looks like a tree, but botanically it is a grass. There are well over a thousand species, ranging from plants a few centimetres tall to giants that reach thirty metres. Most grow in the warm, wet regions of Asia, South America and Africa. Bamboo is famous for its speed: under ideal conditions certain species can grow almost a metre in a single day, faster than any other plant.

B A bamboo stem is hollow and divided into sections by solid joints. This structure makes it both light and extremely strong; weight for weight, it can resist stretching better than steel. In much of Asia it has been used for centuries to build houses, bridges and furniture, and in Hong Kong workers still construct bamboo scaffolding around skyscrapers.

C One of the plant's strangest features is the way it flowers. Many species flower only once, after several decades, and then die. Remarkably, all the plants of the same species often flower in the same year, even when they are growing on different continents. For animals that depend on bamboo, the result can be disastrous. Giant pandas, which eat almost nothing else, have starved when the bamboo in their area died off.

D Bamboo is now attracting interest as an environmentally friendly material. It can be harvested after three to five years, compared with decades for most timber, and it grows back from its roots without replanting. It is being turned into flooring, paper and fabric. Critics point out, however, that producing soft bamboo fabric usually requires strong chemicals, so the finished product is not always as green as its label suggests.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

2 All species of bamboo grow nearly a metre a day. ✔ FALSE
3 Bamboo scaffolding is still used on tall buildings in Hong Kong. ✔ TRUE
4 Bamboo houses last longer than houses built of timber. ✔ NOT GIVEN

  • B Plants of one species flower at the same time worldwide.
  • C Only the tallest species flower.
  • D The flowers are poisonous to pandas.
 ✔ B

Passage 10. How Memory Works Science & Nature 251 words

Новые слова
  • reconstruction — воссоздание, реконструкция
  • recall — вспоминать
  • distinguish — различать
  • rehearse — повторять, репетировать
  • syllable — слог
  • interval — промежуток
  • distort — искажать
  • testimony — показания (свидетеля)

A We tend to think of memory as a kind of video recording that can be played back whenever we wish. Psychologists have shown that this picture is wrong. Remembering is an active process of reconstruction, and each time we recall an event we rebuild it, sometimes adding details that were never there.

B Researchers usually distinguish three stages. Sensory memory holds what we see and hear for a second or two. A small part of this passes into short-term memory, which can keep about seven items for less than half a minute unless we repeat them. Only information that is rehearsed, or that matters to us emotionally, is transferred to long-term memory, where it may last a lifetime.

C The German psychologist Hermann Ebbinghaus studied forgetting in the 1880s by memorising lists of meaningless syllables. He found that most forgetting happens very quickly, within the first day, and then slows down. He also discovered that reviewing material at increasing intervals, a technique now called spaced repetition, is far more effective than studying it many times in one session.

D Because memories are rebuilt, they can be distorted. In a well-known experiment by Elizabeth Loftus, people watched a film of a car accident. Those who were later asked how fast the cars were going when they 'smashed' into each other gave higher estimates than those who heard the word 'hit', and some even remembered broken glass that had not been shown. Such findings have made courts more cautious about relying on the testimony of eyewitnesses.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Psychologists believe memory works like a video recorder. ✔ FALSE
2 Short-term memory can hold information for several minutes without repetition. ✔ FALSE
3 Ebbinghaus used real words in his experiments. ✔ FALSE
4 Loftus's experiment was repeated in several countries. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Complete the sentences. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

5 Ebbinghaus found that the greatest amount of forgetting occurs during the first . ✔ day
6 Reviewing at increasing intervals is known as . ✔ spaced repetition

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

7 What did the Loftus experiment demonstrate?
  • A People forget accidents quickly.
  • B The wording of a question can change what people remember.
  • C Films are remembered better than real events.
  • D Eyewitnesses are usually accurate.
 ✔ B
8 Which information is most likely to enter long-term memory?
  • A anything we see for a second
  • B lists of seven items
  • C things that are emotionally important
  • D meaningless syllables
 ✔ C

Passage 11. The Invention of Paper History 254 words

Новые слова
  • drawback — недостаток
  • damp — влажный, сырой
  • soak — замачивать
  • pulp — бумажная масса, пульпа
  • fragment — обрывок, фрагмент
  • craft — ремесло
  • durable — прочный, долговечный
  • literacy — грамотность

A Before paper existed, people wrote on whatever was available: clay tablets in Mesopotamia, papyrus in Egypt, and bamboo strips or silk in China. Each had drawbacks. Bamboo was heavy, silk was costly, and papyrus cracked easily in damp climates.

B According to Chinese tradition, paper was invented in AD 105 by Cai Lun, an official at the imperial court. He is said to have soaked tree bark, old rags and fishing nets in water, beaten them into a pulp and spread the mixture on a screen to dry. Archaeologists have since found fragments of paper that are two centuries older, so Cai Lun probably improved an existing method rather than creating it. Even so, his version was cheap and light, and it spread rapidly through China.

C For several hundred years the technique remained in East Asia. Its journey westwards is traditionally linked to a battle near the Talas River in Central Asia in 751, after which captured Chinese papermakers are said to have taught their craft in Samarkand. The city soon became famous for its fine paper. From there the knowledge passed to Baghdad, Cairo and, by the twelfth century, to Spain.

D Europeans at first distrusted the new material, considering it less durable than parchment, which was made from animal skin. Opinions changed when printing with movable type appeared in the fifteenth century. A single large Bible on parchment required the skins of about 170 calves; paper made mass production possible. Books became cheaper, literacy rose, and ideas began to travel faster than ever before.

Passage 12. The Silk Road History 249 words

Новые слова
  • network — сеть
  • merchant — купец, торговец
  • caravan — караван
  • inn — постоялый двор
  • oasis — оазис
  • commerce — торговля
  • scholar — учёный
  • cargo — груз

A The Silk Road was never a single road. It was a network of trade routes, stretching more than 6,000 kilometres, that linked China with the Mediterranean from about the second century BC. The name itself is modern: it was invented in 1877 by the German geographer Ferdinand von Richthofen.

B Few merchants travelled the whole distance. Goods were passed from one trader to another in a long chain, and their price rose at every stage. Silk went west, where wealthy Romans paid enormous sums for it, while glass, silver and wool moved east. Spices, paper, horses and precious stones were exchanged along the way. Caravans of camels crossed deserts and mountain passes, resting at roadside inns called caravanserais, which were built roughly a day's journey apart.

C The great oasis cities of Central Asia, among them Samarkand, Bukhara and Merv, grew rich as meeting points. They were centres not only of commerce but also of learning, where scholars studied astronomy, medicine and mathematics. Ideas travelled as easily as goods. Buddhism reached China along these routes, and Islam later spread eastwards in the same way. Unfortunately, so did disease: many historians believe the plague that devastated Europe in the fourteenth century arrived along the trade routes from Asia.

D The overland trade declined after the fifteenth century. European sailors found sea routes to Asia, and ships could carry far more cargo than camels at a lower cost. The old cities lost much of their importance, although their magnificent buildings still attract visitors today.

Passage 13. Gutenberg and the Printing Press History 258 words

Новые слова
  • scribe — писец, переписчик
  • goldsmith — ювелир, золотых дел мастер
  • carve — вырезать
  • movable type — подвижные литеры
  • impractical — непрактичный
  • mould — форма для литья
  • profit — получать прибыль
  • profound — глубокий, значительный

A Until the middle of the fifteenth century, every book in Europe was copied by hand. A scribe might spend a year producing one Bible, and books were so expensive that only churches, universities and the very rich could own them. Mistakes crept in with each new copy.

B Johannes Gutenberg, a goldsmith from the German city of Mainz, changed this. Printing itself was not new: the Chinese had printed from carved wooden blocks for centuries and had even experimented with movable type made of clay. But the thousands of characters in written Chinese made that system impractical. European alphabets, with only a couple of dozen letters, were far better suited to it. Gutenberg's achievement was to combine several elements: metal letters that could be cast quickly in a mould, an oil-based ink that stuck to metal, and a press adapted from those used to make wine.

C Around 1455 he completed his famous Bible, of which about 180 copies were printed. Sadly, Gutenberg did not profit from his work. He had borrowed heavily, and his business partner took him to court and gained control of the workshop.

D The invention itself spread with extraordinary speed. By 1500 there were printing presses in more than 250 European towns, and millions of books had been produced. The effects were profound. Scientists could share accurate copies of their results, religious reformers could reach huge audiences, and ordinary languages such as German, French and English began to replace Latin in print. Some historians regard printing as the most important invention of the last thousand years.

4 Gutenberg became wealthy as a result of his Bible. ✔ FALSE

Passage 14. Building the Great Pyramid History 269 words

Новые слова
  • align — выравнивать
  • monument — памятник, монумент
  • crane — подъёмный кран
  • excavation — раскопки
  • labourer — рабочий
  • quarry — каменоломня, карьер
  • sledge — сани, волокуша
  • ramp — пандус, наклонный настил

A The Great Pyramid of Giza was built for the pharaoh Khufu around 2560 BC. It originally stood about 146 metres high and remained the tallest structure made by humans for nearly four thousand years. It contains roughly 2.3 million blocks of stone, most weighing more than two tonnes, and its sides are aligned almost perfectly with the points of the compass.

B How such a monument was built without iron tools, wheels for heavy transport or cranes has fascinated people for centuries. The Greek writer Herodotus, who visited Egypt some two thousand years after the pyramid was finished, claimed that 100,000 slaves had worked on it. Modern archaeology tells a different story. Excavations near the site have revealed a workers' town with bakeries, breweries and even medical facilities. The builders were not slaves but paid labourers and farmers, perhaps 20,000 to 30,000 of them, who were well fed with bread, beer and meat.

C Most of the limestone was cut in quarries close by. The finer white stone for the outer surface came from across the Nile, and granite for the inner chambers was floated down the river from Aswan, some 800 kilometres to the south. In 2013 archaeologists found the diary of an official named Merer, the oldest inscribed papyrus ever discovered, which describes boat crews delivering stone to Giza.

D The blocks were probably dragged on wooden sledges up ramps made of mud brick and rubble. A wall painting shows a man pouring water on the sand in front of a sledge; experiments have confirmed that wet sand cuts the force needed to pull a heavy load by about half.

Passage 15. A Short History of Tea History 264 words

Новые слова
  • legend — легенда
  • monk — монах
  • elaborate — сложный, изысканный
  • luxury — роскошь
  • smuggle — провозить контрабандой
  • dependence — зависимость
  • botanist — ботаник
  • expose — подвергать воздействию

A After water, tea is the most widely consumed drink in the world. According to legend it was discovered in 2737 BC, when leaves from a wild bush blew into a pot of water being boiled for the Chinese emperor Shennong. Whatever the truth, tea was certainly being drunk in China more than two thousand years ago, at first as a medicine and later for pleasure.

B Buddhist monks, who valued tea because it kept them awake during long hours of meditation, carried it to Japan, where preparing and serving it developed into an elaborate ceremony. Tea reached Europe much later, brought by Dutch and Portuguese traders in the early seventeenth century. In Britain it was at first a luxury, heavily taxed and kept in locked boxes. So much was smuggled into the country that the government finally cut the tax sharply in 1784, and tea became the everyday drink of all classes.

C Britain's thirst created a problem: China would accept only silver in payment. To break this dependence, the British East India Company began growing tea in India. In 1848 it sent the Scottish botanist Robert Fortune to China, where, disguised as a local merchant, he secretly collected thousands of tea plants and seeds and took them to India.

D All true tea comes from one plant, Camellia sinensis. Whether it becomes green, oolong or black depends on how long the leaves are exposed to the air before they are dried. Today the largest producers are China and India, followed by Kenya and Sri Lanka, and around two billion people begin their day with a cup.

Passage 16. Measuring Time History 267 words

Новые слова
  • dusk — сумерки
  • device — устройство
  • drip — капать
  • monastery — монастырь
  • pendulum — маятник
  • latitude — широта
  • longitude — долгота
  • voyage — морское путешествие

A For most of history people had little need for exact time. Farmers rose with the sun and worked until dusk. The earliest timekeeping devices were shadow clocks: the Egyptians used tall stone obelisks, and later sundials, to divide the day into parts. These were useless at night or in cloudy weather, so water clocks were developed, in which water dripped at a steady rate from one container into another.

B Mechanical clocks appeared in European monasteries and towns around 1300. They were driven by falling weights and had no minute hand, since they could gain or lose a quarter of an hour a day. A major advance came in 1656, when the Dutch scientist Christiaan Huygens built the first pendulum clock, reducing the error to a few seconds a day.

C Accurate time was most urgently needed at sea. Sailors could find their latitude from the sun, but to calculate longitude they had to know the precise time at their home port, and pendulum clocks did not work on a rolling ship. After many shipwrecks the British government offered a large prize for a solution. It was eventually won by John Harrison, a self-taught carpenter, whose marine chronometer of 1759 lost only about five seconds on a voyage of several weeks.

D Even then every town kept its own local time, set by the sun. It was the railways that forced a change, since timetables were impossible without a common standard. Britain adopted a single railway time in the 1840s, and in 1884 an international conference divided the world into time zones based on the line through Greenwich in London.

3 John Harrison had formal training as a scientist. ✔ FALSE
4 Harrison received his prize money immediately. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 17. Roman Roads History 266 words

Новые слова
  • paved — мощёный
  • milestone — мильный камень, веха
  • march — маршировать
  • trench — траншея, ров
  • gravel — гравий
  • ditch — канава
  • maintain — поддерживать в порядке, обслуживать
  • surveyor — землемер, геодезист

A At the height of its power the Roman Empire was held together by about 80,000 kilometres of paved roads, reaching from Britain to Syria. The first major one, the Appian Way, was begun in 312 BC to link Rome with the south of Italy. The famous saying that all roads lead to Rome was close to the truth: distances across the empire were measured from a golden milestone in the centre of the capital.

B The roads were built primarily for the army. Soldiers needed to march quickly to wherever trouble arose, and it was usually the soldiers themselves who did the building. Roman engineers preferred straight lines, cutting through hills and bridging valleys rather than going around them. A trench was dug and filled with layers of sand, gravel and crushed stone, then topped with tightly fitted flat stones. The surface was slightly curved so that rainwater ran off into ditches at the sides.

C Although designed for military purposes, the roads soon carried merchants, officials and ordinary travellers. Stone markers showed the distance to the next town, and inns stood at regular intervals. The government ran a postal service in which riders changed horses at relay stations; an urgent message could cover 80 kilometres or more in a day, a speed not matched in Europe until the nineteenth century.

D After the empire collapsed in the fifth century, the roads were no longer maintained, and many were robbed of their stone for other buildings. Yet their routes survived. A number of modern European highways follow exactly the lines that Roman surveyors laid out two thousand years ago.

Passage 18. The Story of Maps History 265 words

Новые слова
  • tablet — табличка
  • disc — диск
  • abandon — отказываться, бросать
  • sphere — сфера, шар
  • grid — сетка
  • preserve — сохранять
  • distortion — искажение
  • equator — экватор

A A map is a way of seeing the world from above, something no human could actually do until the age of flight. The oldest known world map, a clay tablet from Babylon made around 600 BC, shows the earth as a flat disc surrounded by water, with Babylon itself at the centre. Placing one's own homeland in the middle is a habit that mapmakers have rarely abandoned.

B The ancient Greeks took a more scientific approach. They knew the earth was a sphere, and in the second century AD Ptolemy, working in Alexandria, proposed a grid of lines of latitude and longitude to fix the position of any place. His work was forgotten in Europe for a thousand years but was preserved and improved by scholars in the Islamic world.

C Medieval European maps were often religious pictures rather than practical guides, and they were of little use to sailors. This changed when voyages of exploration began. In 1569 the Flemish mapmaker Gerardus Mercator solved a serious problem for navigators: on his map a ship's constant compass course appeared as a straight line. The price was distortion. Because a globe cannot be flattened without stretching, regions far from the equator look much larger than they are. On a Mercator map Greenland seems about the same size as Africa, although Africa is in fact around fourteen times bigger.

D Today satellites photograph every corner of the planet, and digital maps are updated daily. Yet a map is still a selection. Someone decides what to show and what to leave out, and those choices shape how we imagine the world.

4 Digital maps are more objective than paper maps. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 19. Salt: The Mineral that Shaped History History 276 words

Новые слова
  • scarce — дефицитный, редкий
  • refrigeration — охлаждение
  • preserve — консервировать, сохранять
  • allowance — денежное довольствие
  • grievance — недовольство, жалоба
  • monopoly — монополия
  • slab — плита, брусок
  • deposit — залежь, месторождение

A Today salt is so cheap that restaurants give it away, and doctors warn us against eating too much of it. For most of human history the situation was very different. Salt was scarce, valuable and at times literally worth fighting for.

B Its importance came from a simple fact: before refrigeration, salting was one of the few ways of preserving food. Meat and fish packed in salt could be kept through the winter or carried on long voyages. Armies could not march and ships could not sail without it. Humans and their animals also need a certain amount of salt simply to stay alive.

C Because everyone needed it, salt was an ideal thing to tax. Chinese emperors controlled its sale more than two thousand years ago. Roman soldiers were sometimes given an allowance to buy salt, and this is the origin of the English word 'salary'. In France a hated salt tax, the gabelle, was among the grievances that led to the revolution of 1789. In 1930 Mahatma Gandhi led thousands of Indians on a 390-kilometre walk to the sea to make their own salt, in protest against the British monopoly.

D Salt also created wealth. The city of Venice grew rich partly through its salt trade, and in West Africa caravans carried slabs of salt across the Sahara to Timbuktu, where it was at times exchanged for gold. Things changed in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. New drilling methods revealed vast underground deposits, while canning and freezing reduced the need for salted food. Today the bulk of the salt produced worldwide is used not in kitchens but in the chemical industry and for melting ice on roads.

Passage 20. Lighthouses History 279 words

Новые слова
  • harbour — гавань
  • wreck — разбивать (судно); крушение
  • swept away (sweep away) — сметать, уносить
  • interlocking — сцепляющийся
  • trunk — ствол
  • lens — линза
  • beam — луч
  • isolation — изоляция, уединение

A For as long as people have gone to sea, they have needed guidance to reach harbour safely at night. The earliest signals were simply fires lit on hilltops. The most famous lighthouse of the ancient world was the Pharos of Alexandria in Egypt, completed around 280 BC. It was probably more than 100 metres tall, and it was counted among the seven wonders of the world. It guided ships for some 1,500 years before earthquakes finally brought it down.

B Building on land was one thing; building on rocks out at sea was far harder. The Eddystone rocks off the south-west coast of England had wrecked countless ships when Henry Winstanley put up a wooden tower there in 1698. He was so confident of it that he wished to be inside during the greatest storm ever known. In 1703 he got his wish, and both tower and builder were swept away. The fourth Eddystone lighthouse, designed by John Smeaton in 1759, was made of interlocking blocks of granite and shaped like the trunk of an oak tree. It became the model for lighthouses everywhere.

C Light was as much of a challenge as structure. Early lamps burned coal, candles or oil and were visible for only a few kilometres. In 1822 the French physicist Augustin Fresnel invented a lens made of rings of glass prisms, which gathered the light into a single powerful beam that could be seen more than thirty kilometres away.

D For centuries lighthouses were looked after by keepers, who lived in isolation for weeks on end. Automation has made the job disappear. Satellite navigation has reduced the need for lighthouses, yet most remain in service as a backup.

4 Lighthouse keepers were well paid for their work. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 21. Artificial Intelligence in the Clinic Technology 263 words

Новые слова
  • striking — поразительный, заметный
  • detect — обнаруживать
  • accuracy — точность
  • label — помечать, маркировать
  • routine — рутинный
  • flag — помечать, выделять
  • diagnosis — диагноз
  • conclusion — вывод, заключение

A In hospitals around the world, computer programs are beginning to do work that was once reserved for highly trained specialists. The most striking progress has been in reading medical images. Software trained on hundreds of thousands of X-rays and scans can now detect signs of lung disease, breast cancer and eye conditions with an accuracy that, in several studies, matches that of experienced doctors.

B These systems do not follow rules written by programmers. Instead they learn from examples. Shown a vast number of images that doctors have already labelled, the program gradually works out which patterns are associated with illness. This means that its quality depends heavily on its training data. A system taught mainly with images from one group of patients may perform poorly on others, a weakness that has already been observed with skin-cancer tools trained largely on photographs of light skin.

C Supporters argue that the technology will not replace doctors but will free them from routine tasks. A program never gets tired, and it can check thousands of scans overnight, flagging the urgent ones for a human to examine first. In regions with few specialists it could bring expert diagnosis to patients who would otherwise wait months.

D There are concerns as well. It is often impossible to explain exactly why a program reached a particular conclusion, which makes errors hard to detect. If a mistake harms a patient, it is unclear who is responsible: the hospital, the doctor or the company that built the software. For now, most health authorities insist that a qualified person must make the final decision.

Passage 22. The Return of the Electric Car Technology 264 words

Новые слова
  • vehicle — транспортное средство
  • handle — рукоятка
  • assembly line — конвейер, сборочная линия
  • recharge — перезаряжать
  • revival — возрождение
  • incentive — стимул, льгота
  • obstacle — препятствие
  • distribute — распределять

A Electric cars feel like a recent invention, but they are older than cars that burn petrol. In 1900 about a third of the vehicles on American roads were electric. They were quiet, clean and easy to start, and they were especially popular in cities. Petrol cars, by contrast, were noisy and had to be started by turning a heavy handle at the front.

B Two developments ended that early success. The discovery of large oil fields in Texas made petrol cheap, and in 1912 the electric starter removed the need for the handle. Meanwhile Henry Ford's assembly line cut the price of his Model T to less than half that of an electric car. Since batteries allowed only short journeys and there were few places to recharge them outside towns, electric vehicles had almost disappeared by the 1930s.

C Their revival began a century later, driven by concern about air pollution and climate change and made possible by the lithium-ion battery, which stores far more energy for its weight than older types. The cost of these batteries fell by roughly ninety per cent between 2010 and 2020. Many governments now offer buyers financial incentives, and several have announced dates after which new petrol and diesel cars may not be sold.

D Obstacles remain. Charging still takes longer than filling a tank, and charging points are unevenly distributed. Mining the lithium and cobalt needed for batteries can damage the environment. Moreover, an electric car is only as clean as the electricity it uses: if that comes from coal-fired power stations, the benefit to the climate is much reduced.

Passage 23. Printing in Three Dimensions Technology 257 words

Новые слова
  • manufacturing — производство
  • polish — полировать
  • layer — слой
  • prototype — прототип, опытный образец
  • precise — точный
  • implant — имплант
  • spare part — запасная часть
  • warehouse — склад

A Traditional manufacturing usually works by removing material: a block of metal or wood is cut, drilled and polished until the desired shape remains. Three-dimensional printing does the opposite. Guided by a digital design, a printer builds an object layer by layer, each one a fraction of a millimetre thick, from plastic, metal powder or other substances. For this reason engineers call the technique additive manufacturing.

B The idea dates back to the 1980s, when it was used to make prototypes quickly. A company designing a new product could print a model overnight rather than waiting weeks for one to be made by hand. As the machines became cheaper and more precise, they began to produce finished goods as well.

C The advantages are clearest where objects are complex or needed in small numbers. Aircraft manufacturers print lightweight engine parts with internal shapes that could not be made in any other way. In medicine the technology has been used for dental crowns, hearing aids and implants matched exactly to a patient's body. Because the printer uses only the material it needs, very little is wasted. Spare parts can be produced where they are required, even on the International Space Station, instead of being stored in warehouses and shipped around the world.

D Three-dimensional printing is unlikely to replace factories, however. It remains slow compared with conventional methods, and for making millions of identical items, mass production is far cheaper. There are also worries about misuse, since designs for dangerous objects such as weapons can be shared online and printed at home.

Passage 24. The Origins of the Internet Technology 270 words

Новые слова
  • fund — финансировать
  • packet — пакет (данных)
  • destination — пункт назначения
  • crash — давать сбой, «падать»
  • adopt — принимать, внедрять
  • turning point — переломный момент
  • propose — предлагать
  • crucially — что крайне важно

A The internet grew out of a research project funded by the United States Department of Defense in the 1960s. Its aim was to let scientists at different universities share expensive computers. The key idea was packet switching: instead of sending a message along one fixed line, as a telephone call was, the network broke it into small packets that travelled separately and were put back together at their destination. If one route failed, the packets simply took another.

B The first link of the network, called ARPANET, was made in October 1969 between two universities in California. The operators tried to send the word 'login', but the system crashed after two letters, so the first message ever sent was 'lo'. By 1971 the network connected about twenty sites, and in the same year the engineer Ray Tomlinson sent the first email, choosing the @ sign to separate the user's name from the name of the computer.

C During the 1970s other networks appeared, and they could not communicate with each other. Vinton Cerf and Robert Kahn solved the problem by designing a common set of rules, known as TCP/IP, which ARPANET adopted on 1 January 1983. Many regard that date as the true birthday of the internet.

D For ordinary people the turning point came later. In 1989 Tim Berners-Lee, a British scientist working at the CERN laboratory in Switzerland, proposed a system of linked pages that he named the World Wide Web. Crucially, CERN made the technology free for anyone to use. Within a decade the web had hundreds of millions of users, and the internet had moved from laboratories into homes.

2 The first message sent over ARPANET was shorter than intended. ✔ TRUE
3 Ray Tomlinson invented the @ symbol. ✔ FALSE
4 Tim Berners-Lee became rich from the World Wide Web. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 25. Drones at Work Technology 264 words

Новые слова
  • remote control — дистанционное управление
  • propeller — пропеллер
  • reveal — выявлять, показывать
  • fertiliser — удобрение
  • spray — опрыскивать
  • inspect — осматривать, проверять
  • suspend — приостанавливать
  • runway — взлётно-посадочная полоса

A A drone is an aircraft without a pilot on board, flown either by remote control or by its own computer. Drones were developed for military purposes, but small, cheap models with four or more propellers and a camera have found a remarkable range of peaceful uses since about 2010.

B Farmers were among the first to benefit. A drone flying over a field can photograph crops in different kinds of light, revealing areas that need water or are affected by disease long before the damage is visible from the ground. Fertiliser can then be applied only where it is required, saving money and reducing pollution. In Japan, unmanned helicopters have sprayed rice fields for decades.

C Drones are also valuable where work is dangerous or access is difficult. They inspect bridges, power lines and wind turbines, jobs that used to require people to climb to great heights. After earthquakes and floods they search for survivors and map the damage. In Rwanda, a delivery service launched in 2016 flies blood and medicines to remote clinics, cutting journeys that took hours by road to about thirty minutes.

D The rapid spread of drones has raised problems. They can be flown over private gardens with a camera, and their noise disturbs both people and wildlife. More seriously, drones near airports have forced flights to be suspended. Most countries now require operators to register larger models, keep them within sight and stay away from crowds and runways. Whether city skies will ever be filled with drones delivering parcels, as some companies predict, depends as much on such rules as on technology.

Passage 26. How GPS Knows Where You Are Technology 268 words

Новые слова
  • take it for granted — принимать как должное
  • orbit — вращаться по орбите
  • broadcast — передавать (сигнал)
  • receiver — приёмник
  • convert — преобразовывать
  • drift — смещаться, отклоняться
  • civilian — гражданский
  • deliberately — намеренно

A Most smartphone users take it for granted that a blue dot on a map will show their position to within a few metres. Behind that dot is the Global Positioning System, a network of about thirty satellites orbiting roughly 20,000 kilometres above the Earth. The system was built by the United States military and became fully operational in 1995.

B The principle is surprisingly simple. Each satellite continuously broadcasts a signal stating its position and the exact time. A receiver on the ground notes how long each signal took to arrive and, since radio waves travel at the speed of light, converts that delay into a distance. One satellite places the receiver somewhere on a huge sphere; with signals from four satellites, only a single point fits all the measurements.

C Everything depends on timing. An error of one millionth of a second would put the position out by about 300 metres, so every satellite carries atomic clocks. Even Einstein's theory of relativity must be taken into account: because the satellites move fast and experience weaker gravity, their clocks run faster than clocks on the ground by about 38 millionths of a second per day. Without correction, the system would drift by around ten kilometres daily.

D Originally the best signal was reserved for the military, and civilian receivers were deliberately made less accurate. That restriction ended in 2000, and accuracy improved overnight from about 100 metres to about ten. Today GPS and similar systems run by Europe, Russia and China guide aircraft, ships and tractors, and the precise time signals are used to coordinate mobile phone networks and banking transactions.

3 Clocks on GPS satellites run more slowly than clocks on Earth. ✔ FALSE
4 The European system is more accurate than GPS. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 27. Power from the Sun Technology 273 words

Новые слова
  • capture — улавливать, захватывать
  • current — ток
  • cell — элемент, ячейка
  • efficiency — эффективность, КПД
  • generate — вырабатывать
  • output — выработка, объём производства
  • demand — спрос
  • install — устанавливать

A Every hour, the Earth receives more energy from the Sun than humanity uses in a year. Capturing even a small part of it has long been a dream of engineers. The photovoltaic effect, in which light striking certain materials produces an electric current, was first observed by the French physicist Edmond Becquerel in 1839, but the first practical silicon solar cell was not made until 1954, at Bell Laboratories in the United States.

B That early cell converted only about six per cent of the light into electricity and was extremely expensive. Its first important customers were space agencies, which needed a lightweight source of power for satellites and were not worried about cost. On Earth, solar panels were for decades limited to small devices such as calculators and to remote places far from power lines.

C The picture has changed dramatically. Typical panels today have an efficiency of around twenty per cent, and mass production, much of it in China, has reduced their price by about ninety per cent since 2010. In many sunny countries solar farms are now the cheapest way of generating new electricity. Panels have no moving parts, make no noise and can last for twenty-five years or more.

D The obvious drawback is that the Sun does not always shine. Output falls on cloudy days and stops at night, often just when demand rises in the evening. The solution lies in storage. Large batteries are being installed beside solar farms, and some power stations pump water uphill during the day and release it through turbines after dark. Until storage becomes cheaper, most countries will continue to need other sources alongside solar.

2 The first silicon solar cell turned about twenty per cent of light into electricity. ✔ FALSE
3 Space agencies were among the earliest users of solar cells. ✔ TRUE
4 China uses more solar power than any other country. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 28. Robots on the Factory Floor Technology 277 words

Новые слова
  • rebel — восставать
  • plant — завод
  • welding — сварка
  • inflexible — негибкий
  • fenced off (fence off) — огораживать
  • collaborative — совместный
  • sensor — датчик
  • maintenance — техническое обслуживание

A The word 'robot' comes from a Czech play of 1920, in which artificial workers rebel against their human masters. Real robots arrived forty years later and were far less dramatic. In 1961 a machine called Unimate, essentially a mechanical arm, began lifting hot pieces of metal at a General Motors car plant in New Jersey, a job that was unpleasant and dangerous for people.

B Industrial robots spread through car factories in the 1970s and 1980s, particularly in Japan. They were ideal for tasks such as welding and painting, which had to be repeated thousands of times in exactly the same way. A robot does not get bored, take breaks or lose concentration. On the other hand, the early machines were blind and inflexible. They had to be fenced off in cages, because they could not sense a person standing in their path.

C A newer generation, known as collaborative robots or 'cobots', is designed to work beside people. Equipped with cameras and sensors, they stop immediately when they touch something unexpected, and they can be taught a new task simply by guiding the arm through the movements. Because they are relatively cheap, even small firms can afford them.

D Whether robots destroy jobs is a matter of debate. Studies in several countries have found that each new industrial robot replaces a number of factory workers. Yet countries with the highest numbers of robots, such as South Korea and Germany, do not have unusually high unemployment. Economists point out that automation also creates work, in programming, maintenance and new industries, although those jobs demand different skills, and workers who lose routine jobs often find it hard to move into them.

Passage 29. The Attention Economy Technology 276 words

Новые слова
  • purpose — цель
  • feed — лента (новостей)
  • scroll — прокручивать
  • notification — уведомление
  • urgency — срочность
  • persistently — настойчиво, упорно
  • controversial — спорный
  • remedy — средство, решение

A The average smartphone owner now checks the device dozens of times a day, often without any particular purpose. This is not an accident. Many of the most popular apps are free to use, and the companies behind them earn money by selling advertising. The longer people stay on the screen, the more advertisements they see. Human attention has become the product being sold.

B Designers have a range of techniques for holding that attention. One is the endless feed: because there is no natural stopping point, such as the end of a page, users keep scrolling. Another is the notification, a sound or red symbol that creates a small sense of urgency. A third is the unpredictable reward. Psychologists have known since the 1950s that animals repeat an action most persistently when it is rewarded only some of the time. Refreshing a social media page works in a similar way: usually there is nothing new, but occasionally there is a message or a 'like'.

C The consequences are disputed. Some researchers link heavy use of social media to anxiety and poor sleep among teenagers, while others argue that the evidence is weak and that the effects are small. What is less controversial is the cost to concentration. In one experiment, students performed worse on a test when their phone was simply lying on the desk, even though it was switched off.

D In response, phone makers have introduced tools that show how much time is spent on each app and allow users to set limits. Critics note the irony of relying on the device itself to solve the problem, and suggest a simpler remedy: leaving it in another room.

Passage 30. What Happens to Our Plastic? Technology 271 words

Новые слова
  • waterproof — водонепроницаемый
  • estimate — оценивать
  • substance — вещество
  • sort — сортировать
  • batch — партия
  • decline — снижаться
  • divert — перенаправлять
  • deposit — залог

A Plastic is one of the most useful materials ever invented: light, strong, waterproof and cheap. These same qualities make it a problem once it is thrown away. Since large-scale production began in the 1950s, more than eight billion tonnes have been manufactured, and it is estimated that less than ten per cent of all plastic waste has ever been recycled. The rest has been burned, buried or left in the environment.

B One reason is that 'plastic' is not a single substance. There are many types, and they cannot be melted down together. A drinks bottle is made of one kind, its cap of another, and the label of a third. Waste must therefore be sorted, which is costly, and food remains can spoil a whole batch. Even when recycling succeeds, the quality of the material declines each time, so a bottle is more likely to become carpet fibre than another bottle.

C For years many rich countries dealt with the difficulty by shipping their plastic waste abroad, chiefly to China. In 2018 China banned most of these imports, and waste began to pile up in the exporting countries or was diverted to nations with weaker controls.

D Several solutions are being explored. Chemical recycling breaks plastic down into its original building blocks, which can be made into new material of full quality, though the process uses a great deal of energy. Deposit schemes, in which customers get money back for returning bottles, achieve return rates above ninety per cent in countries such as Germany and Norway. Most experts stress, however, that the priority must be to use less plastic in the first place.

4 China's ban was introduced because of protests by its citizens. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 31. Farming in the City Environment 265 words

Новые слова
  • plot — участок земли
  • disused — заброшенный, неиспользуемый
  • stack — складывать ярусами
  • nutrient — питательное вещество
  • sealed — герметичный
  • drought — засуха
  • bankrupt — обанкротившийся
  • staple — основной (продукт питания)

A By 2050 about two thirds of the world's population is expected to live in cities, far from the fields that feed them. One response has been to bring agriculture into the city itself. Urban farming takes many forms, from community gardens on empty plots to greenhouses on supermarket roofs.

B The most ambitious version is the vertical farm. Inside a warehouse or a disused factory, lettuces and herbs grow on shelves stacked many levels high. There is no soil: the roots sit in water containing nutrients, a method known as hydroponics, and LED lamps replace the sun. Because the environment is sealed, no pesticides are needed, and the crop is unaffected by drought, frost or storms. Such farms use up to ninety-five per cent less water than open fields, and harvests continue all year round.

C There are further benefits. Food grown where it is eaten does not need to be transported long distances, so it arrives fresher. Community gardens also bring neighbours together and give children who have grown up among concrete a chance to learn where food comes from.

D Critics are not convinced that vertical farms can feed cities. The lamps and climate control consume a great deal of electricity, and several well-funded companies have gone bankrupt because of energy costs. Moreover, the crops that grow well indoors are leafy vegetables, which supply few calories. Wheat, rice and maize, the staple foods of humanity, need far too much space and light to be grown this way at a reasonable price. Urban farms may add welcome freshness to city diets, but the countryside will remain essential.

Passage 32. Losing the Forests Environment 273 words

Новые слова
  • shrink — сокращаться
  • clear — расчищать, вырубать
  • cattle — крупный рогатый скот
  • logging — заготовка леса
  • settler — поселенец
  • rot — гнить
  • moisture — влага
  • monitoring — наблюдение, мониторинг

A Forests cover about thirty per cent of the Earth's land, but the area is shrinking. Each year around ten million hectares are cleared, most of it in the tropics. The Amazon basin, Central Africa and South-East Asia are losing trees fastest.

B The main cause is agriculture. In South America forest is cleared chiefly for cattle ranches and for fields of soya, much of which is exported as animal feed. In Indonesia and Malaysia the principal driver has been palm oil, an ingredient found in a huge range of supermarket products from biscuits to shampoo. Logging for timber, mining and road building add to the damage; a new road also opens up previously remote areas to settlers.

C The consequences reach far beyond the forest itself. Tropical forests contain more than half of the world's land species, many of which live nowhere else. Trees also store vast amounts of carbon, and when they are burned or left to rot it is released as carbon dioxide; deforestation accounts for roughly a tenth of global emissions. Forests even make their own weather. Trees draw water from the ground and release it through their leaves, and this moisture falls again as rain. Scientists fear that if too much of the Amazon is cut, the remainder could dry out and turn into grassland.

D There are some encouraging signs. Satellite monitoring now allows illegal clearing to be spotted within days. Costa Rica, which pays landowners to protect trees, has doubled its forest cover since the 1980s. Consumers have a role too, since several large companies have promised, under public pressure, to buy only products that are not linked to deforestation.

4 Costa Rica has more forest than it had in the 1980s. ✔ TRUE

Passage 33. Plastic in the Oceans Environment 272 words

Новые слова
  • dump — свалка
  • overboard — за борт
  • debris — мусор, обломки
  • misleading — вводящий в заблуждение
  • decay — разлагаться
  • entangle — запутывать(ся)
  • swallow — глотать
  • packaging — упаковка

A Every year an estimated eight to eleven million tonnes of plastic enter the sea, roughly a lorry load every minute. About eighty per cent of it comes from the land, carried by rivers or blown from beaches and rubbish dumps. The rest is lost or thrown overboard at sea, and abandoned fishing nets make up a large part of this.

B Ocean currents gather floating debris into huge rotating zones. The best known, the Great Pacific Garbage Patch between Hawaii and California, is often imagined as an island of rubbish, but this is misleading. Most of it is a thin soup of small fragments, hardly visible from a boat.

C Plastic does not decay as natural materials do. Sunlight and waves merely break it into smaller and smaller pieces. Fragments under five millimetres across are called microplastics, and they have now been found everywhere from Arctic ice to the deepest ocean trench. They also come from sources that few people suspect, such as the fibres washed out of synthetic clothes and the dust from car tyres.

D The harm to wildlife is well documented. Turtles mistake plastic bags for jellyfish, and seabirds feed bottle tops to their chicks, which may starve with full stomachs. Animals become entangled in old nets and drown. Microplastics are swallowed by tiny creatures at the bottom of the food chain and pass upwards to fish and eventually to humans, though the effect on our health is not yet known.

E Cleaning the open ocean is extremely difficult, so most specialists believe efforts should focus on stopping plastic at its source, by improving waste collection in coastal cities and reducing throwaway packaging.

Passage 34. The World's Water Problem Environment 280 words

Новые слова
  • glacier — ледник
  • fraction — доля, часть
  • household — домохозяйство
  • consumer — потребитель
  • reserve — запас
  • severe — серьёзный, суровый
  • irrigation — орошение
  • leak — протекать; утечка

A Seen from space, the Earth looks like a planet with water to spare. In fact about ninety-seven per cent of that water is salty. Of the remaining fresh water, more than two thirds is frozen in glaciers and ice caps, and most of the rest lies underground. Rivers and lakes, on which humanity mainly depends, hold a tiny fraction of one per cent of the total.

B Demand on this limited supply has grown about six times over the past century. Contrary to what many people assume, households are not the main consumers. Agriculture takes roughly seventy per cent of all the fresh water that people use, industry around twenty per cent and homes only about ten. Some crops are particularly thirsty: producing a single kilogram of rice can require more than two thousand litres.

C The results are visible in many regions. Great rivers such as the Colorado and the Yellow River sometimes fail to reach the sea. Underground reserves in northern India and the central United States are being pumped out much faster than rain can refill them. Around two billion people live in countries where water is under severe pressure, and climate change is making rainfall less reliable.

D Solutions exist, but none is simple. Drip irrigation, which delivers water directly to the roots of plants, can cut a farm's use by half compared with flooding fields. Cities lose a great deal through leaking pipes, in some cases a third or more of their supply. Removing salt from seawater is technically possible but remains expensive. Many economists argue that water is wasted mainly because it is priced too low, although raising the price of something so essential is politically difficult.

Passage 35. Harvesting the Wind Environment 266 words

Новые слова
  • mill — мельница
  • invest — инвестировать
  • blade — лопасть
  • quadruple — увеличивать в четыре раза
  • offshore — морской, в открытом море
  • foundation — фундамент
  • anchor — закреплять якорем
  • collision — столкновение

A People have used the wind for thousands of years, to drive sailing ships and to turn mills that ground grain and pumped water. Using it to make electricity began in the late nineteenth century, but wind power became a serious industry only after the oil crisis of the 1970s, when Denmark in particular began investing in it.

B A modern turbine is a huge machine. Its tower may be over a hundred metres tall, and each of its three blades can be longer than the wing of a large passenger aircraft. Size matters, because doubling the length of the blades roughly quadruples the energy captured, and winds are stronger and steadier higher up. A single large turbine can supply electricity for several thousand homes.

C Increasingly, turbines are being built out at sea. Offshore winds are more powerful and more constant than those on land, and there are no neighbours to complain about noise or spoiled views. The disadvantage is cost: foundations must be fixed to the seabed, and salt water makes maintenance difficult. The newest designs float, anchored by cables, so that they can be placed in deep water.

D Wind power has drawbacks. Its output varies with the weather, so other sources or storage are needed for calm days. Turbines kill birds and bats, though studies indicate that the numbers are far smaller than those killed by cats or by collisions with buildings. Old blades, made of materials that are hard to recycle, often end up buried in the ground. Nevertheless, wind now provides more than half of Denmark's electricity, and its share is rising steadily worldwide.

Passage 36. The Wolves of Yellowstone Environment 275 words

Новые слова
  • pest — вредитель
  • pack — стая
  • multiply — размножаться
  • dam — плотина
  • pond — пруд
  • trap — загонять в ловушку
  • cascade — каскад
  • landscape — ландшафт

A When Yellowstone became the world's first national park in 1872, wolves were regarded as dangerous pests. Hunters, encouraged by the government, shot or poisoned them, and by 1926 the last pack in the park had been killed.

B The effects appeared slowly. Without their main predator, the elk, a large species of deer, multiplied. They no longer needed to keep moving, and they gathered along river banks, where they ate young willow and aspen trees down to the ground. As the trees vanished, so did the songbirds that nested in them. Beavers, which need willow for food and for building their dams, became rare, and the ponds that their dams had created dried up.

C In 1995, after years of argument, biologists brought fourteen wolves from Canada and released them in the park; seventeen more followed the next year. The wolves killed some elk, but, equally important, they changed the behaviour of the rest. Elk began to avoid valleys where they could easily be trapped. In those places the trees recovered, and beavers and birds returned. The wolves also reduced the number of coyotes, so that the small animals coyotes hunt increased, providing more food for foxes and eagles.

D The story has become a famous example of what ecologists call a trophic cascade, in which a change at the top of the food chain flows down through every level. Some scientists warn that it has been simplified: milder winters, drought and hunting outside the park also affected the elk. Even so, few now doubt that large predators shape the landscapes they live in. Farmers near the park, whose sheep and cattle are sometimes attacked, remain less enthusiastic.

4 Tourist numbers increased after the wolves returned. ✔ NOT GIVEN
