Passage 37. Rivers of Ice Environment 278 words

Новые слова
  • compress — сжимать
  • dense — плотный
  • ice sheet — ледяной щит
  • sculptor — скульптор
  • basin — котловина, бассейн
  • retreat — отступать
  • sensitive — чувствительный
  • reservoir — водохранилище, резервуар

A A glacier is a mass of ice that forms on land where more snow falls in winter than melts in summer. Over many years the snow is compressed into dense ice, and when it becomes thick enough it begins to move downhill under its own weight, usually by less than a metre a day. Glaciers and the great ice sheets of Greenland and Antarctica hold about seventy per cent of the world's fresh water.

B Glaciers are powerful sculptors. During the ice ages they carved the deep U-shaped valleys of the Alps and the fjords of Norway, and they left behind the basins that became the Great Lakes of North America.

C Today almost all of the world's glaciers are retreating. Photographs taken a century apart show valleys once filled with ice now occupied by bare rock and lakes. The Alps have lost roughly half of their ice volume since 1900, and some small glaciers have disappeared completely. The cause is rising temperature: a glacier is highly sensitive to climate because a change of only a degree or so tips the balance between snowfall and melting.

D The loss matters to people far from the mountains. Glaciers act as natural reservoirs, storing water in winter and releasing it in the dry summer months when rivers would otherwise run low. Hundreds of millions of people in South and Central Asia depend on rivers fed by the ice of the Himalayas and neighbouring ranges, including the Amu Darya and the Syr Darya. As glaciers shrink, river flow first increases, sometimes causing floods, and then declines permanently. Melting land ice also adds to the rise in sea level, which threatens low-lying coasts and islands.

Passage 38. The Hidden Cost of Noise Environment 271 words

Новые слова
  • underestimate — недооценивать
  • trace — след
  • dismiss — отмахиваться, не принимать всерьёз
  • exposure — воздействие
  • vulnerable — уязвимый
  • pitch — высота (звука)
  • interfere — мешать
  • insulation — изоляция

A Noise is perhaps the most underestimated form of pollution. It leaves no visible trace and disappears the moment its source is switched off, so it is easy to dismiss as a mere annoyance. Evidence gathered over the past few decades suggests it is considerably more than that.

B Sound is measured in decibels. Normal conversation is about 60 decibels, heavy traffic around 85 and a rock concert well over 100. Long exposure to levels above 85 decibels can permanently damage hearing. But harm occurs at much lower levels too. The body reacts to noise as a signal of danger, releasing stress hormones and raising blood pressure, and it continues to do so during sleep, even when the sleeper does not wake. Large studies in Europe have found that people living beside busy roads or under flight paths have a higher risk of heart disease.

C Children seem to be especially vulnerable. In a well-known study in New York in the 1970s, pupils in classrooms facing a railway line were found to be almost a year behind in reading compared with those on the quiet side of the same building. When the noise was reduced, the gap disappeared.

D Wildlife suffers as well. Birds in cities sing at a higher pitch or at night in order to be heard, and the engines of ships interfere with the calls that whales use to communicate over great distances.

E Remedies are available. Quieter road surfaces, lower speed limits, barriers and better insulation in buildings all make a difference. Electric vehicles are quieter at low speeds, although at higher speeds most traffic noise comes from tyres rather than engines.

4 Governments spend too little on reducing noise. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 39. When the Stars Disappeared Environment 274 words

Новые слова
  • pale — бледный
  • scatter — рассеивать(ся)
  • observatory — обсерватория
  • evolve — эволюционировать, развиваться
  • hatch — вылупляться
  • illuminated — освещённый
  • exhausted — обессиленный
  • shield — экранировать, прикрывать

A For almost all of human history, the night sky was filled with thousands of stars and the pale band of the Milky Way. Today about a third of humanity, including most Europeans and North Americans, can no longer see the Milky Way from where they live. The cause is light pollution: the glow of street lamps, advertising signs and buildings that scatters in the atmosphere and brightens the sky.

B Astronomers were the first to complain, and many observatories have been forced to move to remote mountains and deserts. But the loss of darkness affects much more than stargazing. Life on Earth evolved with a regular cycle of day and night, and many species depend on it. Baby sea turtles, which hatch on beaches at night, find the ocean by heading for the brightest horizon; artificial lights lead them inland, where they die. Migrating birds are drawn towards illuminated towers and collide with them. Insects circle street lamps until they are exhausted, which is one probable reason for the decline in insect numbers.

C Human beings are affected too. Light in the evening reduces the body's production of melatonin and disturbs sleep, and some studies have linked night-shift work with higher rates of certain illnesses.

D Unlike most forms of pollution, this one is easy to reverse. Much outdoor lighting is simply wasted, shining upwards or sideways rather than down onto the ground. Lamps that are shielded, dimmed late at night or fitted with motion sensors save energy and money as well as darkness. A growing number of regions have been declared dark-sky reserves, where lighting is strictly controlled and visitors come specifically to see the stars.

Passage 40. The True Price of Fast Fashion Environment 277 words

Новые слова
  • garment — предмет одежды
  • transform — преобразовывать, менять
  • fibre — волокно
  • dye — красить (ткань)

  • wage — заработная плата

A A generation ago, clothing shops introduced new collections two or four times a year. Today some brands add thousands of new designs to their websites every week, at prices so low that a T-shirt may cost less than a sandwich. This business model, known as fast fashion, has transformed the way people dress. Worldwide, clothing production roughly doubled between 2000 and 2015, while the number of times a garment is worn before being thrown away fell by more than a third.

B The environmental cost is heavy. Cotton is a thirsty crop: growing enough for one pair of jeans can take several thousand litres of water. The Aral Sea in Central Asia, once the fourth largest lake in the world, shrank to a fraction of its size largely because the rivers that fed it were diverted to irrigate cotton fields. Polyester, now the most common fibre, is made from oil and releases tiny plastic fibres each time it is washed. Dyeing and finishing fabric pollutes rivers in manufacturing regions.

C There is a human cost too. Most clothes are sewn in low-wage countries, often in poor conditions. In 2013 the Rana Plaza building in Bangladesh, which housed several clothing factories, collapsed, killing more than 1,100 workers. The disaster led to new safety agreements, but wages in the industry remain very low.

D Less than one per cent of the material in old clothes is recycled into new ones, and much of what is donated to charity is shipped abroad and eventually dumped. Campaigners therefore urge shoppers to buy fewer, better items, to repair them and to choose second-hand. Some governments are considering making brands pay for the waste they generate.

Passage 41. The Bilingual Brain Education & Psychology 272 words

Новые слова
  • suspicion — подозрение, недоверие
  • confuse — путать, сбивать с толку
  • background — происхождение, среда
  • monolingual — одноязычный
  • suppress — подавлять
  • competitor — соперник, конкурент
  • strengthen — укреплять
  • dementia — деменция

A More than half of the world's people use two or more languages in daily life, yet for much of the twentieth century bilingualism was regarded with suspicion. Teachers and doctors in several countries warned immigrant parents that speaking two languages at home would confuse their children and hold back their progress at school.

B That belief rested on poorly designed studies, which often compared poor immigrant children with wealthy native speakers. In 1962 two Canadian researchers, Elizabeth Peal and Wallace Lambert, compared children from similar backgrounds in Montreal and found that the bilingual group actually did better on a range of tests. Since then, the picture has become more detailed. Bilingual children do tend to have a slightly smaller vocabulary in each language than monolingual children of the same age, but their total vocabulary across both languages is as large or larger.

C What makes the bilingual mind interesting is that both languages are active all the time, even when only one is being used. The speaker must constantly select the right word and suppress its competitor. Some psychologists argue that this continuous exercise strengthens the brain's ability to focus and to switch between tasks, though recent attempts to repeat these experiments have produced mixed results, and the question remains open.

D The most striking claim concerns old age. Studies of hospital patients in Canada and India have reported that people who used two languages throughout their lives showed symptoms of dementia four to five years later, on average, than those who spoke only one. Whatever the truth about the brain, the practical and cultural benefits of knowing a second language are not in doubt.

Passage 42. Why Children Need to Play Education & Psychology 270 words

Новые слова
  • pretend — притворяться, воображать
  • coordination — координация
  • negotiate — договариваться
  • impulse — порыв, импульс
  • observe — замечать, наблюдать
  • opportunity — возможность
  • arithmetic — арифметика
  • anxiety — тревожность

A To an adult, a child building a tower of blocks or pretending to be a pirate may appear to be doing nothing useful. Developmental psychologists see things differently. Play, they argue, is the main way in which young children learn about the world and about one another.

B Different kinds of play develop different abilities. Physical play, such as running, climbing and wrestling, builds strength and coordination and teaches children to judge risk. Building with blocks or sand introduces ideas of size, balance and number. Most important of all, perhaps, is pretend play. When children agree that a cardboard box is a spaceship, they must negotiate roles, follow shared rules and control their impulses. A child who is playing a guard, for example, will stand still far longer than one who is simply told to do so.

C The Russian psychologist Lev Vygotsky observed in the 1930s that in play a child behaves 'a head taller than himself', practising skills that are still slightly beyond him. More recent research supports this. Children who have plenty of opportunity for free play tend to be better at solving problems and at getting along with others.

D Despite this, time for play has been shrinking. In many countries formal lessons in reading and arithmetic begin earlier than they used to, school breaks have been shortened, and parents worried about traffic and strangers are less willing to let children play outside alone. Some experts connect these changes with rising levels of anxiety among young people. They point to Finland, where children do not start formal schooling until the age of seven, yet achieve excellent results later on.

Passage 43. The Homework Debate Education & Psychology 264 words

Новые слова
  • swung (swing) — колебаться, качаться
  • abolish — отменять
  • thorough — тщательный
  • review — обзор
  • achievement — успеваемость, достижение
  • opponent — противник
  • widen — расширять
  • worksheet — рабочий лист с заданиями

A Few topics in education cause as much disagreement as homework. Attitudes have swung back and forth for more than a century. Around 1900 an American campaign claimed that homework damaged children's health, and some districts abolished it for younger pupils. After the Soviet Union launched the first satellite in 1957, fear of falling behind led American schools to set more of it than ever.

B What does the research show? The most thorough reviews have been carried out by the psychologist Harris Cooper, who analysed dozens of studies. He found that for secondary school students, those who did homework achieved better test results, up to a point: beyond about two hours a night, the benefit disappeared. For primary school children, however, there was almost no link between homework and achievement.

C Supporters reply that test scores are not the only goal. Homework, they say, teaches responsibility and time management and keeps parents informed about what their children are studying. Opponents answer that it eats into time for play, sport, sleep and family life, and that it is unfair. A child with educated parents, a quiet room and a computer has a clear advantage over one who has none of these, so homework may widen the gap between rich and poor.

D Many teachers have concluded that quality matters more than quantity. Short tasks that practise what was taught in class or that ask pupils to read for pleasure seem more valuable than long worksheets. Cooper himself recommends the 'ten-minute rule': ten minutes a night in the first year of school, increasing by ten minutes with each year.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Opinions about homework have remained the same for over a century. ✔ FALSE
2 The launch of a Soviet satellite led to more homework in the USA. ✔ TRUE
3 Cooper found a strong connection between homework and results in primary schools. ✔ FALSE
4 Cooper's own children did very little homework. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 44. Two Ways of Thinking about Ability Education & Psychology 267 words

Новые слова
  • mindset — установка, образ мышления
  • challenge — трудная задача, вызов
  • strategy — стратегия
  • persist — упорствовать, не сдаваться
  • praise — хвалить
  • notion — представление, идея
  • prompt — побуждать
  • modest — скромный, умеренный

A Why do some people give up after a failure while others try harder? The American psychologist Carol Dweck has spent several decades studying this question. Her answer is that much depends on what people believe about their own abilities.

B According to Dweck, individuals with a 'fixed mindset' see intelligence as something you are born with and cannot change. For them, every task is a test of how clever they are. Because failure would show that they lack ability, they tend to avoid challenges and lose interest when work becomes hard. Those with a 'growth mindset', by contrast, believe that ability can be developed through effort and good strategies. They treat mistakes as information and are more likely to persist.

C In one famous experiment, children were given a set of puzzles and then praised. Some were told, 'You must be smart at this'; others, 'You must have worked hard.' When offered a choice afterwards, most of those praised for intelligence chose an easy task, whereas most of those praised for effort chose a more difficult one from which they could learn. After a later, very hard set of problems, the first group's performance fell, while the second group improved.

D These ideas have been enthusiastically adopted by schools, sometimes too simply. Dweck has criticised the notion that teachers need only praise effort; effort that leads nowhere should prompt a change of method. Moreover, large studies testing mindset lessons have found that the improvement in grades is modest, and concentrated among pupils who were previously struggling. A growth mindset appears to be helpful, but it is not a magic solution.

2 Children praised for being smart usually chose the harder task. ✔ FALSE
3 The group praised for effort did better after facing very hard problems. ✔ TRUE
4 Dweck carried out her experiment in several countries. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 45. Putting Things Off Education & Psychology 273 words

Новые слова
  • procrastination — прокрастинация, откладывание дел
  • persistent — устойчивый, постоянный
  • relief — облегчение
  • guilt — чувство вины
  • postpone — откладывать
  • vague — расплывчатый, неясный
  • temptation — соблазн
  • willpower — сила воли

A Almost everyone delays unpleasant tasks from time to time, but for roughly one adult in five procrastination is a persistent habit that damages work, finances and health. It was once explained as laziness or poor time management. Psychologists now tend to see it as a problem of managing emotions.

B The reasoning is as follows. A task such as writing a report or filling in a tax form produces unpleasant feelings: boredom, anxiety or fear of doing it badly. Doing something else instead brings immediate relief. That relief works as a reward, so the habit of avoiding is strengthened. The cost arrives later, in the form of stress, guilt and rushed work, but the human brain gives far more weight to the present than to the future.

C Certain characteristics make a task more likely to be postponed. Deadlines that are distant, goals that are vague and jobs with no clear first step are all easy to put off. Perfectionists are particularly at risk, since starting means facing the possibility that the result will not be perfect.

D Advice that follows from this view is quite practical. Large projects should be divided into small, specific actions, so that the first step takes only a few minutes. Deciding in advance exactly when and where to work, for instance 'at nine o'clock in the library', has been shown to increase the chance of starting. Removing temptations, such as putting the phone in another room, is more reliable than depending on willpower. Finally, research suggests that students who forgive themselves for past procrastination delay less the next time, perhaps because guilt is itself a feeling people try to escape.

2 People value present feelings more than future consequences. ✔ YES
3 Perfectionists rarely procrastinate. ✔ NO
4 Procrastination is more common among men than women. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 46. Reading for Pleasure Education & Psychology 274 words

Новые слова
  • survey — опрос
  • proportion — доля
  • blame — винить
  • complex — сложный
  • spelling — правописание
  • exposure — знакомство, контакт
  • fiction — художественная литература
  • counter-productive — приводящий к обратному результату

A Surveys in many countries tell the same story: young people are reading fewer books for enjoyment than they used to. In one long-running British survey, the proportion of children who said they liked reading in their free time fell in 2024 to its lowest level since the study began almost twenty years earlier. Screens are usually blamed, but the reasons are probably more complex, including the pressure of examinations and the fact that many adults are rarely seen reading.

B Does it matter? The evidence suggests that it does. Researchers who followed thousands of British people from birth found that those who read for pleasure at the age of ten made more progress in vocabulary, spelling and even mathematics by sixteen than those who did not. Reading for pleasure had a greater effect than whether their parents had a university degree.

C Part of the explanation is simply exposure to words. Books, including children's books, contain many more rare words than everyday conversation or television does. A child who reads for twenty minutes a day meets well over a million words a year. Stories also seem to exercise the imagination in a social way: several studies have found that people who read a lot of fiction are better at understanding what others think and feel.

D How can the habit be encouraged? Forcing children to read specified books and write reports on them is often counter-productive. What appears to work is choice. Children read more when they select their own material, whether it is a comic, a magazine or a novel, when they have books at home, and when they see adults around them reading for enjoyment.

Passage 47. Learning Online Education & Psychology 259 words

Новые слова
  • enrol — записываться, зачисляться
  • publicity — огласка, внимание прессы
  • commentator — обозреватель

A In 2012 several leading American universities began offering free courses over the internet to anyone who wished to enrol. These 'massive open online courses', or MOOCs, attracted enormous publicity. A single course on artificial intelligence drew 160,000 students from almost every country, and some commentators predicted that traditional universities would soon be out of business.

B The reality proved less dramatic. Researchers found that only about five to ten per cent of those who signed up for a typical course ever completed it. Furthermore, most participants were not the disadvantaged learners the founders had hoped to reach; the majority already had a degree. Studying alone, without deadlines, classmates or a teacher who notices one's absence, requires a degree of self-discipline that many people lack.

C Online learning nevertheless continued to grow, and in 2020 the pandemic forced schools and universities worldwide to adopt it almost overnight. The experience was mixed. Lessons could continue, but younger children and those without a quiet place to work or a reliable internet connection fell behind, and teachers reported that it was hard to tell whether pupils on a screen had understood.

D The lesson that many educators have drawn is that technology works best in combination with human contact. In the 'flipped classroom', students watch recorded lectures at home and use class time for discussion and problem-solving with the teacher's help. Short videos followed by quizzes hold attention better than hour-long lectures. Online courses have also found a secure place in professional training, where motivated adults need to learn a specific skill without leaving their jobs.

Passage 48. Taking a Year Out Education & Psychology 269 words

Новые слова
  • roots — корни, истоки
  • volunteer — работать волонтёром
  • definite — определённый, чёткий

A In Britain, Australia and a growing number of other countries, it has become common for young people to take a 'gap year' between leaving school and starting university. The idea has older roots: in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries wealthy young Englishmen completed their education with a Grand Tour of Europe lasting many months.

B Today's gap years take many forms. Some students work to save money for their studies. Others travel, volunteer on projects abroad, or learn a language. A successful year usually combines several of these and has a definite plan.

C Supporters claim a number of benefits. Students arrive at university a year older, more independent and often clearer about what they want to study. Research in Australia found that those who had taken a gap year were more motivated than those who entered directly from school, and a study at a British university reported that they achieved slightly higher grades. Employers often say they value the practical skills and maturity that such experience brings.

D There are risks as well. A year spent mostly on the sofa is of little value, and some young people find it hard to return to study after earning a wage. Organised programmes abroad can cost thousands of pounds, which puts them beyond the reach of poorer families, and critics have questioned whether short-term volunteering by unskilled teenagers really helps the communities that receive them. In some cases it may even take paid work away from local people. Advisers therefore suggest that students should arrange their university place before the gap year begins, and choose projects run by organisations with long-term local partners.

Passage 49. Music Lessons and the Mind Education & Psychology 277 words

Новые слова
  • spatial — пространственный
  • reasoning — рассуждение, мышление
  • examination — изучение, проверка
  • demanding — требующий усилий
  • undertake — предпринимать
  • adjust — подстраиваться, корректировать
  • bundle — пучок

A In 1993 a short article in a scientific journal reported that students who listened to ten minutes of a Mozart sonata scored higher on a test of spatial reasoning than those who sat in silence. Newspapers called it the 'Mozart effect', and the idea that classical music makes children more intelligent spread rapidly. The governor of one American state even proposed giving a classical CD to every newborn baby.

B The claim did not survive careful examination. The original improvement lasted only about fifteen minutes, and later studies showed that the same small effect could be produced by anything that puts a listener in a good mood, including a lively pop song or an exciting story.

C Learning to play an instrument is a different matter. It is one of the most demanding activities the brain can undertake, requiring the player to read symbols, move both hands precisely, listen and adjust, all at the same time. Brain scans show that in professional musicians the bundle of fibres connecting the two halves of the brain is larger than in other people, particularly in those who began training before the age of seven.

D Whether these changes make musicians cleverer in general is less certain. Children who take music lessons do, on average, perform better at school, but they also tend to come from wealthier and better-educated families. Experiments that assign children to music lessons at random have found improvements in language skills, such as distinguishing similar sounds, while effects on general intelligence are small. Perhaps the wisest conclusion is that music is worth learning for its own sake: for discipline, for pleasure and for the experience of creating something with others.

4 Mozart himself began playing at the age of three. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 50. Pen or Keyboard? Education & Psychology 284 words

Новые слова
  • scratch — скрип, царапанье
  • tidy — аккуратный
  • obvious — очевидный
  • noticeably — заметно
  • keep up — поспевать
  • processing — обработка
  • stick — закрепляться, запоминаться
  • widespread — обширный, распространённый

A In lecture halls everywhere, the scratch of pens has largely been replaced by the tapping of keys. Typing is faster than handwriting, and typed notes are tidier and easier to search. It might seem obvious that laptops are the better tool for students. A series of experiments suggests otherwise.

B In a study published in 2014, the psychologists Pam Mueller and Daniel Oppenheimer asked university students to watch recorded talks and take notes either by hand or on a laptop that was not connected to the internet. Afterwards they were tested. On questions about simple facts the two groups did equally well, but on questions requiring understanding of ideas, those who had written by hand scored noticeably higher.

C The explanation seems to lie in speed itself. Students with laptops tended to type what the speaker said almost word for word. Those writing by hand could not keep up, so they were forced to listen, select the main points and express them in their own words. This mental processing is precisely what helps information to stick. Even when laptop users were told not to copy word for word, they found it hard to stop.

D Other research points in the same direction. Young children who practise forming letters by hand recognise them more easily than those who learn on a keyboard, and brain imaging shows more widespread activity during handwriting. It should be added that some later studies have failed to repeat Mueller and Oppenheimer's result exactly, so the debate is not closed. Nor does anyone suggest giving up computers. The sensible approach may be to use each tool for what it does best: the keyboard for producing long texts, and the pen for thinking and learning.

Passage 51. Exercise as Medicine Health 282 words

Новые слова
  • prescribe — прописывать (лекарство)
  • stroke — инсульт
  • moderate — умеренный
  • brisk — быстрый, энергичный
  • shift — смена (рабочая)
  • stimulate — стимулировать
  • trial — испытание, исследование
  • medication — лекарства

A If the benefits of physical activity could be put into a pill, it would be the most widely prescribed medicine in the world. Regular exercise lowers the risk of heart disease, stroke, type 2 diabetes and several cancers. It strengthens bones, helps to control weight and improves sleep. The World Health Organization recommends that adults do at least 150 minutes of moderate activity, such as brisk walking, every week, yet about a quarter of the world's adults fail to reach this level.

B The scientific study of the subject began in London in the late 1940s. A doctor named Jeremy Morris noticed that the drivers of the city's double-decker buses, who sat all day, had roughly twice as many heart attacks as the conductors, who climbed around 600 stairs during each shift collecting fares. Later he found a similar difference between office clerks and postmen who delivered letters on foot.

C The effects on the mind are equally impressive. Exercise increases the flow of blood to the brain and stimulates the release of chemicals that improve mood. Several trials have found that for mild depression a programme of regular exercise can work about as well as medication. In older people it appears to slow the decline of memory.

D Importantly, the greatest gain comes from the first step. The difference in health between people who do nothing and those who do a little is much larger than the difference between moderate exercisers and athletes. Nor is a gym necessary. Gardening, dancing, cycling to work and taking the stairs all count. Researchers add a warning, however: an hour of exercise does not fully cancel out the harm of sitting still for the rest of the day.

Passage 52. Sweet and Dangerous Health 278 words

Новые слова
  • ancestor — предок
  • plantation — плантация
  • consumption — потребление
  • exceed — превышать
  • decay — разрушение, кариес
  • obesity — ожирение
  • play down — преуменьшать
  • levy — сбор, налог

A For most of history sugar was a rare luxury. Honey and ripe fruit were the only sweet foods available to our ancestors, and scientists believe that the human liking for sweetness evolved because it signalled a safe and valuable source of energy. Cane sugar reached Europe in quantity only after plantations were established in the Caribbean in the seventeenth century, worked by enslaved Africans. Even in 1700 the average English person ate less than two kilograms a year.

B Today consumption in many countries exceeds thirty kilograms per person. Much of it is hidden. Sugar is added not only to sweets and soft drinks but also to bread, sauces, breakfast cereals and yoghurts labelled as healthy. A single can of cola contains about nine teaspoons, more than the World Health Organization's suggested limit for a whole day.

C The consequences for health are serious. Sugar is the main cause of tooth decay. Sugary drinks are particularly harmful because liquid calories do not make people feel full, so they are consumed on top of normal meals; high consumption is strongly linked to obesity and type 2 diabetes. For several decades public attention was focused instead on fat, partly, as documents later revealed, because the sugar industry in the 1960s paid scientists to play down the risks of its product.

D Governments have begun to respond. More than fifty countries have introduced a tax on sugary drinks. In Britain a levy announced in 2016 led manufacturers to cut the sugar in many drinks before it even came into force. Critics object that such taxes fall most heavily on the poor, while supporters reply that the poor also suffer most from the diseases concerned.

Passage 53. The First Vaccine Health 278 words

Новые слова
  • infect — заражать
  • scar — оставлять шрамы; шрам
  • sore — язва, болячка
  • procedure — процедура
  • ambassador — посол
  • unethical — неэтичный
  • eliminate — устранять, ликвидировать
  • eradicate — искоренять

A Smallpox was one of the most feared diseases in history. It killed about three in ten of those it infected and left survivors scarred and sometimes blind. In eighteenth-century Europe it is thought to have caused 400,000 deaths every year.

B People had long known that those who recovered never caught the disease again. In China, India and the Ottoman Empire this knowledge was used in a risky procedure: material from a smallpox sore was scratched into the skin of a healthy person, who usually developed a mild illness and was then protected. Lady Mary Wortley Montagu, wife of the British ambassador in Constantinople, observed the practice and introduced it to England in 1721. It worked, but a small proportion of those treated died.

C A safer method came from the English countryside. It was commonly said that milkmaids who had caught cowpox, a mild disease of cattle, did not get smallpox. In 1796 the doctor Edward Jenner decided to test this. He took fluid from a cowpox sore on the hand of a milkmaid named Sarah Nelmes and scratched it into the arm of James Phipps, the eight-year-old son of his gardener. Six weeks later he exposed the boy to smallpox. James remained healthy. The new technique was named vaccination, from vacca, the Latin word for cow.

D By modern standards the experiment was highly unethical, but its results spread around the world within a few years. In 1967 the World Health Organization launched a campaign to eliminate smallpox entirely. The last natural case occurred in Somalia in 1977, and in 1980 the disease was declared eradicated, the first, and so far the only, human disease to be wiped out.

2 The older method of protection brought from Constantinople was completely safe. ✔ FALSE
3 James Phipps was Jenner's own son. ✔ FALSE
4 Jenner was paid by the government for his discovery. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 54. The End of the Antibiotic Age? Health 277 words

Новые слова
  • mould — плесень
  • infection — инфекция
  • curable — излечимый
  • resistant — устойчивый
  • natural selection — естественный отбор
  • prescription — рецепт
  • pharmaceutical — фармацевтический
  • incentive — стимул

A In 1928 the Scottish scientist Alexander Fleming returned from holiday to find that mould had grown on one of his laboratory dishes and that the bacteria around it had died. The substance responsible, penicillin, was turned into a usable drug during the Second World War, and it transformed medicine. Infections that had regularly killed people, from pneumonia to an infected scratch, became easily curable. Operations such as transplants, which leave patients open to infection, became possible.

B Fleming himself predicted the problem that now threatens these gains. In his Nobel Prize lecture of 1945 he warned that using too little penicillin, or using it carelessly, would allow bacteria to become resistant. The mechanism is natural selection. In any large population of bacteria a few may by chance be able to survive a drug. When the others are killed, these survivors multiply, and bacteria can even pass resistance to different species.

C Human behaviour has speeded up the process enormously. Antibiotics are frequently prescribed for colds and flu, which are caused by viruses and are unaffected by them. In some countries they can be bought without a prescription. Vast quantities are also given to healthy farm animals to make them grow faster. Resistant infections are already estimated to cause more than a million deaths a year worldwide.

D Meanwhile, few new antibiotics are being developed. A drug that is taken for a week and deliberately used as little as possible earns much less than one taken daily for years, so pharmaceutical companies have little incentive. Proposed solutions include public funding for research, stricter rules on farm use, and rapid tests that tell a doctor whether an infection is bacterial at all.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

Passage 55. The Simplest Exercise Health 276 words

Новые слова
  • equipment — оборудование, снаряжение
  • fee — плата, взнос
  • pedometer — шагомер
  • markedly — заметно, существенно
  • levelled off (level off) — выравниваться, стабилизироваться
  • pace — темп
  • stroll — неторопливая прогулка
  • pavement — тротуар

A Walking is so ordinary that it hardly seems to deserve the name of exercise. It needs no equipment, no training and no membership fee. Yet a large body of research indicates that it is one of the most effective things people can do for their health.

B Many people aim for 10,000 steps a day, but that figure has no scientific origin. It comes from an advertising campaign for a Japanese pedometer sold in 1965, whose name meant '10,000-step meter'. Recent studies give a more encouraging picture. In a study of about 17,000 older American women, those who took around 4,400 steps a day had a markedly lower death rate over the following years than those who took 2,700. The benefit continued to rise up to about 7,500 steps and then levelled off.

C Pace matters too. Brisk walking, fast enough to raise the heart rate but still allow conversation, does more good than a slow stroll. Short walks after meals appear to be particularly useful, because they reduce the sharp rise in blood sugar that follows eating.

D The advantages are not only physical. Experiments at Stanford University found that people produced far more creative ideas while walking than while sitting, which may explain why so many writers and thinkers, from Aristotle to Charles Darwin, were habitual walkers. Walking in parks or woodland has also been shown to lower stress more than walking along busy streets.

E Sadly, modern cities often discourage walking. Wide roads, long distances between homes and shops, and a lack of pavements make the car the only practical choice. Planners are now trying to design neighbourhoods in which daily needs lie within a fifteen-minute walk.

Passage 56. The Science of Laughter Health 274 words

Новые слова
  • inborn — врождённый
  • assume — полагать, предполагать
  • episode — эпизод, случай
  • remark — замечание, реплика
  • tickle — щекотать
  • mock — ненастоящий, притворный
  • exaggerate — преувеличивать
  • loneliness — одиночество

A Laughter is one of the few behaviours shared by every human culture. Babies laugh at around three or four months, long before they can speak, and people who are born deaf and blind laugh in the same way as everyone else, which shows that it is an inborn behaviour rather than a learned one.

B We tend to assume that people laugh because something is funny. When the American psychologist Robert Provine sent his students to listen to conversations in public places, they found something different. Of more than a thousand episodes of laughter that they recorded, fewer than a fifth followed anything resembling a joke. Most came after ordinary remarks such as 'I'll see you later'. Speakers laughed more than their listeners, and people were about thirty times more likely to laugh in company than when alone. Laughter, Provine concluded, is above all a social signal, a way of showing friendliness and agreement.

C Its origins seem to lie in play. Young chimpanzees and gorillas make a panting sound when they are tickled or chase each other, and even rats produce high-pitched chirps, too high for human ears, when they play. The sound tells a partner that a mock attack is not serious.

D Is laughter also 'the best medicine'? The claims are often exaggerated, but there is some evidence. Laughing with others raises the level at which people begin to feel pain, apparently because it releases the brain's natural painkillers, endorphins. It also reduces stress hormones for a short time. Perhaps its most important effect, however, is indirect: by strengthening relationships, laughter helps to protect people from loneliness, which is itself a danger to health.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Babies learn to laugh by copying adults. ✔ FALSE
2 Most laughter recorded in Provine's study followed jokes. ✔ FALSE
3 People laugh much more often when they are with others. ✔ TRUE
4 Rats' play sounds can be heard easily by humans. ✔ FALSE

Passage 57. Meditation under the Microscope Health 274 words

Новые слова
  • mindfulness — осознанность
  • deliberate — намеренный, сознательный
  • wander — блуждать, отвлекаться
  • relieve — облегчать
  • relapse — рецидив
  • promote — продвигать, рекламировать
  • minority — меньшинство
  • workload — рабочая нагрузка

A Meditation has been practised in Asia for at least 2,500 years, mainly within religious traditions such as Buddhism and Hinduism. In recent decades a non-religious form called mindfulness has become popular in the West. It involves paying deliberate attention to the present moment, typically by concentrating on one's breathing, and gently bringing the mind back each time it wanders.

B Its entry into medicine is usually dated to 1979, when Jon Kabat-Zinn, a scientist at the University of Massachusetts, designed an eight-week course for hospital patients suffering from long-term pain that doctors had been unable to relieve. Many reported that although the pain remained, it troubled them less. A later variation of the course, combined with elements of psychological therapy, has been shown in controlled trials to reduce the risk of relapse in people who have had repeated episodes of depression, and it is now recommended by the British health service for that purpose.

C Enthusiasm has, however, run ahead of the evidence. Mindfulness has been promoted to schools, companies and armies as a solution for almost everything. When researchers at Johns Hopkins University reviewed thousands of studies in 2014, they found that most were too small or poorly designed to be reliable. The good-quality trials showed moderate benefits for anxiety, depression and pain, but little effect on attention, sleep or weight.

D Nor is meditation free of risk. A minority of participants report unpleasant experiences, including increased anxiety. Critics add that offering stressed employees a meditation app may distract attention from the causes of their stress, such as excessive workloads. Meditation, it seems, is a useful tool for some people, not a cure for all problems.

Passage 58. Green Spaces and Health Health 277 words

Новые слова
  • recover — выздоравливать
  • identical — одинаковый
  • painkiller — обезболивающее
  • overweight — с избыточным весом
  • income — доход
  • filter — фильтровать, очищать

A In 1984 a researcher named Roger Ulrich published a study that has since become famous. He examined the records of patients who had had the same operation in a hospital in Pennsylvania. Some had recovered in rooms with a window facing trees, others in identical rooms facing a brick wall. Those with the view of trees went home, on average, almost a day earlier, needed fewer strong painkillers and received fewer negative comments in the nurses' notes.

B Since then hundreds of studies have explored the connection between nature and health. People who live near parks tend to be more physically active and less overweight. A large Danish study, which followed nearly a million people, found that children who grew up with little green space around their homes had a risk of developing mental illness in later life up to fifty-five per cent higher than those who grew up with the most, even after family income and other factors were taken into account.

C Several explanations have been suggested. Green areas encourage walking and play, and they bring neighbours into contact with one another. Trees filter polluted air, reduce noise and cool streets in summer. There may also be a direct psychological effect: natural scenes seem to hold our attention gently, allowing a tired mind to rest in a way that busy streets do not.

D City authorities are beginning to act on such findings. Some are planting trees along roads and turning disused railway lines into parks. In Japan the practice of 'forest bathing', spending quiet time among trees, is officially encouraged, and doctors in several countries have begun to give 'green prescriptions' advising patients to spend time outdoors.

Passage 59. Where People Live Longest Health 276 words

Новые слова
  • demographer — демограф
  • peninsula — полуостров
  • inhabitant — житель
  • grain — зерно, злак
  • shepherd — пастух
  • steep — крутой
  • isolated — изолированный, одинокий
  • unreliable — ненадёжный

A In a few places in the world an unusually high proportion of people live to ninety or a hundred in good health. The writer Dan Buettner, working with demographers, identified five of them and called them 'Blue Zones': the Japanese island of Okinawa, the mountain villages of Sardinia in Italy, the Greek island of Ikaria, the Nicoya peninsula in Costa Rica, and a religious community in Loma Linda, California.

B Although these places are far apart, their inhabitants have a good deal in common. Their diet consists largely of plants, especially beans, vegetables and whole grains, with meat eaten only occasionally. In Okinawa people follow an old saying that advises them to stop eating when the stomach is eighty per cent full. Nobody goes to a gym, but everyone moves constantly: Sardinian shepherds walk for miles over steep hills, and Okinawans tend their gardens into their nineties.

C Social factors appear just as important. Older people in these communities are not isolated. They live with or near their families and have a recognised role. Okinawans have a word, ikigai, which means roughly 'a reason for getting up in the morning'. Many also belong to small groups of lifelong friends who support one another.

D The Blue Zones idea has been questioned. Some researchers have pointed out that in certain regions birth records were unreliable, so a number of supposed hundred-year-olds may be younger than claimed. In Okinawa, moreover, the advantage is disappearing as younger generations adopt fast food and cars. Even so, the general advice drawn from these communities, to eat mostly plants, keep moving and stay connected to others, agrees well with what medical research has found elsewhere.

Passage 60. The Doctor Who Washed His Hands Health 275 words

Новые слова
  • maternity — родильный, материнство
  • midwife — акушерка
  • beg — умолять
  • breakthrough — прорыв
  • ward — палата, отделение
  • corpse — труп
  • hostile — враждебный
  • germ — микроб

A In the 1840s Vienna General Hospital had two maternity clinics. In the first, where babies were delivered by doctors and medical students, about one mother in ten died of childbed fever. In the second, staffed by midwives, the death rate was less than a third of that. Women begged not to be sent to the first clinic.

B A young Hungarian doctor, Ignaz Semmelweis, was determined to find out why. He examined and rejected one explanation after another: overcrowding, diet, even the position in which women gave birth. The breakthrough came in 1847 after a tragedy. A colleague cut his finger while examining a dead body and died of an illness exactly like childbed fever. Semmelweis realised that the doctors and students often came straight from the dissecting room to the maternity ward. Midwives never handled dead bodies. Something from the corpses, he reasoned, was being carried on the doctors' hands.

C He ordered everyone to wash their hands in a chlorine solution before examining patients. Within months the death rate in the first clinic fell to around one or two per cent.

D The reaction of the medical profession was hostile. Semmelweis could not explain what the 'particles' were, since the role of germs in disease had not yet been discovered, and many doctors were offended by the suggestion that they themselves were killing their patients. He lost his post, grew increasingly bitter, and died in an asylum in 1865, aged forty-seven. Only later, when Louis Pasteur and Joseph Lister established the germ theory, was he proved right. Today hand washing is recognised as the single most effective way of preventing the spread of infection in hospitals.

4 Semmelweis's wife supported his campaign. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 61. Working from Home Society & Business 272 words

Новые слова
  • productivity — производительность
  • commuting — поездки на работу и обратно
  • resign — увольняться
  • promote — повышать в должности
  • drawback — недостаток

A Before 2020 only a small minority of employees worked mainly from home. The pandemic changed this within weeks, as offices closed and millions of people set up laptops on kitchen tables. Many expected productivity to collapse. For the most part it did not, and when offices reopened a large number of workers were unwilling to return to their old routine.

B The attractions are obvious. Commuting disappears, saving the average worker about an hour a day as well as the cost of travel. Parents can collect children from school, and people can live farther from expensive city centres. In a well-known experiment at a Chinese travel company, carried out by the economist Nicholas Bloom, call-centre staff who were randomly chosen to work at home completed thirteen per cent more calls than those in the office, and far fewer of them resigned.

C The same experiment revealed a cost, however: the home workers were promoted less often, apparently because managers forgot about people they did not see. Other drawbacks have become clear since. New employees learn less when they cannot watch experienced colleagues. Chance conversations in corridors, which often lead to fresh ideas, do not happen on video calls. Some people feel lonely, and the line between work and private life becomes blurred when the office is also the bedroom.

D Most organisations have therefore settled on a hybrid arrangement, typically three days in the office and two at home. Bloom's later research suggests that this pattern keeps employees satisfied without harming performance. The consequences reach beyond the workplace. City-centre cafés and shops have lost customers, while demand for larger homes in the suburbs has grown.

Passage 62. Too Many Tourists Society & Business 270 words

Новые слова
  • arrival — прибытие

  • resident — житель

A In 1950 there were about twenty-five million international tourist arrivals worldwide. By 2019 the figure had reached almost one and a half billion. Cheap flights, rising incomes and websites that let anyone rent out a spare room have put foreign travel within reach of a vast number of people. For many countries tourism is a vital source of jobs and income. But in the most popular places the crowds have begun to destroy the very things that visitors come to see.

B Venice is the classic case. The historic centre has fewer than 50,000 residents, yet it receives around twenty million visitors a year, many of them arriving on cruise ships for only a few hours. Such day trippers spend little but add greatly to the congestion. Shops selling bread and hardware have been replaced by souvenir stalls, and rents have risen as flats are turned into holiday accommodation. The local population has fallen by more than half since the 1950s.

C Natural sites suffer too. Maya Bay in Thailand, made famous by a Hollywood film, was receiving up to 5,000 visitors a day when the authorities closed it in 2018 because most of its coral had died. It reopened in 2022 with strict limits on numbers, and no boats may now enter the bay.

D Other destinations are experimenting with similar controls. Venice has introduced an entry fee for day visitors on busy dates. Amsterdam has stopped advertising itself and banned new hotels in the centre. Bhutan charges every tourist a substantial daily tax. Experts also recommend spreading visitors more evenly, by promoting less well-known regions and travel outside the peak season.

Passage 63. The Art of Persuasion Society & Business 270 words

Новые слова
  • influence — влиять
  • desire — желание
  • associate — связывать, ассоциировать
  • longed for (long for) — страстно желать
  • boast — хвастаться
  • scarcity — дефицит, нехватка
  • track — отслеживать
  • privacy — неприкосновенность частной жизни

A Advertising is as old as trade. Shopkeepers in ancient Pompeii painted notices on their walls, and the first newspaper advertisements appeared in the seventeenth century. For a long time advertisements simply announced that a product existed and described what it did.

B That changed in the early twentieth century, when manufacturers discovered that people buy for emotional reasons at least as much as for practical ones. An important figure was Edward Bernays, a nephew of Sigmund Freud, who applied his uncle's ideas about hidden desires to selling. Instead of praising a product, advertisers began to associate it with things people longed for: love, status, freedom or youth. A car was no longer merely a means of transport but a symbol of success.

C Psychologists have since identified a number of techniques that reliably influence us. One is social proof: we are more likely to choose something if we believe many others have done so, which is why sellers boast of being 'the nation's favourite'. Another is scarcity; an offer that is 'for a limited time only' seems more desirable. Simple repetition is effective too, because familiar names feel more trustworthy.

D The internet has transformed the industry once more. Traditional advertisements were shown to everybody, and a famous saying held that half the money spent was wasted, though nobody knew which half. Online advertisers, by contrast, can track what individuals search for and buy, and can direct messages at precisely the people most likely to respond. This is efficient, but it has raised serious concerns about privacy, and about the targeting of children and of people who are vulnerable, for instance to gambling.

Passage 64. The Four-Day Week Society & Business 273 words

Новые слова
  • leisure — досуг
  • reduce — сокращать
  • revenue — выручка, доход
  • arrangement — договорённость, схема
  • interrupt — прерывать
  • sceptic — скептик
  • volunteer — вызываться добровольно
  • intense — напряжённый

A The two-day weekend is a relatively recent invention. In 1926 Henry Ford closed his car factories on Saturdays as well as Sundays, partly on the reasoning that workers with more leisure would buy more goods, including cars. A century later, some employers are asking whether the week could be shortened again.

B The largest test so far took place in Britain in 2022. About sixty companies, with nearly 3,000 employees between them, agreed to reduce working time to four days for six months with no cut in pay. In exchange, staff promised to maintain their output. At the end, the companies' revenue was broadly unchanged, the number of people resigning had fallen by more than half, and employees reported less stress and better sleep. More than ninety per cent of the firms decided to continue the arrangement.

C How is it possible to do five days' work in four? Participants said the answer lay in removing wasted time. Meetings were shortened or cancelled, and periods were set aside in which nobody could be interrupted. Research has long suggested that in office jobs only a few hours of each day are truly productive.

D Sceptics are not convinced. The companies in the trial had volunteered, so they were likely to be those best suited to the change; most were small firms in fields such as marketing and technology. In hospitals, schools, restaurants and factories, where staff must be present for fixed hours, a shorter week means hiring more people. Some workers also found that squeezing the same tasks into fewer days made each day more intense. Whether the idea can spread through the whole economy remains uncertain.

Passage 65. The Global Journey of Coffee Society & Business 285 words

Новые слова
  • goatherd — пастух коз
  • native — родом из, местный
  • cultivate — выращивать
  • prayer — молитва
  • ruler — правитель
  • nickname — давать прозвище
  • insurance — страхование
  • threat — угроза

A According to a popular legend, coffee was discovered in Ethiopia by a goatherd called Kaldi, who noticed that his goats became lively after eating the red berries of a certain bush. Whether or not Kaldi existed, the coffee plant is certainly native to the Ethiopian highlands. By the fifteenth century it was being cultivated across the Red Sea in Yemen, where religious communities drank it to stay awake during night-time prayers.

B From Yemen the drink spread through the Islamic world. Coffee houses opened in Mecca, Cairo and Istanbul, and they quickly became places where men gathered to talk, play chess and exchange news. Rulers who feared that such conversation might turn to politics tried several times to close them, without lasting success. The same pattern was repeated in Europe after coffee arrived in the seventeenth century. In London, coffee houses were nicknamed 'penny universities', because for the price of a cup anyone could listen to learned discussion. The insurance market Lloyd's of London began in one of them.

C For a long time Yemen guarded its monopoly, exporting only beans that could not grow. Eventually the Dutch obtained living plants and established plantations in Java, and the French and Portuguese followed in the Caribbean and Brazil. Brazil has been the world's largest producer for more than 150 years.

D Coffee today supports some twenty-five million farming families, most of them working small plots. Yet they receive only a small share of the price paid in a café, and their income swings sharply with the world market. Climate change is a further threat: the arabica plant is sensitive to heat, and studies suggest that around half of the land now suitable for it could be lost by 2050.

Passage 66. Sharing Instead of Owning Society & Business 273 words

Новые слова
  • drill — дрель
  • seldom — редко
  • enthusiast — энтузиаст
  • lend — одалживать
  • landlord — арендодатель
  • contractor — подрядчик
  • genuine — подлинный, настоящий
  • scale — масштаб

A A typical private car is parked for about ninety-five per cent of its life. An electric drill may be used for only a few minutes in total before it is thrown away. Observations like these lie behind the idea of the sharing economy, which became fashionable around 2010. Why should everybody own things that are seldom used, when the internet makes it easy to borrow them from a neighbour?

B Early enthusiasts imagined a world of neighbours lending tools and offering spare seats in their cars. The promised benefits were social and environmental as well as financial: fewer goods would need to be manufactured, and strangers would learn to trust one another through online reviews.

C What actually grew was rather different. The most successful platforms were not really about sharing but about renting. Home-rental sites began with people letting a spare bed, but a large proportion of listings now belong to professional landlords with several properties, and in popular cities this has reduced the housing available to residents. Ride services have given passengers a cheap and convenient alternative to taxis, yet their drivers are generally treated as independent contractors, without a guaranteed wage, paid holidays or sick pay. Courts in several countries have been asked to decide whether they are in fact employees.

D Meanwhile, genuine sharing has survived on a smaller scale. 'Libraries of things' lend out tools, tents and kitchen equipment for a small fee, and community repair cafés help people fix broken items. Car clubs, in which members book a vehicle by the hour, appear to deliver a real environmental gain: studies indicate that each shared car replaces several privately owned ones.

Passage 67. The Age of the Megacity Society & Business 272 words

Новые слова
  • inhabitant — житель
  • density — плотность
  • specialised — специализированный
  • settlement — поселение
  • slum — трущоба
  • sewer — канализация
  • dweller — житель, обитатель
  • demolish — сносить

A In 1950 only two cities on Earth, New York and Tokyo, had more than ten million inhabitants. Today there are more than thirty such 'megacities', and most of the new ones are in Asia and Africa. Greater Tokyo remains the largest, with about thirty-seven million people, but cities such as Delhi, Dhaka and Lagos are growing far faster.

B People move to cities for good reasons. Urban areas offer jobs, schools, hospitals and a variety of opportunities that villages cannot match. Economists point out that density makes people more productive: when firms and workers are close together, ideas spread and specialised businesses can find customers. Cities generate more than eighty per cent of the world's economic output.

C Rapid growth, however, often outruns planning. Around a billion people live in informal settlements, or slums, without secure housing, clean water or sewers. Traffic congestion wastes hours of every working day, and air pollution in many megacities far exceeds safe limits. Cities built on coasts and river deltas, as many are, face increasing danger from floods.

D Yet big cities can also be surprisingly efficient. Because residents live in smaller homes and travel shorter distances, often by public transport, the average city dweller in a rich country uses less energy than someone living in the suburbs or the countryside. The task for governments is to capture these advantages while reducing the harm. Successful examples exist. Seoul demolished an elevated motorway and restored the river beneath it as a park, and the Colombian city of Medellín linked its poor hillside districts to the centre with cable cars, cutting journey times from over an hour to a few minutes.

Passage 68. Too Much Choice Society & Business 278 words

Новые слова
  • self-evident — самоочевидный
  • assumption — предположение
  • sample — образец, проба
  • variety — разновидность
  • reverse — менять на противоположное
  • paradox — парадокс
  • reject — отвергать
  • default — вариант по умолчанию

A It seems self-evident that the more options we have, the better off we are. A shop with a hundred kinds of tea must serve its customers better than one with ten. This assumption was challenged by a now famous experiment conducted in a California supermarket in 2000 by the psychologists Sheena Iyengar and Mark Lepper.

B On some days they set up a table offering samples of twenty-four varieties of jam; on others the table offered only six. The large display attracted more shoppers. But when it came to buying, the results were reversed: about thirty per cent of those who stopped at the small display bought a jar, compared with only three per cent at the large one.

C The psychologist Barry Schwartz popularised the idea as 'the paradox of choice'. Too many options, he argued, make deciding harder work, and after deciding we are less satisfied, because it is easy to imagine that one of the rejected alternatives would have been better. He distinguished two types of people. 'Maximisers' feel they must find the very best and compare endlessly, whereas 'satisficers' settle for something that is good enough. In his studies maximisers often obtained objectively better results, such as higher starting salaries, yet felt less happy with them.

D Later research has complicated the story. When scientists combined the results of some fifty similar experiments, the average effect of having more choice was close to zero: sometimes it discouraged buyers and sometimes it did not. Overload seems most likely when the options are hard to compare and people have no clear preference beforehand. For businesses the practical lesson is to organise choices into categories and offer a sensible default.

1 More shoppers stopped at the table with twenty-four jams. ✔ TRUE
2 Shoppers at the larger display were more likely to buy. ✔ FALSE
3 Maximisers tend to feel happier with their decisions than satisficers. ✔ FALSE
4 The jam experiment was repeated in the same supermarket ten years later. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Complete the summary. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

Passage 69. Why People Volunteer Society & Business 272 words

Новые слова
  • coach — тренировать
  • workforce — рабочая сила
  • motive — мотив
  • cause — дело, идея (общественная)
  • retirement — выход на пенсию
  • duty — долг, обязанность
  • commit — брать на себя обязательство

A Every year hundreds of millions of people around the world give their time without payment. They coach children's football teams, serve meals to the homeless, staff lifeboats and clear rubbish from beaches. If volunteers were counted as a workforce, it would be one of the largest on the planet.

B Their motives are varied. Surveys show that most volunteers wish to help others or to support a cause they believe in. Many also mention more personal reasons: meeting people, learning skills, gaining experience that may lead to a job, or simply filling time after retirement. Researchers do not regard these mixed motives as a problem. Volunteers who gain something for themselves tend to continue for longer than those driven by duty alone.

C There is good evidence that volunteering benefits the giver. Studies following older adults over many years have found that those who volunteer regularly report greater satisfaction with life and, on average, live longer, even when their initial health is taken into account. The benefit seems to come from having a sense of purpose and regular social contact. Interestingly, it appears at quite modest levels, around two hours a week, and volunteering much more than that brings no additional gain.

D Organisations that depend on volunteers face a challenge, however. In many countries people are less willing than before to commit to a fixed weekly duty over many years. They prefer short projects that fit around work and family. Charities have responded by offering one-day events, tasks that can be done online, and 'micro-volunteering' through phone apps, which can take just a few minutes, such as describing a photograph for a blind person.

Passage 70. Businesses Owned by Their Members Society & Business 275 words

Новые слова
  • weaver — ткач
  • adulterated — с примесями, фальсифицированный
  • crucial — ключевой
  • cooperative — кооператив
  • dividend — дивиденд
  • surplus — излишек, прибыль
  • loan — заём, кредит
  • shareholder — акционер

A In 1844 twenty-eight workers in the English town of Rochdale, most of them weavers, opened a small shop selling flour, butter, sugar and oatmeal. They were tired of shopkeepers who charged high prices for goods that were often adulterated; flour, for instance, was sometimes mixed with chalk. Their shop was different in one crucial respect: it belonged to its customers.

B The rules they drew up, later known as the Rochdale Principles, became the foundation of the cooperative movement. Membership was open to all. Each member had one vote, regardless of how much money he or she had invested. Goods were to be pure and honestly weighed. Profits were returned to members in proportion to what they had bought, a payment called the dividend. A share of the surplus was set aside for education.

C The model was copied widely. Today around a billion people worldwide are members of some kind of cooperative. Farmers join together to sell their milk or grain and to buy equipment. Credit unions offer savings and loans to their members. In worker cooperatives the employees themselves own the firm; the best known is Mondragon in the Basque region of Spain, a group of businesses with tens of thousands of worker-owners, where the highest-paid manager may earn only a few times more than the lowest-paid worker.

D Supporters argue that cooperatives are more stable than ordinary companies, since they are not under pressure from outside shareholders to maximise short-term profit, and studies indicate that they survive economic crises rather better. Their weakness is finance. Because they cannot sell shares to investors, they often find it hard to raise the money needed to grow quickly.

4 The original Rochdale shop is now a museum. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 71. What Are Museums For? Culture & Arts 275 words

Новые слова
  • institution — учреждение
  • assemble — собирать
  • cram — набивать битком
  • admit — впускать
  • studious — прилежный, любознательный
  • interactive — интерактивный
  • acquire — приобретать
  • colonial — колониальный

A The word 'museum' comes from the Greek for a temple of the Muses, the goddesses of the arts, but the modern institution has a more recent origin. In sixteenth- and seventeenth-century Europe, princes and wealthy scholars assembled 'cabinets of curiosities': rooms crammed with shells, stuffed animals, ancient coins and strange objects from distant lands. These collections were private and were shown only to invited guests.

B The first public museums appeared in the eighteenth century. The British Museum opened in London in 1759, admitting 'all studious and curious persons' free of charge, although at first visitors had to apply for a ticket in advance. After the French Revolution the royal art collection in the Louvre was opened to the people in 1793. The idea behind these institutions was that knowledge and beauty should not belong only to the rich.

C For much of their history museums were quiet places where objects sat in glass cases with small labels. Since the late twentieth century many have tried to become more lively, with interactive displays, activities for children and late-night events. Science museums in particular encourage visitors to touch and experiment.

D Museums today also face difficult questions about their past. Many famous objects were acquired during the colonial period, sometimes by force. Greece has long asked for the return of the marble sculptures removed from the Parthenon in Athens in the early nineteenth century and now held in London, and Nigeria has requested the Benin Bronzes, taken by British soldiers in 1897. Some museums have begun to send such items back; others argue that they can be seen by more people, and in a wider context, where they are.

Passage 72. The Origins of Music Culture & Arts 270 words

Новые слова
  • funeral — похороны
  • worship — богослужение, поклонение
  • ivory — слоновая (мамонтовая) кость
  • courtship — ухаживание
  • soothing — успокаивающий
  • lullaby — колыбельная
  • bonding — укрепление связей
  • by-product — побочный продукт

A Every human society that has ever been studied has music. People sing to their babies, at weddings and funerals, at work and in worship. This universality suggests that music is very old, and archaeology confirms it. Flutes made from the bones of birds and from mammoth ivory, found in caves in southern Germany, are around 40,000 years old. Since singing and clapping leave no trace, music itself must be far older.

B Why humans became musical is a question that has divided scientists since Charles Darwin. Darwin proposed that music began as a form of courtship, like birdsong: individuals who sang well attracted mates. Others think it grew out of the soothing sounds that mothers make to infants. Parents everywhere speak to babies in a high, melodic voice, and lullabies around the world share similar features.

C A third theory emphasises group bonding. When people sing, drum or dance together, they move in time with one another, and experiments show that this synchrony increases trust and willingness to cooperate. For our ancestors, who depended on the group for survival, anything that strengthened such bonds would have been valuable. Soldiers marching in step and workers singing as they pull on ropes are familiar examples.

D Not everyone agrees that music needs an evolutionary explanation. The psychologist Steven Pinker famously called it 'auditory cheesecake': a pleasant by-product of abilities that evolved for other purposes, such as language. Whatever its origin, music affects us deeply. Brain scans show that a favourite piece activates the same reward system as food, and familiar songs can awaken memories in people with dementia who no longer recognise their own relatives.

4 Pinker himself plays a musical instrument. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 73. The Birth of the Cinema Culture & Arts 280 words

Новые слова
  • basement — подвал
  • audience — зрители, публика
  • hose — шланг
  • project — проецировать
  • spectator — зритель
  • terror — ужас
  • magician — фокусник
  • synchronised — синхронизированный

A On 28 December 1895, in the basement of a Paris café, the brothers Auguste and Louis Lumière showed ten short films to a paying audience of about thirty people. Each film lasted less than a minute and showed an everyday scene: workers leaving a factory, a baby being fed, a gardener being soaked with his own hose. The event is generally regarded as the birth of the cinema.

B The Lumières were not the only inventors working on moving pictures. In the United States Thomas Edison's company had already produced the Kinetoscope, a box in which one person at a time could watch a film through a small window. The Lumières' machine, the Cinématographe, had two advantages. It projected the image onto a screen, so that many people could watch together, and it was light enough to be carried anywhere, serving as camera, printer and projector in one. A well-known story claims that when the brothers showed a film of a train arriving at a station, spectators ran away in terror; historians now doubt that this ever happened.

C Curiously, Louis Lumière is said to have believed that the cinema was 'an invention without a future'. It was others who saw its possibilities. Georges Méliès, a professional magician, discovered by accident that stopping the camera could make objects appear to vanish, and he went on to make fantasy films such as A Trip to the Moon in 1902.

D For three decades films had no recorded dialogue, though they were rarely silent, since a pianist or orchestra played in the cinema. The Jazz Singer of 1927 was the first feature film with synchronised speech, and within a few years silent films had disappeared.

Passage 74. Writing on the Wall Culture & Arts 272 words

Новые слова
  • graffiti — граффити
  • reputation — репутация
  • carriage — вагон
  • vandalism — вандализм
  • celebrated — прославленный
  • stencil — трафарет
  • rebellion — бунт
  • mural — настенная роспись

A People have always written and drawn on walls; examples survive from ancient Rome. Modern graffiti, however, began in the late 1960s in Philadelphia and New York, when teenagers started writing their nicknames, or 'tags', on walls and subway trains. The aim was fame: the more often a name appeared, and the more dangerous the location, the greater the writer's reputation. By the 1970s tags had grown into huge, colourful paintings covering whole train carriages.

B City authorities saw only vandalism. New York spent many millions of dollars cleaning its trains and by 1989 had removed the last painted carriage from service. Yet at the same time art galleries were beginning to take an interest. Jean-Michel Basquiat, who had started out spraying short poetic messages on walls in Manhattan, became one of the most celebrated painters of the 1980s.

C Since then a broader movement known as street art has developed, using stencils, posters and sculpture as well as spray paint, and usually aiming to communicate with the public rather than with other writers. Its best-known figure is the British artist Banksy, whose identity has never been officially confirmed. His images, often humorous and political, appear overnight, and they are now so valuable that walls bearing them have been cut out and sold.

D This success has created a paradox. An art form that began as illegal rebellion is now used by city councils to attract tourists and by property developers to make neighbourhoods fashionable. Districts once considered dangerous, such as Shoreditch in London, have become expensive partly because of their murals, and the artists who painted them can often no longer afford to live there.

Passage 75. Before Writing: The Storytellers Culture & Arts 276 words

Новые слова
  • vehicle — средство (передачи)
  • narrative — повествование
  • oral — устный
  • rhyme — рифма
  • epic — эпический
  • stock — запас
  • accurate — точный
  • appetite — интерес, тяга

A Writing was invented only about five thousand years ago, and for most of the time since then few people could read. For the greater part of human history, therefore, everything a society knew, its history, its laws, its practical skills and its beliefs, had to be carried in memory and passed on by word of mouth.

B Stories were the main vehicle. A list of facts is hard to remember, but a narrative with characters and dramatic events sticks in the mind. Oral traditions also used rhythm, rhyme and repeated phrases as aids to memory. The scholars Milman Parry and Albert Lord, studying singers of epic poems in the Balkans in the 1930s, found men who could perform poems thousands of lines long. They had not learned the words by heart; instead they rebuilt the poem at each performance from a stock of ready-made phrases. Parry argued that Homer's Iliad and Odyssey had been composed in the same way.

C Oral knowledge can be remarkably accurate. Some Aboriginal Australian stories describe a time when the coastline lay much farther out than it does today, and geologists have matched these accounts to the rise in sea level at the end of the last ice age, around ten thousand years ago. In West Africa, professional storytellers called griots have preserved the histories of families and kingdoms for many generations.

D Printing and mass education reduced the importance of the storyteller, but the appetite for spoken stories has never disappeared. Radio drama, audiobooks and podcasts are its modern forms, and research suggests that listening to a story engages the brain's language and emotional regions in much the same way as reading it.

Passage 76. The Game of Kings Culture & Arts 278 words

Новые слова
  • trace — прослеживать (историю)
  • infantry — пехота

  • conquest — завоевание
