  • prodigy — вундеркинд

A Chess is one of the oldest games still widely played. Most historians trace it to northern India around the sixth century, where a game called chaturanga represented the four divisions of an Indian army: infantry, cavalry, elephants and chariots. From India it travelled to Persia. The expression 'checkmate' comes from the Persian shah mat, usually translated as 'the king is helpless'. After the Arab conquest of Persia the game spread across the Islamic world and reached Europe by about the year 1000.

B The rules changed on the way. In the original game the piece beside the king was a weak adviser that could move only one square. In fifteenth-century Europe it became the queen, the most powerful piece on the board, and the bishop was given its long diagonal move. These changes made the game much faster and produced, in essence, the chess played today.

C Chess has long been used as a test of machine intelligence. In 1997 the IBM computer Deep Blue defeated the world champion Garry Kasparov in a six-game match, an event reported around the world. Deep Blue relied on calculating some 200 million positions per second. Twenty years later a program called AlphaZero took a different approach: given only the rules, it taught itself by playing millions of games against itself, and within hours it could beat the strongest existing programs.

D Far from killing the game, computers have helped it to flourish. Players use programs to train, and online platforms allow anyone to find an opponent at any hour. Interest surged in 2020, when lockdowns kept people at home and a popular television drama about a chess prodigy sent sales of chess sets soaring.

3 AlphaZero learned chess by studying games played by human champions. ✔ FALSE
4 Kasparov asked for a rematch against Deep Blue. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 77. Languages in Danger Culture & Arts 274 words

Новые слова
  • distribute — распределять
  • endangered — находящийся под угрозой исчезновения
  • shift — переходить, смещаться
  • indigenous — коренной
  • boarding school — школа-интернат
  • mother tongue — родной язык
  • identity — идентичность
  • revive — возрождать

A There are roughly 7,000 languages spoken in the world today, but they are very unequally distributed. About half of the world's population speaks one of just twenty-five languages, while several thousand languages have fewer than ten thousand speakers each. Linguists estimate that at least forty per cent of all languages are endangered, and that one disappears, on average, every few weeks when its last speaker dies.

B Languages rarely die because their speakers do. More commonly, a community shifts to a more powerful language that offers better access to education and work. Parents stop using the old language with their children, believing they are helping them to succeed, and within two or three generations it is gone. In the past the process was often forced. In Australia, Canada and the United States, indigenous children were sent to boarding schools where they were punished for speaking their mother tongue.

C Why should this loss concern anyone else? Each language is a unique record of how a group of people has understood the world. Many contain detailed knowledge of local plants, animals and weather that exists nowhere else. For the speakers themselves, language is closely tied to identity and to the stories and songs of their ancestors.

D Decline is not always final. Hebrew, which for almost two thousand years had been used mainly for prayer and study, was revived as an everyday spoken language at the end of the nineteenth century and now has millions of native speakers. Welsh and Maori have been strengthened by schools that teach entirely in those languages. Technology is helping too: linguists record elderly speakers, and communities are creating dictionaries and learning apps.

3 Hebrew was never used for any purpose for two thousand years. ✔ FALSE
4 Maori is now spoken by most New Zealanders. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 78. Bridges: Crossing the Gap Culture & Arts 270 words

Новые слова
  • beam — балка
  • sag — провисать
  • arch — арка
  • load — нагрузка
  • suspension — подвес, подвесной
  • gorge — ущелье
  • reassure — успокаивать
  • span — пролёт

A The earliest bridges were probably tree trunks laid across streams, and in principle the simple beam bridge has not changed: a horizontal structure resting on supports at each end. Its weakness is length. A long beam sags in the middle, so beam bridges need many supports.

B The Romans overcame this with the arch. In a stone arch each block presses against its neighbours, so the load is carried outwards and down into the ground. Roman arches were so well built that some, like the Pont du Gard in southern France, are still standing after two thousand years. Arches, however, require a great quantity of stone and solid foundations on both sides.

C The widest gaps are crossed by suspension bridges, in which the roadway hangs from cables stretched between tall towers. The idea is ancient: the Incas of South America built suspension bridges of woven grass across mountain gorges. Modern versions use steel. When the Brooklyn Bridge opened in New York in 1883, it was by far the longest in the world, and many people feared it would fall. To reassure them, a circus owner is said to have led twenty-one elephants across it the following year.

D Suspension bridges are light and flexible, and this can be a danger. In 1940 the Tacoma Narrows Bridge in the United States began to twist violently in a moderate wind and collapsed just four months after opening; the only victim was a dog. Since then, designs have been tested in wind tunnels. The current record holder, the 1915 Çanakkale Bridge in Turkey, opened in 2022, has a main span of just over two kilometres.

3 The Incas built suspension bridges from steel. ✔ FALSE
4 Several people were killed when the Tacoma Narrows Bridge collapsed. ✔ FALSE

Passage 79. Capturing Light Culture & Arts 277 words

Новые слова
  • faintly — слабо, едва заметно
  • exposure — выдержка, экспозиция
  • courtyard — внутренний двор
  • likeness — изображение, портрет
  • unique — единственный в своём роде
  • negative — негатив
  • slogan — лозунг, слоган
  • ironically — по иронии судьбы

A The oldest surviving photograph was taken in 1826 or 1827 by a French inventor, Nicéphore Niépce, from an upstairs window of his country house. It shows, rather faintly, the roofs of the surrounding buildings. The exposure took at least eight hours, so that sunlight appears on both sides of the courtyard.

B Niépce's partner Louis Daguerre developed a far more practical process, which the French government bought and presented 'free to the world' in 1839. A daguerreotype produced a wonderfully sharp image on a silvered metal plate. Exposure times soon fell to under a minute, and portrait studios opened in cities everywhere. For the first time ordinary families could own an accurate likeness of a relative. The method had limits, though: each picture was unique and could not be copied, and sitters had to keep perfectly still, which is one reason why people in early photographs rarely smile.

C In England, meanwhile, William Henry Fox Talbot had invented a process that produced a negative on paper, from which any number of positive prints could be made. This principle became the basis of photography for the next 150 years.

D Photography remained a business for professionals until 1888, when the American George Eastman launched the Kodak camera with the slogan 'You press the button, we do the rest'. It came loaded with a roll of film for a hundred pictures; the customer posted the whole camera back to the factory for developing. Photography became a popular hobby. The latest revolution began in 1975, when an engineer at Eastman's own company built the first digital camera. Ironically, the firm was slow to develop the idea, and in 2012 it was declared bankrupt.

Passage 80. How Football Got Its Rules Culture & Arts 280 words

Новые слова
  • leather — кожа (материал)
  • medieval — средневековый
  • injury — травма
  • confusion — путаница
  • representative — представитель
  • tavern — таверна
  • forbid — запрещать
  • host nation — страна-хозяйка

A Games in which a ball is kicked have been played for thousands of years. In China a game called cuju, in which players kicked a leather ball through an opening in a net, was popular more than two thousand years ago. In medieval England, 'mob football' was played between whole villages, with hundreds of players, few rules and frequent injuries. Several kings tried to ban it, without success.

B The modern game was shaped in English private schools in the early nineteenth century. Each school had its own version: at some the ball could be carried, at others only kicked. This caused confusion whenever former pupils from different schools wanted to play together. On 26 October 1863 representatives of a number of clubs met at a London tavern and founded the Football Association to agree on a single set of rules. The crucial decision was to forbid carrying the ball and kicking opponents' legs. Clubs that disagreed left and later developed the separate game of rugby.

C Football spread quickly among industrial workers, who by then had Saturday afternoons free, and many famous clubs began as teams from factories or churches. British sailors, engineers and traders carried the game abroad, especially to South America and continental Europe. The international governing body, FIFA, was founded in Paris in 1904, and the first World Cup was held in Uruguay in 1930, when the host nation won.

D The game's success owes much to its simplicity. It needs only a ball and some open ground, and its basic rules can be explained in a minute. Today it is estimated that several billion people follow the World Cup, making it the most watched sporting event on Earth.

3 Rugby developed from clubs that rejected the Football Association's rules. ✔ TRUE
4 The 1863 meeting lasted for several days. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 81. The Social Life of Elephants Animals & Geography 277 words

Новые слова
  • complexity — сложность
  • matriarch — матриарх, старшая самка
  • roar — рёв
  • calf (calves) — детёныш (слонёнок)
  • rumble — гул, рокот
  • companion — спутник, товарищ
  • trunk — хобот
  • poaching — браконьерство

A Elephants are the largest animals living on land, and among the most intelligent. An African elephant's brain weighs about five kilograms, more than that of any other land animal. But what impresses researchers most is not the size of the brain; it is the complexity of elephant society.

B The basic unit is the family, a group of related females and their young led by the oldest female, known as the matriarch. Males leave the family in their early teens and live alone or in loose groups with other males. The matriarch's experience is vital. She remembers the location of water holes that may be needed only once in many years, and studies in Kenya have shown that families led by older matriarchs are better at recognising the roar of a dangerous lion. During a severe drought in Tanzania in 1993, groups with older leaders lost far fewer calves, apparently because the leaders remembered a previous drought and led their families to distant food.

C Elephants communicate constantly. Besides trumpeting, they produce deep rumbles, many of them too low for humans to hear, which can travel several kilometres. They show what looks very much like emotion. They greet returning relatives with obvious excitement, help injured companions, and show a striking interest in the bones of dead elephants, touching them gently with their trunks.

D Their future is uncertain. In the early twentieth century Africa may have held several million elephants; today there are about 415,000. Poaching for ivory killed vast numbers, and although the international ivory trade was banned in 1989, illegal hunting continues. An increasing problem is conflict with farmers, as expanding villages and fields leave elephants less room.

Passage 82. Emperors of the Ice Animals & Geography 291 words

Новые слова
  • breed — размножаться, выводить потомство
  • harsh — суровый
  • colony — колония
  • fold — складка
  • huddle — плотная группа, куча
  • abundant — обильный
  • stable — устойчивый
  • feather — перо

A The emperor penguin is the largest of all penguins, standing more than a metre tall, and it breeds in the harshest conditions faced by any bird. While other Antarctic animals raise their young in the brief summer, emperors do so in the depths of winter, on the sea ice, in darkness, with temperatures falling below minus forty degrees and winds of over 150 kilometres an hour.

B The cycle begins in autumn, when the birds walk as much as a hundred kilometres inland to their colonies. The female lays a single egg and immediately passes it to the male, who balances it on his feet beneath a warm fold of skin. She then returns to the sea to feed. For about two months the male keeps the egg warm. He eats nothing during this time and may lose almost half his body weight.

C To survive, the males cooperate. Thousands crowd together in a tight huddle, which may be twenty degrees or more warmer in the centre than outside. The huddle is constantly in motion: birds on the cold outer edge gradually work their way inwards while those in the middle move out, so that each takes a turn in the warmth.

D The females come back around the time the chicks hatch and take over, and the hungry males finally leave to feed. The timing of the whole cycle means that the young are ready to swim in summer, when the ice edge is close and food is abundant.

E Because emperors depend on stable sea ice for most of the year, they are highly exposed to a warming climate. In 2022 several colonies in one region lost almost all their chicks when the ice broke up before the young had grown waterproof feathers.

3 Penguins at the centre of the huddle remain there all winter. ✔ FALSE
4 Emperor penguins live longer than other penguin species. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 83. The Changing Sahara Animals & Geography 290 words

Новые слова
  • dune — дюна
  • bare — голый
  • adapt — приспосабливаться
  • grassland — степь, луга
  • tilt — наклон
  • axis — ось
  • monsoon — муссон
  • vegetation — растительность

A The Sahara is the largest hot desert in the world, covering about nine million square kilometres, an area nearly as large as the United States. The popular image is of endless sand dunes, but sand seas make up only around a fifth of it. Most of the Sahara consists of bare rock, gravel plains and mountains, some rising above 3,000 metres.

B Daytime temperatures in summer regularly exceed forty-five degrees, yet nights can be cold, because dry air and clear skies let heat escape quickly. Rain is rare and unpredictable; some areas receive none for years. Life has nevertheless adapted. The fennec fox loses heat through its enormous ears, and the camel can go for a week or more without drinking.

C Remarkably, the Sahara was not always a desert. Rock paintings in the mountains of Algeria and Libya, thousands of years old, show giraffes, elephants, hippos and people swimming. Between about 11,000 and 5,000 years ago the region was a grassland with lakes and rivers. This 'Green Sahara' was the result of a slight change in the tilt of the Earth's axis, which moved the African monsoon rains northwards. When the rains shifted south again, the land dried out within a few centuries, and many of its people moved towards the Nile valley, which may have contributed to the rise of Egyptian civilisation.

D The Sahara affects places far beyond Africa. Each year winds lift hundreds of millions of tonnes of dust from it. Some of this crosses the Atlantic and falls on the Amazon rainforest, where the phosphorus it contains helps to fertilise the soil. On the desert's southern edge, African nations are planting a belt of trees and vegetation, the Great Green Wall, to stop farmland from turning to desert.

4 The Great Green Wall has already been completed. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 84. The Mighty Amazon Animals & Geography 290 words

Новые слова
  • discharge — сбрасывать (воду)
  • shore — берег
  • dispute — оспаривать
  • source — исток, источник
  • tributary — приток
  • exaggerate — преувеличивать
  • upstream — вверх по течению
  • mercury — ртуть

A By the amount of water it carries, the Amazon is easily the greatest river on Earth. It discharges about a fifth of all the fresh water that rivers pour into the oceans, more than the next seven largest rivers combined. So powerful is the flow that fresh water can be found on the surface of the Atlantic more than a hundred kilometres from the shore. Whether the Amazon or the Nile is the longer river is still disputed, since it depends on which stream is counted as the source; both are well over 6,000 kilometres.

B The river rises high in the Andes of Peru, less than 200 kilometres from the Pacific Ocean, and flows east across the whole width of South America. More than a thousand tributaries join it. There is not a single bridge across the main river along its entire length, mainly because it passes through thinly populated forest where there are few roads to connect.

C Each year, rains in the Andes cause the river to rise by as much as fifteen metres, flooding an area of forest larger than England for months at a time. Fish swim among the tree trunks and feed on fallen fruit, spreading the seeds. The river is home to more species of fish than any other, probably over 3,000, along with pink river dolphins, giant otters and the piranha, whose danger to humans is greatly exaggerated.

D The Amazon has been a highway for people for thousands of years, and ocean-going ships can travel upstream as far as the Peruvian city of Iquitos, some 3,600 kilometres from the sea. Today dams on its tributaries, gold mining that pollutes the water with mercury, and the clearing of the surrounding forest all threaten the system.

Passage 85. The Coldest Continent Animals & Geography 277 words

Новые слова
  • interior — внутренняя часть
  • expedition — экспедиция
  • permanent — постоянный
  • treaty — договор
  • prohibit — запрещать
  • bubble — пузырёк
  • ozone layer — озоновый слой
  • stability — устойчивость

A Antarctica is the coldest, windiest and driest continent. The lowest natural temperature ever measured at ground level, minus 89.2 degrees Celsius, was recorded at the Russian Vostok station in 1983. Although about ninety-eight per cent of the land is covered by ice, so little snow falls in the interior that it technically counts as a desert. The ice sheet is on average about two kilometres thick, and it holds roughly seventy per cent of the planet's fresh water.

B No human set eyes on the continent until 1820, and the first people reached the South Pole in December 1911, when a Norwegian team led by Roald Amundsen arrived five weeks ahead of the British expedition under Robert Scott. Scott and his four companions died on the return journey.

C Antarctica has no permanent inhabitants and belongs to no country. Under the Antarctic Treaty, signed in 1959 and now accepted by more than fifty states, the continent is reserved for peaceful purposes. Military activity is prohibited, and a later agreement forbids mining. Several thousand scientists and support staff live at research stations in summer, falling to about a thousand in the dark winter months.

D Their research is of global importance. Ice drilled from deep within the sheet contains tiny bubbles of ancient air, providing a record of the atmosphere going back 800,000 years. It was in Antarctica, in 1985, that British scientists discovered the hole in the ozone layer, a finding that led to an international ban on the chemicals responsible. Today attention is fixed on the stability of the ice itself: if the West Antarctic ice sheet were to melt entirely, global sea level would rise by several metres.

Passage 86. Clever Crows Animals & Geography 284 words

Новые слова
  • tool — орудие, инструмент
  • strip — обдирать; полоска
  • twig — веточка
  • hook — крючок
  • captive — содержащийся в неволе
  • fable — басня
  • pebble — камешек, галька
  • scold — ругать, браниться

A For a long time the use of tools was thought to be a uniquely human ability, and later one shared only with apes. Then scientists turned their attention to the crow family. On the Pacific island of New Caledonia lives a crow that not only uses tools but manufactures them. It strips twigs and shapes them into hooks, and tears strips from the edges of tough leaves, in order to pull insects out of holes in wood. Different populations on the island produce tools of different designs, which suggests that the techniques are learned from other birds.

B The most famous individual was a captive New Caledonian crow named Betty, studied at Oxford University. In 2002 she was given a straight piece of wire and a small bucket of food at the bottom of a tube. Without any training, Betty bent the wire into a hook and lifted the bucket out.

C Other experiments recall a fable by the ancient Greek writer Aesop, in which a thirsty crow drops pebbles into a jug to raise the water. Real crows presented with a tube of water and a floating piece of food do exactly this, and they choose heavy objects that sink rather than light ones that float. Crows in a Japanese city have been filmed placing nuts on the road for cars to crack and waiting for the traffic lights to change before collecting them.

D Crows also have excellent memories for people. Researchers in Seattle who wore a particular mask while trapping crows found that the birds scolded anyone wearing that mask for years afterwards. Even crows that had not been born at the time joined in, having apparently learned from their elders whom to distrust.

Passage 87. Songs of the Humpback Animals & Geography 285 words

Новые слова
  • eerie — жуткий, таинственный
  • random — случайный
  • mating — спаривание
  • compete — соревноваться
  • keep track of — следить, отслеживать
  • evolve — развиваться, меняться
  • commercial — коммерческий
  • drown out — заглушать

A In 1967 the biologist Roger Payne and his colleague Scott McVay made a remarkable discovery while analysing underwater recordings made near Bermuda. The long, eerie sounds produced by humpback whales were not random. They were arranged in repeated patterns, in the same way as the song of a bird, although on a far larger scale. A single song may last up to half an hour, and a whale may repeat it for many hours.

B Only the males sing, mostly in the warm waters where humpbacks gather to breed, so the songs are thought to be connected with mating, though exactly how is unclear. They may attract females, or they may be a means by which males compete with or keep track of one another.

C The most extraordinary feature is the way the songs change. All the males in one population sing the same song, but it gradually evolves through the season as new phrases are added and old ones dropped. Occasionally something more dramatic happens. Researchers studying whales off eastern Australia found that within two years the entire population had abandoned its song and adopted a completely different one, which had been introduced by a small number of visitors from the Indian Ocean. Such songs then spread eastwards across the South Pacific from one population to the next. Scientists have described this as one of the clearest examples of culture in animals.

D Payne's recordings were released as an album in 1970 and became an unexpected bestseller. The public response helped to bring about the international ban on commercial whaling in 1986, and humpback numbers have since recovered strongly. The whales now face a different threat: the noise of ships, which can drown out their songs.

Passage 88. The Ship of the Desert Animals & Geography 283 words

Новые слова
  • domesticated — одомашненный
  • hump — горб
  • endure — выносить, выдерживать
  • cope — справляться
  • sweat — потеть
  • kidney — почка
  • nostril — ноздря
  • antibody — антитело

A Few animals are as well adapted to their environment as the camel. There are two domesticated species: the one-humped dromedary of Arabia and North Africa, and the two-humped Bactrian camel of Central Asia, which can endure winters of minus thirty degrees as well as summer heat.

B Contrary to popular belief, a camel's hump does not hold water. It is a store of fat, weighing up to thirty-six kilograms, which the animal draws on when food is scarce. Keeping its fat in one place instead of spreading it under the skin helps the camel to stay cool.

C Its ability to cope without water depends on several other features. A camel can lose up to a quarter of its body weight in water without harm, a loss that would kill most mammals. Its body temperature can vary by about six degrees during the day, so it does not begin to sweat until the air is very hot. Its kidneys produce highly concentrated urine, and its nostrils recover moisture from the air it breathes out. When water is finally available, a thirsty camel can drink more than a hundred litres in around ten minutes. Long eyelashes and nostrils that can close protect it from blowing sand, and broad, padded feet stop it from sinking into soft ground.

D Camels were domesticated more than three thousand years ago, and they transformed life in dry regions. A loaded camel can walk around forty kilometres a day, and without camels the caravan trade across the Sahara and along the Silk Road would have been impossible. Today they are still valued for milk, meat and wool, and scientists are studying the unusually small antibodies in camel blood for use in medicine.

Passage 89. Iceland: Energy from the Earth Animals & Geography 277 words

Новые слова
  • boundary — граница
  • geyser — гейзер
  • spout — бить струёй
  • peat — торф
  • pipe — подавать по трубам; труба
  • renewable — возобновляемый
  • greenhouse — теплица
  • smelting — выплавка (металла)

A Iceland sits on the Mid-Atlantic Ridge, the boundary where the North American and Eurasian plates are slowly moving apart. As a result the island has about thirty active volcanic systems and hundreds of hot springs. The English word 'geyser' comes from Geysir, the name of a famous spouting spring in the south of the country.

B For centuries Icelanders used the hot water mainly for washing and bathing. Houses were heated with peat and, later, with imported coal, and photographs of the capital, Reykjavik, from the early twentieth century show a city under a cloud of smoke. The change began in the 1930s, when hot water was first piped to a school and some nearby houses. The oil crisis of the 1970s, which sharply raised the cost of imported fuel, persuaded the government to extend the system across the country.

C Today about nine out of ten Icelandic homes are heated directly with geothermal water. Almost all of the nation's electricity is renewable as well, roughly seventy per cent coming from hydroelectric dams and thirty per cent from geothermal power stations. The warm water has other uses. Pavements and car parks in Reykjavik are heated from below to keep them free of ice, greenhouses grow tomatoes and even bananas close to the Arctic Circle, and outdoor swimming pools are open all year.

D Cheap, clean electricity has attracted energy-hungry industries, particularly aluminium smelting and data centres. Not all Icelanders welcome this, since new power stations and dams change wild landscapes that are valued for their own sake and for tourism. Iceland's experience cannot simply be copied elsewhere, but its engineers now advise geothermal projects in East Africa, Asia and beyond.

Passage 90. The Sea that Disappeared Animals & Geography 299 words

Новые слова
  • fleet — флот
  • irrigate — орошать
  • canal — канал
  • evaporate — испаряться
  • rust — ржаветь
  • residue — остаток
  • toxic — токсичный
  • shrub — кустарник

A In 1960 the Aral Sea, lying between Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan, was the fourth largest lake in the world, with an area of about 68,000 square kilometres. Fishing fleets working from ports such as Moynaq and Aralsk landed tens of thousands of tonnes of fish a year. Sixty years later most of it had become desert.

B The sea was fed by two great rivers, the Amu Darya and the Syr Darya. In the 1960s Soviet planners decided to use their water to irrigate vast new fields of cotton and rice in the surrounding dry lands. Thousands of kilometres of canals were dug, many of them unlined, so that a large part of the water soaked into the sand or evaporated before it reached the crops. With far less water arriving, the sea began to shrink. By the late 1980s it had split in two, and by the 2010s it had lost about ninety per cent of its volume.

C The results were disastrous. As the water grew saltier, the native fish died and the fishing industry collapsed; rusting ships now lie on dry sand far from any shore. The exposed seabed, covered in salt and residues of farm chemicals, is swept by winds that carry toxic dust over a wide region, and rates of breathing and other illnesses among local people have risen. Without the moderating influence of the water, summers have become hotter and winters colder.

D There has been a partial recovery in the north. A dam completed by Kazakhstan in 2005, with support from the World Bank, raised the level of the small northern sea, and fish and fishermen have returned. The larger southern part is unlikely to be restored. In Uzbekistan, efforts now focus on planting hardy saxaul shrubs on the dry seabed to hold down the dust.

Passage 91. Next Stop, Mars Space & Ideas 285 words

Новые слова
  • fascinate — очаровывать, увлекать
  • optical illusion — оптическая иллюзия
  • diameter — диаметр
  • mineral — минерал
  • mission — миссия, экспедиция
  • radiation — радиация
  • weightlessness — невесомость
  • spacecraft — космический корабль

A Mars has fascinated humanity for centuries. In the 1890s the American astronomer Percival Lowell announced that he could see canals on its surface, built, he believed, by an intelligent civilisation struggling to survive on a drying planet. The canals turned out to be an optical illusion, but the idea of Martians has never quite left the popular imagination.

B The real Mars is a harsh place. It is about half the diameter of the Earth, and its thin atmosphere, composed almost entirely of carbon dioxide, has less than one per cent of the pressure of ours. The average temperature is around minus sixty degrees. Yet it was not always so. Photographs from orbit show dry river valleys and deltas, and robot vehicles on the surface have found minerals that form only in water. Billions of years ago Mars had lakes and perhaps an ocean. Whether life ever arose there is the central question that current missions are trying to answer.

C Sending people is far more difficult than sending machines. Because of the way the planets move, a journey is practical only about once every twenty-six months, and it takes six to nine months each way. Astronauts would be exposed to dangerous radiation, and their muscles and bones would weaken in weightlessness. A radio signal takes up to twenty minutes to reach Earth, so the crew could not rely on immediate help. Landing is particularly hard: the atmosphere is too thin for parachutes alone to slow a heavy spacecraft, yet thick enough to cause intense heating.

D Space agencies and private companies have nevertheless announced plans to send crews in the coming decades. Critics ask whether the enormous expense is justified when robots are becoming ever more capable.

Passage 92. Eyes on the Universe Space & Ideas 282 words

Новые слова
  • naked eye — невооружённый глаз
  • defect — дефект
  • fringe — кайма, ореол
  • mirror — зеркало
  • reflector — рефлектор (зеркальный телескоп)
  • blur — размывать
  • successor — преемник
  • infrared — инфракрасный

A Until the early seventeenth century, everything humans knew about the sky had been learned with the naked eye. In 1609 the Italian scientist Galileo Galilei heard of a Dutch invention that used two lenses to make distant objects appear closer. He built his own improved version and pointed it at the night sky. Within months he had seen mountains on the Moon, four moons circling Jupiter and countless stars invisible to the eye. His observations supported the idea, dangerous at the time, that the Earth is not the centre of the universe.

B Telescopes with lenses suffered from a defect: they surrounded bright objects with fringes of false colour. In 1668 Isaac Newton solved the problem by using a curved mirror instead of a lens to gather the light. Nearly all large telescopes since then have been reflectors, because mirrors can be made much bigger than lenses. The bigger the mirror, the more light it collects and the fainter the objects it can detect.

C Even the best telescope on the ground must look through the Earth's atmosphere, which blurs the image and blocks some kinds of light entirely. The answer was to go above it. The Hubble Space Telescope, launched in 1990, at first returned blurred pictures because its main mirror had been ground to very slightly the wrong shape. Astronauts fitted corrective optics in 1993, and Hubble went on to transform astronomy.

D Its successor, the James Webb Space Telescope, was launched in December 2021. With a gold-coated mirror 6.5 metres across, it observes infrared light and orbits the Sun 1.5 million kilometres from Earth. It can see galaxies as they were more than thirteen billion years ago, soon after the universe began.

4 The Hubble telescope has now stopped working. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 93. Food of the Gods Space & Ideas 278 words

Новые слова
  • humid — влажный
  • bitter — горький
  • foam — пениться
  • roast — обжаривать
  • warrior — воин
  • conqueror — завоеватель
  • condensed milk — сгущённое молоко
  • certification — сертификация

A Chocolate is made from the seeds of the cacao tree, which grows only in hot, humid regions close to the equator. Its scientific name, Theobroma, means 'food of the gods'. The tree was first cultivated in Central America more than three thousand years ago, and for most of its history chocolate was not eaten but drunk.

B The Maya and later the Aztecs prepared a bitter, foaming drink from roasted cacao seeds, water, chilli and maize. It was reserved largely for rulers, warriors and priests. Cacao beans were so valuable that the Aztecs used them as money: according to one sixteenth-century record, a turkey cost a hundred beans and a tomato one.

C Spanish conquerors took cacao back to Europe in the 1500s. There the chilli was replaced with sugar and the drink was served hot, and for the next two centuries chocolate was a fashionable luxury of the wealthy. The transformation into a solid food came with the Industrial Revolution. In 1828 a Dutch chemist, Coenraad van Houten, invented a press that separated cocoa butter from the bean, leaving a fine powder. In 1847 the English firm Fry's mixed cocoa powder, sugar and melted cocoa butter to produce the first chocolate bar, and in 1875 the Swiss Daniel Peter added condensed milk to create milk chocolate.

D Today about two thirds of the world's cocoa comes from West Africa, chiefly Ivory Coast and Ghana, where it is grown mostly on small family farms. Many farmers earn very little, and the use of child labour on some farms has been widely criticised. Certification schemes that promise farmers a fairer price have grown, though they still cover only part of the market.

Passage 94. The Grain that Feeds Half the World Space & Ideas 286 words

Новые слова
  • tolerate — переносить, выдерживать
  • weed — сорняк
  • seedling — саженец, рассада
  • transplant — пересаживать
  • terrace — терраса
  • famine — голод
  • yield — урожайность
  • methane — метан

A Rice is the staple food of more than half of humanity. It was domesticated in China, in the valley of the Yangtze River, around nine thousand years ago, and today roughly ninety per cent of the world's crop is still grown and eaten in Asia.

B Most rice is grown in flooded fields called paddies. The plant does not actually need to stand in water, but it tolerates flooding, whereas most weeds do not, so the water acts as a natural weedkiller. Traditionally the seedlings are raised in a nursery and then transplanted by hand into the mud, a task demanding great labour. In mountainous regions farmers have carved steep hillsides into terraces to hold the water; those in the northern Philippines are some two thousand years old.

C In the 1960s many experts predicted famine in Asia, as populations were growing faster than harvests. The disaster was avoided largely thanks to plant breeders at the International Rice Research Institute in the Philippines. In 1966 they released a variety known as IR8. Traditional rice plants were tall, and when given fertiliser they grew top-heavy and fell over. IR8 had a short, stiff stem and could convert fertiliser into grain, so that yields doubled or even tripled. It became known as 'miracle rice' and was a central part of what was called the Green Revolution.

D New challenges have since appeared. Flooded paddies release methane, a powerful greenhouse gas, and rice farming consumes a large share of Asia's fresh water. Researchers are testing methods in which fields are allowed to dry out periodically, which saves water and cuts emissions. Others have developed varieties that survive two weeks under water, a valuable quality in areas where floods are becoming more frequent.

Passage 95. The Machine that Changed Mobility Space & Ideas 279 words

Новые слова
  • ancestor — предшественник, предок
  • frame — рама
  • pedal — педаль
  • tyre — шина
  • enlarge — увеличивать
  • chain — цепь
  • affordable — доступный по цене
  • independence — независимость

A The bicycle seems such a simple machine that it is surprising how late it appeared. Its ancestor was the 'running machine' built in 1817 by the German baron Karl von Drais: two wheels joined by a wooden frame, which the rider pushed along with his feet on the ground. Some historians link the invention to the crop failures of 1816, which had led to the deaths of many horses.

B Pedals were attached to the front wheel in France in the 1860s. The result, with its iron tyres, was so uncomfortable that it was nicknamed the 'boneshaker'. Since each turn of the pedals turned the wheel once, the only way to go faster was to enlarge the front wheel, and so the high-wheeler, or penny-farthing, appeared in the 1870s. It was fast but dangerous: a small stone could throw the rider head first to the ground.

C The breakthrough was the 'safety bicycle' of 1885, designed by the Englishman John Kemp Starley. It had two wheels of equal size and a chain driving the rear wheel, essentially the design used today. Three years later John Dunlop, a Scottish vet living in Belfast, developed the air-filled tyre to make his son's tricycle more comfortable.

D The effect on society was enormous. For the first time ordinary people had an affordable means of travelling beyond walking distance, to find work or simply for pleasure. The bicycle was especially significant for women, giving them new independence and encouraging more practical clothing; the American campaigner Susan B. Anthony said it had done more to free women than anything else in the world. Cyclists' demands for smoother roads also prepared the way, ironically, for the motor car.

Passage 96. Transparent and Everywhere Space & Ideas 282 words

Новые слова
  • transparent — прозрачный
  • crystal — кристалл
  • blade — лезвие
  • bead — бусина
  • vessel — сосуд
  • craftsman — ремесленник, мастер
  • spectacles — очки
  • strand — нить, волокно

A Glass is made mainly from one of the commonest substances on Earth: sand. When sand is heated to a very high temperature with soda and lime, it melts, and on cooling it hardens without forming crystals, which is why it is transparent. Nature produces glass too. Obsidian, formed when volcanic lava cools quickly, was shaped into razor-sharp blades by Stone Age people.

B Humans began making glass more than four thousand years ago in Mesopotamia and Egypt, at first as beads and small bottles formed around a core of clay. The crucial advance came in the first century BC, probably on the coast of what is now Syria and Lebanon: glass-blowing. By blowing air through a hollow tube into a ball of molten glass, a craftsman could make a vessel in minutes. Glass became cheap enough for ordinary Roman households.

C In the Middle Ages the finest glass came from Venice. In 1291 the city ordered all its glassmakers to move to the island of Murano, officially to reduce the risk of fire, but also to keep their techniques secret. Craftsmen were forbidden to leave the republic.

D It is hard to overstate how much modern life owes to this material. Spectacles, invented in Italy around 1290, extended the working lives of scholars and craftsmen. Lenses made possible the telescope and the microscope, and hence modern astronomy and biology; the glass test tube and thermometer were essential to chemistry. Large flat windows became possible in 1959, when the British firm Pilkington perfected a method of floating molten glass on a bath of liquid tin. Today, strands of ultra-pure glass thinner than a hair carry almost all of the world's internet traffic as pulses of light.

Passage 97. The Number that Means Nothing Space & Ideas 286 words

Новые слова
  • slanted — наклонный
  • notion — понятие, идея
  • numeral — цифра, числительное
  • subtract — вычитать
  • digit — цифра
  • influential — влиятельный
  • cipher — шифр; ноль
  • alter — изменять

A It is difficult to imagine arithmetic without zero, yet for most of history people managed without it. The Babylonians, around 300 BC, used a pair of slanted marks to show an empty place within a number, rather as we distinguish 205 from 25, but they never treated it as a number in itself. The Maya of Central America independently invented a similar symbol, shaped like a shell, for their calendar.

B The ancient Greeks, for all their brilliance in geometry, were uncomfortable with the notion. How, their philosophers asked, can nothing be something? The Romans had no zero either, and anyone who has tried to multiply using Roman numerals will understand the difficulty this caused.

C Zero as a true number, which can be added, subtracted and multiplied like any other, appeared in India. In AD 628 the mathematician Brahmagupta set out rules for calculating with it, stating for example that a number minus itself is zero. From India the system of nine digits and zero passed to the Islamic world. In ninth-century Baghdad the scholar al-Khwarizmi, who was born in the region of Khorezm in present-day Uzbekistan, wrote an influential book explaining it; the word 'algorithm' comes from the Latin form of his name. The Arabic word for zero, sifr, meaning 'empty', gave English both 'zero' and 'cipher'.

D Europe was slow to accept the new numbers. The Italian merchant's son Leonardo of Pisa, known as Fibonacci, promoted them in a book of 1202, having learned them in North Africa. Some authorities remained suspicious; Florence banned them from official accounts in 1299, fearing that a zero could easily be altered into a six or a nine. Only with the spread of printing did they finally replace Roman numerals.

2 The Maya developed a zero symbol without outside influence. ✔ TRUE

4 Brahmagupta travelled to Baghdad to teach his rules. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Passage 98. The Psychology of Colour Space & Ideas 285 words

Новые слова
  • confident — уверенный
  • combat — бой, единоборство
  • contest — состязание, поединок
  • dominance — доминирование, превосходство
  • shade — оттенок
  • aggressive — агрессивный
  • fortune — удача
  • boundary — граница

A Does the colour of a room change how we feel? Does a red shirt help an athlete to win? Popular books give confident answers, but the scientific evidence is more mixed.

B Some effects appear to be real. In a study of the 2004 Olympic Games, researchers examined combat sports such as boxing and wrestling, in which competitors were randomly given red or blue clothing. Those in red won about fifty-five per cent of the contests, an advantage that was greatest when opponents were evenly matched. The researchers suggested that red is a natural signal of dominance, as it is in many animals. In other experiments, students who briefly saw the colour red before a test performed slightly worse on problems requiring flexible thinking, perhaps because red is associated with danger and with teachers' corrections.

C Many common claims, however, rest on little evidence. In the late 1970s a particular shade of pink was reported to calm aggressive prisoners, and some jails painted cells with it. Later, better-controlled studies failed to confirm any lasting effect.

D Culture complicates matters further. White is the colour of weddings in Western countries, whereas in China and India it is traditionally worn at funerals. Red suggests danger to many Europeans but good fortune in China. Even the boundaries between colours vary: Russian has two separate basic words for light blue and dark blue, and experiments show that Russian speakers are slightly quicker than English speakers at telling apart shades that fall on either side of that line.

E Asked for their favourite colour, people in most countries surveyed choose blue. Researchers think the reason is simple: we tend to like colours associated with things we like, such as clear skies and clean water.

2 Wearing red may give a small advantage in combat sports. ✔ YES
3 Painting prison cells pink is an effective way to reduce violence. ✔ NO
4 Companies should choose blue for their logos. ✔ NOT GIVEN

 ✔ B

Passage 99. Why Do We Dream? Space & Ideas 287 words

Новые слова
  • glimpse — беглый взгляд, проблеск
  • interpretation — толкование
  • disguised — замаскированный
  • lid — веко
  • paralysed — парализованный
  • deprive — лишать
  • rehearsal — репетиция
  • lucid — осознанный, ясный

A People have always wondered about the meaning of dreams. In the ancient world they were commonly taken to be messages from the gods or glimpses of the future. In 1900 the Austrian doctor Sigmund Freud proposed a different interpretation: dreams, he argued, are disguised expressions of wishes that we hide even from ourselves.

B Scientific study of dreaming became possible in 1953, when researchers at the University of Chicago noticed that sleepers' eyes move rapidly beneath their lids at certain periods of the night. Volunteers woken during this rapid eye movement, or REM, sleep nearly always reported a vivid dream. During REM the brain is almost as active as when awake, yet the muscles of the body are effectively paralysed, which prevents us from acting out our dreams. We now know that some dreaming occurs in other stages of sleep as well.

C There is still no agreed explanation of what dreams are for. One influential theory, put forward in 1977, holds that they have no purpose at all: the sleeping brain produces random signals, and the thinking part of the brain does its best to turn them into a story. Others believe that dreaming helps to process memories and emotions. People deprived of REM sleep are worse at learning new skills, and experiments show that the brain replays recent experiences during sleep. A further idea is that dreams are rehearsals. A large proportion involve being chased or threatened, and practising such situations in safety might have helped our ancestors to survive.

D Most dreams are forgotten within minutes of waking. A few people, however, experience lucid dreams, in which they are aware of dreaming and can sometimes direct what happens, an ability that researchers have confirmed in the laboratory.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 Freud thought dreams were messages from the gods. ✔ FALSE
2 REM sleep was identified in the middle of the twentieth century. ✔ TRUE
3 During REM sleep, the body's muscles are very active. ✔ FALSE
4 Women remember their dreams more often than men. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Complete the summary. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

5 According to a theory from 1977, dreams result from signals in the brain. ✔ random
6 Another view is that dreaming helps process memories and . ✔ emotions
7 In a dream, the sleeper knows that he or she is dreaming. ✔ lucid

Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

8 Why might dreams of being chased have been useful?
  • A They kept people awake.
  • B They allowed safe practice for dangerous situations.
  • C They improved eyesight.
  • D They helped people forget fear.
 ✔ B

Passage 100. The Olympic Games, Ancient and Modern Space & Ideas 277 words

Новые слова
  • sprint — спринт, забег на короткую дистанцию
  • wreath — венок
  • sacred — священный
  • truce — перемирие
  • millennium — тысячелетие
  • pagan — языческий
  • amateur — любительский
  • boycott — бойкот

A The Olympic Games were first held, according to tradition, in 776 BC at Olympia in southern Greece, as part of a religious festival in honour of the god Zeus. At the earliest Games there was a single event, a sprint of about 190 metres. Wrestling, boxing, chariot racing and other contests were added later. Only free Greek men could compete, and married women were not even permitted to watch. Winners received no medals, only a wreath of olive leaves, but their home cities rewarded them generously. A sacred truce allowed athletes and spectators to travel safely through warring states.

B The Games were held every four years for more than a millennium, until they were abolished at the end of the fourth century AD by a Christian Roman emperor who regarded them as pagan.

C Their revival was the work of a French aristocrat, Pierre de Coubertin, who believed that sport built character and that international competition could promote peace. The first modern Games took place in Athens in 1896, with about 240 athletes, all of them men, from fourteen countries. Women first took part in Paris in 1900. The marathon, incidentally, was a modern invention, inspired by the legend of a messenger who ran to Athens with news of a victory.

D The Games have grown enormously since then: more than 10,000 athletes from over 200 teams now take part in the summer event. Growth has brought problems. Coubertin's ideal of amateur competition has given way to professionalism, the Games have repeatedly been disrupted by political boycotts, and scandals over banned drugs are frequent. The cost of hosting has also soared, and fewer cities are now willing to bid.

Do the following statements agree with the information in the passage? Choose TRUE, FALSE or NOT GIVEN.

1 The earliest Olympic Games consisted of one running race. ✔ TRUE
2 Winners at the ancient Games were given gold medals. ✔ FALSE
3 Women competed at the first modern Games in 1896. ✔ FALSE
4 Coubertin himself competed in the 1896 Games. ✔ NOT GIVEN

Which paragraph contains the following information? Choose the correct letter.

5 difficulties facing the modern Games ✔ D
6 the end of the ancient Games ✔ B
7 the ideas of the man who restarted the Games ✔ C

Complete the sentences. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER from the passage for each answer.

8 Ancient winners were given a wreath made of leaves. ✔ olive

Guide: Good afternoon, everyone, and welcome to the City Museum. Before you start exploring, I'd like to tell you a little about the building and what you can see today.

Guide: The museum opened in eighteen ninety-two. The building was originally a wool warehouse, which explains the large windows and high ceilings. It was converted into a museum after a local businessman left his private collection to the city.

Guide: We're now standing in the entrance hall on the ground floor. To your left is the Local History gallery, which tells the story of the town from Roman times to the present. The most popular exhibit there is a model of the old harbour as it looked three hundred years ago.

Guide: On the first floor you'll find the Natural World gallery. Children love the dinosaur skeleton, which is over nine metres long. Next to it is our temporary exhibition. This month it's about the history of photography, and it finishes on the thirtieth of June.

Guide: The second floor has the art collection, mainly paintings of the sea and the coast by local artists. Please note that photography is allowed everywhere in the museum except in the art gallery.

Guide: If you're hungry, the café is in the basement and it serves hot food until half past two. The gift shop is beside the main exit.

Guide: We have two free talks today. At two o'clock there's a talk about Roman coins in the Local History gallery, and at three thirty a member of staff will show how old clocks are repaired. For the second one you need a ticket from the information desk, because there are only twenty places.

Guide: Finally, the museum closes at five thirty, and the last entry to the galleries is at five. Lockers for bags are next to the stairs. You'll need a one pound coin, which you get back afterwards. Enjoy your visit.

  • A the recycling centre.
  • B supermarkets.

 ✔ B

  • A ten pounds.
  • B twenty pounds.
  • C thirty pounds.
 ✔ B

  • A the town hall.
  • B the recycling centre.

Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Presenter: From next month the way our rubbish is collected is going to change. Here to explain is David Reid from the city council.

Officer: Thank you. Yes, from the first of March every household will have three bins instead of two. The reason is simple. At the moment only thirty-eight per cent of our waste is recycled, and we want to reach sixty per cent within five years.

Officer: Let me go through the bins. The blue bin is for paper and cardboard. Please flatten boxes so they take up less room. The green bin is for glass, tins and plastic bottles. You don't need to remove labels, but please rinse everything first.

Officer: The third bin is new. It's a small brown bin for food waste, such as vegetable peelings, tea bags and leftovers. We'll deliver one to every home, along with a roll of special bags. The food waste will be turned into gas, which is used to produce electricity.

Officer: Everything else goes in your old black bin. But please note, the black bin will now be emptied every two weeks instead of every week. The brown food bin will be collected weekly.

Officer: Collections start at half past six in the morning, so the safest thing is to put your bins out the night before.

Officer: A few things must never go in any bin. Batteries are the most dangerous, because they can cause fires in the lorries. Take them to the collection boxes in supermarkets. Old clothes should go to charity shops, and garden waste to the recycling centre on Dock Road, which is open seven days a week.

Officer: If you have a large item, such as a sofa or a fridge, we can collect it from your home for twenty pounds. Book through the council website.

Officer: A leaflet explaining all of this will arrive through your door next week. And we're holding an information evening at the town hall on the twentieth of February at seven o'clock.

Presenter: David, thank you very much.

Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Guide: Good morning and welcome to Ashdown Botanical Garden. Before you set off, let me give you an idea of what there is to see.

That's the Palm House. It's the oldest building in the garden, and inside the temperature is kept at twenty-seven degrees, so you may want to take off your coat.

Guide: To the east of the Palm House is the Rock Garden, with plants from mountain regions. The path there is steep and has steps, so it's not suitable for wheelchairs or pushchairs.

Guide: On the west side is the lake. Please don't feed the ducks. Bread is bad for them. Next to the lake is our newest attraction, the Treetop Walk, which opened last year. It's a wooden bridge eighteen metres above the ground, and it gives you a bird's view of the forest area. There's a small extra charge of three pounds, and children must be with an adult.

Guide: For families, the children's garden is behind the café. Children can dig, plant seeds and look for insects. There's a workshop there at two o'clock today on making a bee hotel.

Choose the correct letter, A, B or C.

9 According to the presenter, a rail pass is best for people who
  • A make a few short trips.
  • B make many long journeys in a short time.
  • C book their tickets early.
 ✔ B
10 Next week's episode is about
  • A night trains.
  • B the Swiss Alps.

 ✔ C
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Host: Hello and welcome back to The Slow Traveller, the podcast for people who think the journey matters as much as the destination. I'm Kate, and this week I'm answering your questions about seeing Europe by train.

Host: The question I get most often is about rail passes. Are they worth it? The honest answer is, it depends. A pass gives you unlimited travel in thirty-three countries, and for people under twenty-eight it's about a quarter cheaper. If you're planning lots of long journeys in a short time, it saves money. But if you only want three or four trips, ordinary tickets booked early are usually cheaper.

Host: And booking early really matters. Tickets for most high-speed trains go on sale about three months ahead, and the lowest prices disappear quickly. One thing people forget is that even with a pass, you often need a seat reservation on fast trains. In France and Spain that's compulsory.

Host: My second tip is to try a night train. They're coming back all over Europe. You fall asleep in Vienna and wake up in Rome, and you save the cost of a hotel. I'd pay a little extra for a bed in a sleeping compartment rather than an ordinary seat. Trust me, your back will thank you.

Host: Third, pack light. You'll be lifting your bag onto racks and carrying it up station stairs. I travel with one backpack of about nine kilos, and I've never wished I had more.

Host: What about food? Station restaurants are expensive. Do what locals do and buy bread, cheese and fruit at a market before you board. A picnic by the window is one of the great pleasures of train travel.

Host: Finally, leave time for connections. I allow at least forty minutes when changing trains, because delays happen, especially in summer.

Host: My favourite route? The line through the Swiss Alps from Chur to Tirano. It crosses nearly two hundred bridges, and every one has a view.

Host: Next week, travelling with children. Until then, enjoy the ride.

Ranger: A few rules. Fires are forbidden everywhere, because the forest is very dry this summer. Please take all your litter home. Dogs must be kept on a lead between March and July, when birds are nesting on the ground.

Ranger: Camping is allowed only at the official site by the lake, which costs twelve pounds a night. You need to book at this desk.

Ranger: This centre has an exhibition about the park's wildlife and a café. We close at five thirty. And at eight this evening there's a guided walk to look for bats. It's free, but bring a torch.

Guide: The castle was begun in the year eleven twenty by a knight called Sir Hugh de Vere. It was built on a hill above the river, so that soldiers could see enemies approaching from any direction. The oldest part is the Great Tower, directly in front of you, whose walls are four metres thick.

Guide: The castle was attacked many times but captured only once, in thirteen thirty-two, after its defenders ran out of food. It remained in the same family for five centuries, until it was badly damaged by fire in seventeen eighty. After that it stood empty and local people took its stones to build their houses. Restoration began only in the nineteen sixties.

Guide: Your visit follows the numbers on the walls. Number one is the gatehouse, where you are now. From here, go through the courtyard to number two, the Great Hall, where the family ate and entertained guests. Look up at the wooden roof, which is original.

Guide: Number three is the kitchen, with a fireplace large enough to roast a whole cow. Number four is the chapel, which has the castle's only coloured glass window.

Guide: Number five is the Great Tower. There are one hundred and twelve steps to the top. The staircase is narrow and turns to the right, which made it easier for defenders coming down to use their swords. The view from the top is magnificent, but the climb is not recommended for anyone with a heart condition.

Guide: Beneath the tower is the prison, number six. Children usually like this best.

Guide: The castle gardens were replanted using a drawing from sixteen ten. There, at two o'clock each day, you can watch a display of birds of prey.

Guide: To hear about each place, press its number, then the green button. Now walk forward into the courtyard.

Tutor: Good. A narrow topic is easier to handle in fifteen minutes. Which cities are you comparing?

Tom: Copenhagen and London. Copenhagen is the obvious success story. Nearly half of all trips to work or school there are made by bike.

Tutor: And why is that, in your view?

Maya: Mainly the infrastructure. The cycle lanes are separated from the cars by a kerb, so people feel safe. In London many lanes are just painted lines.

Tom: I'd add cost. Owning a car in Denmark is very expensive because of the tax on new vehicles.

Tutor: That's a fair point. What sources are you using?

Maya: We have official statistics from both city councils, and Tom found a good journal article.

Tom: Yes, but it was published in two thousand and twelve, so the figures are quite old.

Tutor: Then use it for the background, not for current data. Have you thought about doing any research of your own?

Maya: We'd like to do a short survey of students here, asking why they do or don't cycle to campus.

Tutor: Excellent. Keep it short, though. Five or six questions at most, and aim for about fifty responses.

Tom: How should we divide the talk?

Tutor: I'd suggest Maya presents the comparison of the two cities, and Tom, you present the survey and the recommendations. And please don't read from your notes. Last year that was the most common weakness.

Maya: Should we use slides?

Tutor: Yes, but no more than ten, with very little text. Use graphs and photographs instead. One more thing. Leave three minutes at the end for questions from the audience.

Tom: When do you need our outline?

Tutor: By next Friday, by email. The presentation itself is on the twenty-first of November.

Laura: Right. What should I do first?

Laura: When would you like the proposal?

Supervisor: By the end of October. Then we'll meet every two weeks. The final dissertation is ten thousand words and is due in May.

Laura: That sounds like a lot.

Supervisor: It is, but if you write a little every week it's manageable. Most students leave the writing too late. That's the biggest mistake.

Listening 29. A Psychology Experiment on Sleep Section 3

You will hear two psychology students, Emma and Jack, discussing an experiment they are designing.

mp3

Choose the correct letter, A, B or C.

1 Why do the students reject a ninety-minute nap?
  • A It would not improve memory.
  • B Volunteers would not give so much time.
  • C The room is not available for so long.
 ✔ B
2 What will the group that stays awake do?
  • A use their phones
  • B read a textbook
  • C watch a film without speech
 ✔ C
3 Why will participants be placed in groups at random?
  • A to save time
  • B to prevent tired people all choosing the nap
  • C because the tutor requires it
 ✔ B
4 Why will the students wait before testing?
  • A People may be confused just after waking.
  • B The room must be cleaned.
  • C The film must finish.
 ✔ A

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

5 Material to learn: a list of word ✔ pairs
6 Length of the nap: minutes ✔ 20/twenty
7 Minimum number of participants: ✔ 40/forty
8 Place for sleeping: the quiet room in the ✔ library
9 Time of the experiment: about o'clock ✔ 2/two
10 Participants must be told not to straight afterwards ✔ drive
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Emma: Jack, we need to finalise the design of our experiment today. The proposal is due on Monday.

Jack: I know. So we're testing whether a short sleep after learning improves memory, right?

Emma: Yes. I think we should keep it simple. Two groups. Everyone learns a list of word pairs, then one group has a nap and the other stays awake.

Jack: How long should the nap be?

Emma: The studies I read used ninety minutes, but that's too long for volunteers to give up. I'd say twenty minutes.

Jack: Would that be enough to show an effect?

Emma: One paper found an improvement after only six minutes, so I think so.

Jack: OK. And what does the other group do during that time?

Emma: That's the tricky part. If they use their phones, they'll be learning new things, which might interfere. I suggest they watch a nature film with no speech.

Jack: Good idea. How many participants do we need?

Emma: Doctor Lewis said at least forty, so twenty in each group.

Jack: We should put them in groups at random, not let them choose. Otherwise all the tired people will choose the nap.

Emma: Exactly. We could toss a coin for each person.

Jack: Where can people sleep, though? The psychology lab is so noisy.

Emma: I asked about that. We can use the quiet room in the library, as long as we book it a week ahead.

Jack: Great. And what time of day?

Emma: Early afternoon, about two o'clock. That's when people naturally feel sleepy.

Jack: Should we test them straight after the nap?

Emma: I'd wait ten minutes, so they're properly awake. People are often confused just after waking.

Jack: Fine. What about the ethics form?

Emma: We must tell participants not to drive straight afterwards, in case they still feel sleepy. And nobody under eighteen can take part.

Jack: I'll write the method section if you do the introduction.

Emma: Deal. Send me your part by Saturday.

Daniel: Thanks. I spent ages in the library.

Tutor: It shows. But that's also part of the problem. In places the essay becomes a summary of what other historians think. I can't hear your own argument.

Daniel: I wasn't sure I was allowed to disagree with them.

Tutor: Not only allowed, but expected. The question asks how far railways caused economic growth. You need to take a position and defend it.

Daniel: I think they were important, but less than people say. Canals had already reduced transport costs.

Tutor: Good. Say that in your first paragraph, and build the essay around it.

Daniel: OK. What about the structure?

Tutor: The third section, on working conditions in factories, is interesting, but it doesn't answer the question. I'd cut it completely.

Daniel: That's five hundred words gone.

Tutor: Which is useful, because you're four hundred words over the limit anyway. Use the space to add evidence. You make claims about the price of coal, for example, without any figures.

Daniel: I found a table of coal prices in one of the books. I could include that.

Tutor: Perfect. Then the conclusion. At the moment it introduces a new idea about steamships. A conclusion should only bring together what you've already said.

Daniel: Right. And my references?

Tutor: Mostly fine, but you've forgotten page numbers for the quotations. Check each one.

Daniel: I will. Is the writing style all right?

Tutor: It's clear, but some sentences are very long. One of them had seventy-two words. Break them up.

Daniel: When do you want the final version?

Tutor: The deadline is the thirtieth of January. If you send me a new plan by Friday, I'll look at it.
