Sam: And when is the test day?

Mei: The twenty-second of May, in the main hall. Every team's bridge is tested in front of the whole year.

Sam: No pressure, then.

Listening 38. The History of the Postal Service Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture about the development of postal services.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 In the Persian Empire, riders changed at stations along the royal road ✔ horses
2 Early postal systems were only for use ✔ government
3 Before 1840, the cost was paid by the person who the letter ✔ received
4 Rowland Hill worked as a ✔ teacher
5 His pamphlet was published in ✔ 1837/eighteen thirty-seven
6 He proposed a price of one for any distance ✔ penny
7 The first stamp was called the Penny ✔ Black
8 Within a year, the number of letters more than ✔ doubled
9 The international agreement of 1874 was signed in ✔ Switzerland
10 Today the number of is rising because of online shopping ✔ parcels
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: Today I want to look at something we tend to take for granted, the postal service, and to show how it shaped modern communication.

Lecturer: Organised systems for carrying messages are extremely old. The Persian Empire, around five hundred BC, had a royal road with stations where riders changed horses. A message could cross the empire in about a week. But this system, like the later Roman one, was for government use only. Ordinary people had to rely on travellers or merchants to carry their letters.

Lecturer: Public postal services developed in Europe from the sixteenth century, but they were expensive and complicated. In Britain before eighteen forty, the cost of a letter depended on the distance and on the number of sheets of paper. And, importantly, the cost was paid not by the sender but by the person who received it. Many people simply refused to accept letters they could not afford.

Lecturer: The reform came from a teacher called Rowland Hill. In eighteen thirty-seven he published a pamphlet arguing that the real cost of carrying a letter was tiny, and that most of the expense came from calculating and collecting the charges. He proposed a single low price of one penny for any distance, paid in advance by the sender. To show payment, he suggested a small piece of paper with glue on the back. This was the first postage stamp, known as the Penny Black, issued in May eighteen forty.

Lecturer: The results were dramatic. Within a year the number of letters sent in Britain more than doubled. Other countries copied the system quickly. Cheap post encouraged people to learn to read and write, helped businesses to grow, and allowed families separated by migration to stay in contact.

Lecturer: In eighteen seventy-four, an international agreement signed in Switzerland created what is now the Universal Postal Union, so that a stamp bought in one country would carry a letter to any other. Today, of course, the number of letters is falling because of email, but parcels are increasing rapidly as a result of online shopping.

Listening 39. How Bees Communicate Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on animal behaviour.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 Von Frisch began his experiments in the ✔ 1920s/nineteen twenties
2 He marked bees with small spots of ✔ paint
3 The round dance means food is within about metres ✔ 50/fifty
4 The round dance does not show ✔ direction
5 One second of waggling represents about one ✔ kilometre/kilometer/km
6 In the hive, bees use to represent the sun ✔ gravity
7 Other bees touch the dancer with their ✔ antennae
8 Von Frisch shared the Nobel Prize in ✔ 1973/nineteen seventy-three
9 Later experiments used small devices fixed to bees ✔ radar
10 Bees also dance when choosing a new ✔ home
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: In this lecture I'd like to describe one of the most remarkable communication systems in the animal world, the dance of the honeybee.

Lecturer: Beekeepers had noticed for centuries that when one bee found a rich source of food, many others soon arrived at the same place. The explanation was discovered by an Austrian scientist, Karl von Frisch, who began his experiments in the nineteen twenties. He marked individual bees with tiny spots of paint and watched them through a glass wall in the hive.

Lecturer: He found that a bee returning from flowers close to the hive performs what he called the round dance. She simply runs in circles. This tells the others that food is nearby, within about fifty metres, but it gives no direction.

Lecturer: For food that is farther away, the bee performs the waggle dance. She runs in a straight line while shaking her body from side to side, then circles back and repeats the run. The two key pieces of information are in that straight run. First, its length shows the distance. Roughly speaking, one second of waggling means one kilometre. Second, the angle of the run shows the direction. Inside the dark hive the bee dances on a vertical surface, and she uses gravity to represent the sun. A run straight up means fly towards the sun. A run at sixty degrees to the right of vertical means fly sixty degrees to the right of the sun.

Lecturer: The other bees follow the dancer closely, touching her with their antennae, and they also learn the smell of the flowers from her body.

Lecturer: Many scientists at first refused to believe that an insect could use such a symbolic language. Von Frisch's ideas were only fully accepted decades later, and he shared the Nobel Prize in nineteen seventy-three. Later experiments using tiny radar devices attached to bees confirmed that they really do fly to the place the dance indicates.

Lecturer: Bees also use the dance when choosing a new home. Scouts dance to advertise sites they have found, and the colony moves only when enough scouts agree. It is, in effect, a kind of democratic vote.

Listening 40. The Development of the Shipping Container Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on the history of world trade.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 Before containers, loading could make up of a voyage's cost ✔ half
2 Another common problem in ports was ✔ theft
3 McLean's business was in ✔ trucking
4 The Ideal X carried containers ✔ 58/fifty-eight
5 Loading cost fell to about cents a ton ✔ 16/sixteen
6 Dock workers went on ✔ strike
7 Standard container lengths are twenty and feet ✔ 40/forty
8 Factories moved especially to East ✔ Asia
9 About per cent of non-bulk goods travel in containers ✔ 90/ninety
10 In 2021 a ship blocked the Suez Canal for days ✔ 6/six
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: When we talk about globalisation, we usually think of the internet or of trade agreements. Today I want to argue that a simple steel box has been just as important.

Lecturer: Until the nineteen fifties, loading a ship was slow and costly. Goods arrived at the port in sacks, barrels and wooden crates of every size. Each item had to be carried on board by hand and packed carefully into the ship. A vessel could spend more time in port than at sea, and loading could account for half the total cost of a voyage. Theft was common too.

Lecturer: The man who changed this was not a sailor but an American trucking businessman, Malcolm McLean. He asked a simple question. Why unload the truck at all? Why not lift the whole body of the truck onto the ship? On the twenty-sixth of April nineteen fifty-six, his converted oil tanker, the Ideal X, sailed from New Jersey to Texas carrying fifty-eight containers. The cost of loading fell from nearly six dollars a ton to about sixteen cents.

Lecturer: Success was not immediate. Ports needed huge new cranes, and dock workers, who feared for their jobs, went on strike. Above all, different companies used boxes of different sizes. Only in the nineteen sixties were international standards agreed, the most common being lengths of twenty and forty feet. Standard boxes could move from ship to train to truck anywhere in the world.

Lecturer: The consequences were enormous. Transport became so cheap that it made sense to manufacture goods thousands of kilometres from the customer. Factories moved to countries with lower wages, especially in East Asia. Old ports in city centres, such as London and New York, declined, because container ships needed deep water and a great deal of space. New ports like Rotterdam and Singapore grew rapidly.

Lecturer: Today about ninety per cent of non-bulk goods travel in containers, and the largest ships carry more than twenty thousand of them. The system is efficient but also fragile, as we saw in twenty twenty-one, when a single ship blocked the Suez Canal for six days and delayed trade around the world.

Listening 41. Urban Heat Islands Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on the climate of cities.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 The effect was first measured in the city of ✔ London
2 The temperature difference is greatest at ✔ night
3 At night, a city centre can be up to degrees warmer ✔ 10/ten
4 Dark surfaces such as absorb sunlight ✔ asphalt
5 Trees cool the air by giving and releasing water ✔ shade
6 Tall buildings trap heat and block the ✔ wind
7 Air conditioners pump outside ✔ heat
8 The people most at risk in heat waves are the ✔ elderly
9 A white roof can be degrees cooler than a black one ✔ 30/thirty
10 Melbourne plans to nearly its tree cover ✔ double
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: Good morning. Today's topic is a problem that affects more than half of the world's population, the urban heat island.

Lecturer: The term describes the fact that cities are warmer than the countryside around them. The effect was first measured in London in the early nineteenth century by an amateur scientist called Luke Howard, who compared thermometer readings in the centre with those in nearby villages. Today the difference in a large city is typically between one and three degrees during the day. But the important point is that it is greatest at night, when a city centre can be as much as ten degrees warmer than the surrounding fields.

Lecturer: Why does this happen? There are four main causes. First, materials. Dark surfaces such as asphalt and roof tiles absorb sunlight during the day and release the heat slowly after sunset. Second, the lack of plants. Trees cool the air, both by giving shade and by releasing water through their leaves. Third, the shape of cities. Tall buildings on narrow streets trap heat and block the wind. And fourth, waste heat from vehicles, factories and, ironically, air conditioners, which cool the inside of a building by pumping heat outside.

Lecturer: The consequences are serious. During heat waves the death rate rises, especially among elderly people, because the body cannot recover when nights stay hot. Demand for electricity increases sharply, and air quality gets worse.

Lecturer: What can be done? The cheapest solution is to change the colour of surfaces. A white roof reflects most of the sunlight that falls on it, and can be thirty degrees cooler than a black one. New York has painted more than a million square metres of roofs white.

Lecturer: Planting trees is the second approach. Studies show that a street with mature trees can be several degrees cooler than one without. The city of Melbourne plans to almost double its tree cover by twenty forty.

Lecturer: Green roofs, covered with soil and plants, are a third option, though they cost more to build. Next week we'll look at how planners are using these ideas.

Listening 42. The History of the Pencil Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture about the history of an everyday object.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 Graphite was found near the village of Borrowdale in the fifteen ✔ sixties/60s
2 It was first used for marking ✔ sheep
3 People wrongly believed the material was ✔ lead
4 Early graphite sticks were wrapped in or sheepskin ✔ string
5 Conte's method was developed in the year ✔ 1795/seventeen ninety-five
6 He mixed powdered graphite with ✔ clay
7 More clay makes a pencil ✔ harder
8 In 1858 a was attached to the end of the pencil ✔ rubber
9 In China, yellow was associated with ✔ royalty
10 One pencil can draw a line about kilometres long ✔ 56/fifty-six
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: In this series on everyday technology, I want to turn today to an object so ordinary that we hardly notice it, the pencil.

Lecturer: Its story begins in the north of England in the fifteen sixties. According to tradition, a storm blew down a large tree near the village of Borrowdale, and under its roots shepherds found a black, shiny substance. They discovered it was excellent for marking their sheep. People believed the material was a kind of lead, which is why we still talk about the lead of a pencil. In fact it was graphite, a form of carbon, and pencils have never contained any lead at all.

Lecturer: The Borrowdale graphite was unusually pure and solid. It could be cut into thin sticks, which at first were wrapped in string or sheepskin, and later placed inside a wooden case. For two centuries England had almost a monopoly, and the graphite was so valuable that it was transported under armed guard.

Lecturer: The modern pencil was born out of war. In the seventeen nineties France was fighting Britain and could no longer import English graphite. An officer in the French army, Nicolas Conte, was asked to find a solution. In seventeen ninety-five he mixed powdered graphite of poor quality with clay, formed the mixture into rods and baked them in an oven. The method had an unexpected advantage. By changing the amount of clay, he could make the pencil harder or softer. More clay gives a harder, lighter line. That is the origin of the grades we still use, such as H for hard and B for black.

Lecturer: Two later improvements came from America. In eighteen fifty-eight Hymen Lipman patented a pencil with a rubber attached to the end. And the familiar yellow colour appeared in eighteen ninety. The best graphite then came from China, where yellow was associated with royalty, so manufacturers painted their finest pencils yellow as a sign of quality.

Lecturer: Why has the pencil survived the computer age? It is cheap, it needs no battery, and it works upside down, under water and even in space. A single pencil can draw a line roughly fifty-six kilometres long.

Listening 43. How Animals Sleep Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on sleep in the animal kingdom.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 Koalas sleep for up to hours a day ✔ 20/twenty
2 Eucalyptus leaves contain very little ✔ energy
3 Wild elephants were found to sleep about hours a night ✔ 2/two
4 Dolphins must come to the surface to ✔ breathe
5 Dolphins sleep with one of the brain at a time ✔ half
6 The two sides of a dolphin's brain change over after about hours ✔ 2/two
7 Ducks at the ends of a row keep one open ✔ eye
8 Frigatebirds can stay in the air for two ✔ months
9 While flying, frigatebirds sleep about minutes a day ✔ 40/forty
10 Sea otters wrap themselves in ✔ seaweed
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: Last week we looked at human sleep. Today I want to widen the picture and ask how other animals sleep, because the variety is astonishing.

Lecturer: Let's begin with the amount. The koala is one of the champions, sleeping for up to twenty hours a day. The reason is its diet. Eucalyptus leaves contain very little energy and take a long time to digest. At the other extreme are large plant-eaters that must spend most of their time feeding and watching for predators. A study published in two thousand and seventeen, which fitted wild African elephants with activity monitors, found that they slept for only about two hours a night, the shortest sleep recorded for any mammal.

Lecturer: Now consider animals that can never fully stop. Dolphins must come to the surface to breathe, so they cannot become completely unconscious. Their solution is to sleep with one half of the brain at a time. The other half stays awake, and the eye connected to it remains open. After about two hours the two sides change over.

Lecturer: Some birds do something similar. Ducks sleeping in a row are a good example. The birds at the ends of the row keep the outer eye open, watching for danger, while those in the middle close both eyes.

Lecturer: Perhaps the most remarkable case is the frigatebird, a seabird that can stay in the air over the ocean for two months without landing. Researchers who attached small recorders to these birds found that they do sleep while flying, but for only about forty minutes a day, in bursts of a few seconds. On land the same birds sleep for more than twelve hours.

Lecturer: Sea otters have a different problem, which is drifting away while asleep. They wrap themselves in seaweed, and mothers and young sometimes hold each other's paws.

Lecturer: What does all this tell us? Sleep is clearly so important that evolution has kept it even where it is dangerous or inconvenient. No animal that has been studied carefully manages without it entirely.

Listening 44. The Science of Habits Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on the psychology of habits.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 About per cent of daily actions are done out of habit ✔ 43/forty-three
2 The first part of the habit loop is the ✔ cue
3 The third part of the loop is the ✔ reward
4 The figure of twenty-one days comes from a book by a plastic ✔ surgeon
5 That book was published in ✔ 1960/nineteen sixty
6 Number of volunteers in the London study: ✔ 96/ninety-six
7 Average time to form a habit: days ✔ 66/sixty-six
8 A behaviour that took much longer to become automatic: ✔ exercise
9 To break a habit, change only the ✔ routine
10 A good time to start a habit is when you move or start a new job ✔ house
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: Today we're looking at habits, the things we do automatically, without making a decision. Research by the psychologist Wendy Wood suggests that about forty-three per cent of our daily actions are performed out of habit, while we are thinking about something else.

Lecturer: How does a habit work? Researchers describe a loop with three parts. First there is a cue, a signal that starts the behaviour. It might be a time of day, a place, or a feeling such as boredom. Second comes the routine, the behaviour itself. And third is the reward, which tells the brain that the loop is worth remembering. After enough repetitions, the cue alone produces a desire for the reward.

Lecturer: You have probably heard that it takes twenty-one days to form a habit. That figure comes from a book published in nineteen sixty by a plastic surgeon, Maxwell Maltz, who noticed that his patients needed about three weeks to get used to their new faces. It was an observation, not an experiment.

Lecturer: A proper study was carried out in two thousand and nine at University College London. Ninety-six volunteers chose a new daily behaviour, such as eating fruit with lunch, and reported each day how automatic it felt. On average, a habit took sixty-six days to form. But the range was enormous, from eighteen days to two hundred and fifty-four. Simple actions like drinking water became automatic quickly. Exercise took much longer. Encouragingly, missing a single day made no real difference.

Lecturer: What about breaking a bad habit? The evidence suggests that habits are not deleted, they are replaced. The most effective method is to keep the cue and the reward, and change only the routine. Someone who eats biscuits at four o'clock because they want a break might take a short walk instead.

Lecturer: Changing the environment also helps. Studies of students who moved to a different university found that they changed their habits more easily, because the old cues had disappeared. So if you want to start a new habit, a good moment is when you move house or begin a new job.

Listening 45. The Origins of Writing Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on the earliest writing system.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 The lecturer says writing was invented by ✔ accountants
2 The city of Uruk had about inhabitants ✔ 40000/40,000/forty thousand
3 Before writing, people counted with small clay ✔ tokens
4 The tokens were sealed inside a hollow clay ✔ ball
5 The first written signs were simple ✔ pictures
6 Scribes pressed the end of a cut into the clay ✔ reed
7 Cuneiform is named after the Latin word for ✔ wedge
8 Later, signs began to represent as well as things ✔ sounds
9 Training as a scribe took about years ✔ 12/twelve
10 Cuneiform was deciphered in the century ✔ nineteenth/19th
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: When we think about why writing was invented, we imagine poetry, or laws, or letters. The truth is less romantic. Writing was invented by accountants.

Lecturer: The story begins in Mesopotamia, in what is now southern Iraq. By about three thousand three hundred BC the city of Uruk had perhaps forty thousand inhabitants, and its temples controlled huge stores of grain, sheep and beer. Somebody had to keep records.

Lecturer: For thousands of years before this, people in the region had used small clay tokens for counting. One shape stood for a measure of grain, another for a jar of oil. To prevent cheating, the tokens for a delivery were sealed inside a hollow clay ball. The problem, of course, was that you had to break the ball to check what was in it. So people began pressing the tokens into the outside of the ball before sealing it. And eventually someone realised that the marks alone were enough. The ball became a flat tablet, and the tokens were no longer needed.

Lecturer: The first signs were simple pictures. A head of barley meant barley. But pictures are slow to draw in wet clay, so scribes began pressing the end of a cut reed into the surface instead. This made marks shaped like wedges. The Latin word for wedge is cuneus, and so we call the script cuneiform.

Lecturer: The next step was the decisive one. Signs began to represent sounds as well as things. This allowed scribes to write names and, eventually, whole sentences of the spoken language. The number of signs fell from more than a thousand to about six hundred. That was still a great many to learn, and training as a scribe took around twelve years in a school known as a tablet house.

Lecturer: Cuneiform was used for three thousand years, for a dozen different languages. It recorded contracts, medical texts and literature, including the story of Gilgamesh. Then it was forgotten completely, until scholars deciphered it in the nineteenth century. Because clay survives fire, hundreds of thousands of tablets still exist, most of them, I'm afraid, receipts.

Listening 46. Plants of the Desert Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on how plants survive in deserts.

mp3

Complete the notes. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer.

1 A desert receives less than millimetres of rain a year ✔ 250/two hundred and fifty
2 A cactus's spines are really ✔ leaves
3 The saguaro's stem is folded so that it can ✔ expand
4 After a storm, a large saguaro can take up about litres of water ✔ 750/seven hundred and fifty
5 The saguaro's roots are but wide ✔ shallow
6 A cactus opens its tiny holes only at ✔ night
7 A saguaro can live for two ✔ centuries
8 Mesquite roots can go deeper than metres ✔ 50/fifty
9 Desert flower seeds are protected by a coat ✔ chemical
10 The chemical is washed away only by heavy ✔ rain
Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: A desert is defined as a region that receives less than two hundred and fifty millimetres of rain a year. For a plant, that creates one overwhelming problem, how to obtain water and how to keep it. Today I'll describe three strategies that desert plants have evolved.

Lecturer: The first strategy is storage, and the best-known example is the cactus. Look at a saguaro cactus from the deserts of Arizona. It has no leaves. Its spines are in fact leaves that have changed over millions of years. They lose almost no water, they protect the plant from thirsty animals, and they even provide a little shade. The stem of the saguaro is folded, like an accordion, so that it can expand. After a storm, a large plant can take up about seven hundred and fifty litres of water in a few days. Its roots are shallow, but they spread out as far as the plant is tall, to catch rain before it disappears.

Lecturer: Cacti also breathe differently from other plants. Most plants open the tiny holes in their surface during the day to take in carbon dioxide, and they lose water as they do so. A cactus opens them only at night, when the air is cool, stores the gas, and uses it for photosynthesis the next day. The price is slow growth. A saguaro may be only a few centimetres tall after ten years, though it can live for two centuries.

Lecturer: The second strategy is to reach water that other plants cannot. The mesquite tree sends its roots down to water lying deep underground. Roots have been found at depths of more than fifty metres.

Lecturer: The third strategy is simply to avoid the dry season. Many small desert flowers live for only a few weeks. Their seeds can lie in the soil for years, protected by a chemical coat that prevents them from growing. Only heavy rain washes the chemical away. Then the desert suddenly turns into a carpet of colour, the plants flower, produce new seeds and die, all before the ground dries out again.

Lecturer: Next time, we'll consider desert animals.

Listening 47. Lifts and the Rise of the Skyscraper Section 4

You will hear part of a lecture on the history of architecture.

mp3

1 The Romans used lifts in the Colosseum to raise ✔ animals

5 The first passenger lift was installed in a department ✔ store

Транскрипт (открывай только после проверки)

Lecturer: If I asked you which invention made the modern city possible, you might say steel, or concrete. I'd like to suggest another candidate, the lift, or elevator.

Lecturer: Lifting platforms are very old. The Romans used them in the Colosseum to raise animals into the arena. Nineteenth-century factories and warehouses had steam-powered lifts for goods. But nobody would travel in one by choice, for a simple reason. If the rope broke, the platform fell.

Lecturer: The man who solved this was an American mechanic, Elisha Otis. He designed a safety brake, a spring that pushed metal teeth into the guide rails on each side if the rope lost its tension. In eighteen fifty-four, at a great exhibition in New York, he demonstrated it in dramatic style. He stood on a platform high above the crowd and ordered the rope to be cut. The platform dropped a few centimetres, and stopped.

Lecturer: The first passenger lift using his brake was installed three years later in a department store in New York. It was a building of only five storeys, and the lift travelled at less than a kilometre an hour. But the principle was established.

Lecturer: Consider what this meant for buildings. Before the lift, few were higher than six floors, because that was as far as people were willing to climb. And the top floors were the cheapest, occupied by servants and the poor. The lift turned that upside down. Suddenly the highest floors, with light, quiet and a view, became the most desirable and the most expensive.

Lecturer: Height then depended on structure. Steel frames, first used in Chicago in the eighteen eighties, allowed walls to become thin. Electric lifts arrived in the same decade and were faster and cleaner than steam.

Lecturer: Today's limits are different. In very tall buildings the steel cables become too heavy to support their own weight, which restricts a single lift to about five hundred metres. Engineers are now using cables of carbon fibre, which weigh far less. And in the tallest towers, lifts take up so much floor space that designers use double-deck cars, serving two floors at once.

Host: So what's the secret? There are three parts to the answer. The first is water, or rather the lack of it. Honey is only about seventeen per cent water. Bacteria and moulds need moisture to grow, and when they land in honey the sugar actually pulls the water out of them, and they die.

Host: The second reason is that honey is acidic. Its acidity is similar to that of orange juice, and most microbes can't survive in that.

Host: The third reason is my favourite, because it involves the bees themselves. Nectar from flowers is mostly water, as much as eighty per cent. That enzyme produces tiny amounts of hydrogen peroxide, the same chemical used to clean wounds. Then the bees fan the nectar with their wings to evaporate the water, and finally they seal each cell with wax.

Host: That, by the way, explains why honey has been used as medicine for thousands of years. People put it on cuts and burns long before anyone knew what bacteria were, and some hospitals use a special medical honey today.

Host: One warning. Honey only lasts if the jar is closed. Leave it open in a damp kitchen and it absorbs water from the air, and then it can spoil. And if your honey goes hard and cloudy, don't throw it out. It has only crystallised. Stand the jar in warm water and it will turn liquid again.

Host: Finally, a number that changed how I look at my breakfast. In her whole life, a single worker bee makes about one twelfth of a teaspoon of honey.

Host: Thanks, Olivia. Keep the questions coming.

Host: You're listening to Everyday Genius, the show about the hidden design in ordinary things. I'm Rachel. Today, an invention you see perhaps fifty times a day and never think about, the barcode.

Host: Our story starts in nineteen forty-eight in Philadelphia. A supermarket manager visited a local college and begged the dean to find a way of recording products automatically at the checkout. He realised that instead of dots and dashes, he could use thin lines and thick lines.

Host: The two friends received a patent in nineteen fifty-two. Interestingly, their design was not rectangular. It was circular, like a target, so that it could be read from any direction.

Silver died in nineteen sixty-three, before a single product had been scanned.

Host: Two inventions changed everything. The laser, in nineteen sixty, provided the light. The microchip provided cheap computing. In the early seventies American supermarkets agreed on a standard code, designed by an engineer at I B M called George Laurer. He chose a rectangle, because the round design smeared when it was printed.

Host: The first scan took place on the twenty-sixth of June nineteen seventy-four, at a supermarket in Ohio. The product was a packet of chewing gum, which cost sixty-seven cents. That packet is now in a museum in Washington.

Host: Shops were slow to adopt scanners, because they were expensive. But the benefits were enormous. Queues moved faster, and for the first time a shop knew exactly what it had sold. Today barcodes are scanned around ten billion times a day.

Host: Welcome to Blue Planet Stories. I'm Tom, and today's episode is about one of the strangest scientific experiments ever carried out. Nobody planned it, and its equipment was made for the bath.

Host: In January nineteen ninety-two, a container ship was crossing the Pacific Ocean from Hong Kong to the United States when it ran into a violent storm. Twelve containers were washed overboard. Host: Now, to most people that's a small accident. To an oceanographer called Curtis Ebbesmeyer, it was a gift. Scientists who study ocean currents normally release five hundred or a thousand floating bottles and hope that a few are found. Here were tens of thousands of bright objects, all released at a known place at a known time.

Host: Ten months later, the first toys washed up on the coast of Alaska, about three thousand two hundred kilometres from where they had started. Ebbesmeyer built a network of people who walk beaches, and asked them to report every toy they found. With a colleague, he put the data into a computer model of the currents.

Host: The model predicted that some of the toys would circle the North Pacific, and they did. That great circular current takes about three years to complete one round. Others, the model said, would drift north through the Bering Strait and become frozen in the Arctic ice. The ice moves slowly across the top of the world, and the prediction was that the toys would emerge years later in the Atlantic. Sure enough, in the early two thousands, faded ducks were reported on the east coast of North America, and later in Britain.

Host: There's a serious side to the story. After years at sea the toys had lost their colour. The ducks and beavers had turned white. But the plastic itself was still whole. It showed just how long plastic lasts in the ocean, and how far it can travel.

Host: And if you find one of those ducks on a beach today, keep it. Collectors have paid up to a thousand dollars for one.

Настоящие подкасты для ежедневной практики

Записи выше озвучены синтезатором. Чтобы привыкать к живой речи, слушай бесплатные подкасты на их сайтах — по одному выпуску в день.

  • BBC Learning English — 6 Minute English: 6 минут, два ведущих, есть транскрипт и словарь. Лучший старт для уровня 5.5–6.5.
  • BBC Learning English — The English We Speak: 3 минуты, разговорные выражения для Speaking.
  • BBC World Service — Global News Podcast: новости в естественном темпе, разные акценты.
  • BBC World Service — The History Hour и Discovery: история и наука — близко к Section 4.
  • BBC Radio 4 — Inside Science и In Our Time: академические обсуждения, уровень 7+.
  • TED Talks Daily: лекции 10–15 минут, американский акцент.
  • ABC — The Science Show: австралийский акцент, который тоже встречается на экзамене.

Как слушать с пользой: первый раз без текста и запиши 5–10 ключевых фактов (цифры, имена, даты); второй раз с транскриптом; выпиши 5 новых выражений.

Writing

Вопросы ниже собраны из отчётов кандидатов за август–ноябрь 2026 и из сборников Cambridge IELTS 19–20. Формулировки пересказаны близко к экзаменационным. Официального списка «будущих вопросов» у IELTS нет: темы повторяются, поэтому это лучший материал для тренировки, но не гарантия.

Формат и время

ЗаданиеОбъёмВремяВесЧто оценивают
Task 1 (Academic) — описание графика, таблицы, карты или процессаот 150 слов20 мин1/3Task Achievement, Coherence & Cohesion, Lexical Resource, Grammar
Task 1 (General Training) — письмоот 150 слов20 мин1/3то же; важен тон (formal / informal)
Task 2 — эссеот 250 слов40 мин2/3Task Response, Coherence & Cohesion, Lexical Resource, Grammar

Структура Task 1 (Academic)

  1. Introduction — перефразируй условие одним предложением: The line graph illustrates / compares / gives information about …
  2. Overview — 2 главные тенденции без цифр: Overall, it is clear that … while … Без overview выше 5.0 за Task Achievement не поставят.
  3. Body 1 и Body 2 — детали с цифрами, сгруппированные по смыслу (например, что росло и что падало). Сравнивай, а не перечисляй.

Никогда не пиши своё мнение и причины — только то, что видно на графике.

Полезная лексика для Task 1

  • Рост: rise, increase, grow, climb, go up; сильный — surge, soar, rocket; a sharp / steady / gradual / slight increase
  • Падение: fall, decline, decrease, drop, dip; plummet, plunge
  • Стабильность: remain stable / steady, level off, plateau at, stay at around
  • Пики: peak at, reach a high of, hit a low of, bottom out at
  • Сравнение: twice as high as, three times the figure for, considerably higher than, by contrast, whereas, while
  • Приблизительность: approximately, roughly, just under, just over, nearly, about a third, the vast majority
  • Процесс: first of all, in the first stage, once X has been done, after that, subsequently, finally; пассив — the clay is dug out, is then mixed with…
  • Карты: was replaced by, was converted into, was extended, was demolished, was relocated to; to the north of, adjacent to, in the south-west corner

Task 1 Academic — что реально попадалось (август–ноябрь 2026)

  1. Line graph — уровень занятости мужчин и женщин 18–34 лет в Австралии, 1976–2011 (25 сен и 28 окт 2026)
  2. Table — число автомобилей на 1 000 жителей в восьми странах, 1990 и 2000 (25 окт 2026)
  3. Map — план этажа музея в 1998 и 2008 (18 окт 2026)
  4. Maps — жилой квартал сейчас и план будущей застройки (14 окт 2026)
  5. Table — использование интернета взрослыми в одной европейской стране, 2006–2010 (12 авг 2026)
  6. Process — производство керамических горшков (1 авг 2026)
  7. Bar chart — продажи автопроизводителя по регионам, 2006–2010, % (июль 2026)
  8. Bar chart — средний возраст выхода на пенсию мужчин и женщин в семи странах, 2004 и 2008 (июль 2026)
  9. Chart — расходы домохозяйств на товары и услуги, 1995–2005 (июль 2026)
  10. Chart + table — средний заработок по отраслям (июль 2026)
  11. Table — временные трудовые мигранты в четырёх странах, 2003 и 2006 (март 2026)
  12. Graphs — количество выпущенных фильмов и продажи билетов, 1996 и 2000 (апрель 2026)

Ниже — 8 тренировочных заданий на эти же темы. Цифры в них учебные (придуманы для практики), а не с экзамена.

Practice Task 1.1 — Line graph

The graph below shows the employment rates of men and women aged 18–34 in Australia between 1976 and 2011. Summarise the information by selecting and reporting the main features, and make comparisons where relevant. Write at least 150 words.
Employment rate (%)19761981198619911996200120062011
Men8986847879808381
Women5256616366697273
Model answer (Band 8+), 178 words

The line graph compares the proportions of Australian men and women aged 18 to 34 who were in work over a 35-year period from 1976 to 2011.

Overall, although men were more likely to be employed throughout the period, the gap between the sexes narrowed considerably, as the female rate rose steadily while the male rate fell slightly.

In 1976, almost nine in ten young men (89%) had a job, compared with just over half of young women (52%). Male employment then declined gradually to 84% in 1986 before dropping more sharply to a low of 78% in 1991. After that it recovered slowly, reaching 83% in 2006, and ended the period at 81%, eight percentage points below its starting level.

By contrast, the figure for women increased in every five-year period. It climbed from 52% to 63% by 1991 and continued to rise to 72% in 2006, before levelling off at 73% in 2011. As a result, the difference between the two groups shrank from 37 percentage points at the beginning to only 8 by the end.

The table below shows the number of motor vehicles per 1,000 inhabitants in eight countries in 1990 and 2000. Summarise the information by selecting and reporting the main features, and make comparisons where relevant.
Vehicles per 1,000 peopleUSAItalyGermanyJapanPolandBrazilChinaIndia
19907525294854681688754
2000779656572570312118128

Подсказка: сгруппируй страны — высокий уровень (США, Италия, Германия, Япония), средний (Польша, Бразилия), низкий (Китай, Индия). Отметь, что рост был везде, а самый быстрый относительный рост — в Польше и Китае.

Practice Task 1.6 — Process

The diagram below shows the process by which ceramic pots are manufactured. Summarise the information by selecting and reporting the main features, and make comparisons where relevant.
  1. Raw clay is dug out of the ground by an excavator and transported by lorry to the factory.
  2. The clay is crushed and passed through a sieve to remove stones.
  3. Water is added in a mixing tank; the liquid clay is poured into moulds.
  4. The moulds are left for 4–6 hours; the pots are then removed and smoothed by hand.
  5. Pots are placed in a drying room (2 days, 40°C).
  6. First firing in a kiln at 1,000°C.
  7. Pots are painted and coated with glaze.
  8. Second firing at 1,200°C; cooling for 24 hours.
  9. Quality check → packaging → delivery to shops.

Подсказка: в overview укажи число этапов и где процесс начинается и заканчивается (Overall, there are nine main stages, beginning with the extraction of clay and ending with the delivery of finished pots). Используй пассив и связки последовательности.

Task 1 General Training — письма (2026)

  1. Formal. You use your local library regularly. Write a letter to the librarian. Say what you like about the library, describe something you are not satisfied with, and suggest improvements.
  2. Formal. You want to rent a car for a holiday. Write to the rental agency. Explain what type of car you need, give the dates, and ask about the cost and conditions.
  3. Formal. You bought some clothes online but were not satisfied. Write to the company. Describe what you bought, explain the problem, and say why you need a replacement urgently.
  4. Formal. You are due to move into a rented apartment but need to delay. Write to the landlord. Explain why, say when you can move in, and ask what can be arranged.
  5. Semi-formal. Write to your manager asking for a salary increase. Describe your current role, explain why you deserve the increase, and say what you plan to do in future.
  6. Informal. You have recently moved to a new home. Write to an English-speaking friend. Explain why you moved, describe the new place, and invite them to visit.
  7. Informal. A friend has got engaged. Write to them. Say how you found out, how you feel about the news, and suggest meeting to celebrate.
  8. Informal. You are moving away. Write to your neighbours to say goodbye, thank them for their help, and say how to stay in touch.
  9. Informal. Invite a friend to a photography exhibition: say what it is, why they would enjoy it, and suggest arrangements.

Тон: formal — Dear Sir or Madam … Yours faithfully (имя неизвестно) или Dear Mr Brown … Yours sincerely; informal — Hi Anna … Best wishes / Take care. Каждому из трёх пунктов — свой абзац.

Task 2 — недавно попадалось на экзамене (с датами)

  1. Museums and historical sites are mainly visited by tourists rather than local people. Why is this the case? What can be done to attract more local people? (24 окт и 3 ноя 2026 · causes + solutions)
  2. Some people think that languages should be taught in small classes, while others believe that the number of students in a class does not matter. Discuss both views and give your own opinion. (30 сен, 14 окт, 4 ноя 2026 · discussion)
  3. The extinction of some animal species, as happened with dinosaurs and dodos, is a natural process, and people should not try to prevent it. To what extent do you agree or disagree? (7 сен, 2 ноя 2026 · opinion)
  4. Because people around the world watch the same films and television programmes and use the same media, countries are becoming more and more alike. Do the advantages of this outweigh the disadvantages? (21 авг, 28 окт 2026 · advantages / disadvantages)
  5. In many countries, fewer and fewer people use bicycles as a means of transport. Why is this happening? What could be done to encourage people to cycle more? (27 авг, 21 окт 2026 · causes + solutions)
  6. Some people believe that the best way to improve road safety is to raise the minimum legal age for driving cars and motorbikes. To what extent do you agree or disagree? (31 июл 2026 · opinion)

Task 2 — банк актуальных вопросов 2026 по темам

Education

  1. Some people think that the values children learn from their family and parents have more influence on their future success than the knowledge and skills they learn at school. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  2. Since higher education mainly benefits individuals rather than society, students should pay the full cost of their university studies. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  3. Competition for university places is increasing in many countries. Why is this happening? Is it a positive or negative development?
  4. Some people think young people should go to university after school, while others believe they should start work straight away. Discuss both views and give your opinion.
  5. Children can learn effectively by watching television, so they should be encouraged to watch it both at home and at school. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  6. Some people say all parents should be required to attend parenting courses. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  7. It is important to give children the chance to act and make decisions independently. To what extent do you agree or disagree?

Work & economy

  1. If people could choose between a life without work and a life spent working most of the time, they would always choose not to work. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  2. Employers should give their staff at least four weeks' holiday a year in order to make them better at their jobs. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  3. Sport has become a big business with high earnings for professional sportspeople. Is this a positive or negative development?
  4. Working for a multinational company is the best way to understand other cultures. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  5. Advertisements, whether amusing or annoying, do not really influence what people buy. To what extent do you agree or disagree?

Environment & transport

  1. Some cities have vehicle-free days, when private cars are banned from the centre. Do the advantages outweigh the disadvantages?
  2. Some people think governments should be responsible for reducing pollution from transport, while others think individuals should be. Discuss both views and give your opinion.
  3. A great deal of food is wasted around the world. Why does this happen? How can the problem be solved?
  4. Some people believe it is impossible to be a responsible tourist who respects the local culture and environment. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  5. Organised tours to remote places and communities are increasingly popular. Is this a positive or negative development for the local people and the environment?
  6. Some people think animals may be used in any way that benefits humans; others think this is wrong. Discuss both views and give your opinion.

Society, culture & technology

  1. Some people think that anyone can create art, while others believe it requires special talent. Discuss both views and give your opinion.
  2. Some people think governments should fund artists such as painters and musicians; others think this is a waste of public money. Discuss both views and give your opinion.
  3. Live performances such as theatre and concerts are no longer necessary because people can watch them on television or online. To what extent do you agree or disagree?
  4. More and more knowledge is stored on the internet instead of in books. Do the advantages outweigh the disadvantages?
  5. The number of crimes committed by young people is rising in many countries. Why is this? How should young offenders be dealt with?
  6. Online gambling apps should be banned rather than made freely available. To what extent do you agree or disagree?

Task 2 — темы из Cambridge IELTS 19 и 20

Темы пересказаны; точные формулировки — в самих книгах.

  1. Cambridge 20, Test 1. Access to clean water is a basic human right, so every home should have a water supply provided free of charge. Agree / disagree.
  2. Cambridge 20, Test 2. In many countries school children have summer holidays of two months or more. What is the value of long holidays? What are the arguments for making them shorter? Two-part question.
  3. Cambridge 20, Test 3. Some people have decided to fly less or stop flying altogether for environmental reasons. Do the environmental benefits outweigh the disadvantages for individuals and businesses? Opinion.
  4. Cambridge 20, Test 4. The clothes people wear are increasingly influenced by global trends. How has this changed the way people dress? Is it a positive or negative development? Two-part question.
  5. Cambridge 19. Supermarkets now sell food from all over the world. Positive or negative development?
  6. Cambridge 19. Some think competition at work, at school and in daily life is good; others think we should cooperate instead. Discuss both views.
  7. Cambridge 19. It is important for everyone, including young people, to save money for the future. Agree / disagree.
  8. Cambridge 19. The working week should be shorter and people should have a longer weekend. Agree / disagree.

Как писать Task 2: схемы по типам

ТипКак узнатьСтруктура (4 абзаца)
OpinionTo what extent do you agree or disagree?Intro + чёткая позиция → Довод 1 + пример → Довод 2 (или уступка + опровержение) → Вывод
DiscussionDiscuss both views and give your opinionIntro + твоя позиция → Взгляд A → Взгляд B (тот, с которым согласен, — вторым) → Вывод
Advantages / disadvantagesDo the advantages outweigh the disadvantages?Intro + ответ «да/нет» → Более слабая сторона → Более сильная сторона → Вывод
Causes + solutionsWhy…? What can be done?Intro → 2 причины → 2 решения (связаны с причинами) → Вывод
Two-part / positive–negativeДва прямых вопросаIntro (ответ на оба) → Ответ на вопрос 1 → Ответ на вопрос 2 → Вывод

Фразы

  • Intro: It is often argued that… / There is an ongoing debate about whether… / In recent years, … has become increasingly common.
  • Позиция: I firmly believe that… / While I accept that…, I would argue that… / In my view, the benefits clearly outweigh the drawbacks.
  • Доводы: The main reason for this is that… / A further point is that… / This is largely because…
  • Пример: For instance, … / A clear illustration of this is… / This can be seen in…
  • Уступка: Admittedly, … / It is true that… However, … / Although some would claim that…, this ignores the fact that…
  • Вывод: In conclusion, although…, I am convinced that… / To sum up, …

Частые ошибки, которые стоят баллов

  • Ответ не на все части вопроса (например, обсудил только одну точку зрения) — потолок 5.0 за Task Response.
  • Меньше 250 слов; заученные «шаблонные» абзацы не по теме.
  • Позиция меняется от вступления к выводу.
  • Списки доводов без развития: лучше 2 идеи с объяснением и примером, чем 5 коротких.

Образец эссе (Band 8+)

Museums and historical sites are mainly visited by tourists rather than local people. Why is this the case? What can be done to attract more local people?
Model answer, 289 words

In many cities, the people queuing outside famous museums and monuments are far more likely to be foreign visitors than residents. This essay will examine why local people tend to stay away and suggest how they might be encouraged to return.

There are two main reasons for this pattern. The first is simply familiarity. People who have grown up near a castle or gallery often feel they can go at any time, so the visit is postponed indefinitely, whereas a tourist with only three days in the city treats it as a priority. The second reason is that many such places are designed with outsiders in mind. Ticket prices are set at a level tourists will accept, permanent displays rarely change, and opening hours coincide with the working day. A resident who visited once on a school trip therefore has little incentive, and little opportunity, to go again.

Fortunately, both problems can be addressed. To begin with, institutions could offer residents free or heavily discounted entry, as several European cities already do, together with late opening on at least one evening a week so that working people can attend. In addition, museums need to give locals a reason to come back by staging temporary exhibitions, talks and family workshops that change regularly. If these events draw on the history of the surrounding community, for example by displaying residents' own photographs and stories, people are more likely to feel that the museum belongs to them rather than to visitors.

In conclusion, local people neglect their own heritage mainly because it is always available and seldom aimed at them. Lower prices, more convenient hours and a changing programme rooted in the community would go a long way towards bringing them back.

Speaking

Темы актуального сезона: сентябрь–декабрь 2026. Списки составлены по сообщениям кандидатов (официально IELTS вопросы не публикует), поэтому на экзамене формулировка может немного отличаться. Карточки с пометкой NEW появились в этом сезоне.

Формат (11–14 минут)

ЧастьВремяЧто происходитКак отвечать
Part 14–5 минВопросы о тебе: работа/учёба, дом + 2 бытовые темы2–3 предложения: ответ + причина + пример
Part 23–4 минКарточка: 1 мин на подготовку, 1–2 мин монологГовори до остановки экзаменатора; иди по пунктам карточки
Part 34–5 минАбстрактные вопросы по теме карточки4–6 предложений: мнение → причина → пример → оговорка

Критерии (по 25%): Fluency & Coherence · Lexical Resource · Grammatical Range & Accuracy · Pronunciation.

Полезные фразы

  • Выиграть время: That's an interesting question. / I've never really thought about it, but… / Let me think…
  • Мнение: As far as I'm concerned… / I'd say that… / From my point of view… / I tend to think…
  • Пример: For instance… / Take my brother, for example… / A good example of this is…
  • Сравнение прошлого и настоящего: Compared with the past… / People used to…, whereas nowadays… / It's much more common now to…
  • Оговорка: Having said that… / It depends on… / On the other hand… / That's not always the case, though.
  • Part 2, начало и конец: I'd like to talk about… / The reason I chose this is… / All in all, it was… / Looking back, I think…
  • Если не понял: Sorry, could you repeat that, please? / Do you mean…?

Схема ответа Part 2 на 2 минуты

  1. 10 сек — что/кто это (I'd like to tell you about…)
  2. 30 сек — фон: когда, где, как ты об этом узнал
  3. 50 сек — детали: что происходило (Past Simple / Past Continuous / Past Perfect)
  4. 30 сек — чувства и почему это важно; что изменилось (If I hadn't…, I would never have…)

Part 1 — темы сентября–декабря 2026

Обязательные (одна из них будет всегда)

Work or Study. Are you working or studying? · Why did you choose your subject / job? · Do you enjoy it? · Do you prefer studying in the morning or afternoon? · What technology do you use when studying?

Home and Accommodation. Do you live in a house or an apartment? · Which part of your home do you like the most? · Do you plan to live there for a long time? · Would you prefer a house or an apartment in the future?

Hometown. Where is your hometown? · Do you like it? · What is it famous for? · Has it changed much?

The Area You Live In. Do you like the area where you live? · Do you know your neighbours? · Is your area noisy? · Has the area changed recently?

The City You Live In. What city do you live in? · Do you like living there? · Would you recommend your city to others?

Сменные темы сезона

Teacher. Do you have a favourite teacher? · How did your favourite teacher help you? · Are you still in touch with your old teachers? · Would you like to become a teacher?

Social Media. When did you start using social media? · Do you think you spend too much time on it? · What do people usually do on social media? · Have you ever posted anything online?

Music. Do you like music? · What kind of music do you enjoy? · Do you prefer happy or sad music? · Does happy music make you excited?

Singing. Do you like singing? · Did you sing as a child? · Do you sing in the car?

Headphones. Do you use headphones often? · Are headphones useful? · When should people avoid using headphones?

Tidiness. Do you like keeping things tidy? · Were you tidy as a child? · Are you a tidy person now?

Websites. What kinds of websites do you visit? · What is your favourite website? · Have websites changed in recent years? · Would you like to have your own website?

Computers and Tablets. Do you use a computer regularly? · Do you prefer computers or tablets? · When did you first start using computers?

Watches. Do you wear a watch? · Have you ever received a watch as a gift? · Why do people wear expensive watches? · Is wearing a watch still important?

Shopping. Do you enjoy shopping? · How often do you go shopping? · Do you prefer online or in-store shopping? · Do you compare prices?

Street Markets. Do you visit street markets? · What do people normally buy there? · Do you prefer street markets or shopping malls?

Clothing. What kind of clothes do you normally wear? · Do you prefer casual or smart clothes? · Do you like wearing T-shirts? · Do you spend a long time choosing clothes?

Cars. Did you enjoy travelling by car as a child? · What type of cars do you like? · Do you prefer being a driver or a passenger? · What do you do in a traffic jam?

Parks. Did you enjoy going to parks as a child? · Do you still visit parks? · Should cities have more parks?

Science. Do you like science? · Which area of science interests you most? · Do you watch science programmes?

Outer Space and Stars. Have you learned about space before? · Would you like to learn more about it? · Do you enjoy science-fiction films? · Would you like to travel into space?

History. Do you like history? · Did you like history at school? · Have you visited a historical museum? · When did you last read about history?

Old Buildings. Are there old buildings in your city? · Should old buildings be protected? · Would you prefer an old or a modern home? · Is there an old building you'd like to visit?

Feeling Bored. Do you often feel bored? · What kinds of things bore you? · What do you do when you're bored? · Did you find school boring?

Mirrors. Do you often look in the mirror? · Have you ever bought a mirror? · Do you carry a mirror? · Would you decorate a room with mirrors?

Jokes and Comedies. Are you good at telling jokes? · Do your friends like telling jokes? · Do you like watching comedy? · Are comedy shows popular in your country?

Dream and Ambition. What was your childhood dream? · Has your dream changed? · What is your dream job? · Are you ambitious?

Morning Routines. What do you do immediately after waking up? · Is breakfast important to you? · Do you like getting up early? · Has your morning routine changed?

Evening Time. Do you prefer mornings or evenings? · What do you usually do in the evening? · Are your evenings different from when you were a child? · Are your weekend evenings different?

Sports Programmes. Do you like watching sports programmes? · Do you prefer watching live sport? · Who do you normally watch sport with? · Have you watched a match in a stadium?

Films and Cinema. What types of films do you like? · How often do you watch films? · Do you prefer watching films at home or at the cinema? · Do you go to the cinema with friends?

Collecting Things. Do you collect anything? · Have you kept anything from childhood? · Why do people collect things?

Образец ответа Part 1 (Band 8)

Do you often feel bored? — Not very often, to be honest. I've got quite a full timetable at university, so there isn't much room for boredom. If it does happen, it's usually when I'm stuck somewhere with nothing to do, like waiting in a long queue at the bank.

Do you wear a watch? — I used to, but I stopped a couple of years ago because I check the time on my phone anyway. Having said that, I still keep the watch my grandfather gave me, and I put it on for special occasions.

Part 2 и Part 3 — 50 cue cards сезона

Тема карточки — из отчётов сезона; пункты «You should say» и вопросы Part 3 составлены в стандартном экзаменационном формате для тренировки.

People

1. Describe a person who is much older than you that you admire.NEW
You should say:
  • who this person is
  • how you know them
  • what they have done in their life
  • and explain why you admire them
Part 3:
  • What can young people learn from older generations?
  • Are old people respected enough in your country?
  • Why do some young people ignore advice from the elderly?

Part 3:

  • What skills should every child learn before leaving school?
  • Is it harder to learn new skills as an adult?

Образец ответа Part 2 (Band 8)

Describe a time when you saved some money to buy something special.

I'd like to talk about the time I saved up for my first proper laptop, which was about three years ago, when I was in my final year of school.

At that time I was using an old family computer that froze every ten minutes, and I'd just become interested in video editing, so I desperately needed something faster. The model I had my eye on cost roughly six hundred dollars, which was a fortune for a teenager, and my parents made it clear that if I wanted it, I'd have to earn it myself.

So I came up with a plan. I started tutoring two younger kids from my neighbourhood in maths, twice a week, and I also cut back on little things like snacks and taxi rides. I kept the cash in an envelope at the back of my drawer and wrote the running total on it, which was surprisingly motivating. It took me almost eight months, and there were moments when I was tempted to spend the money on a new phone instead, but I managed to resist.

When I finally walked into the shop and paid in cash, I felt incredibly proud. Honestly, the laptop itself wasn't the best part. What mattered was knowing that I'd bought it with money I had earned. Looking back, I think that experience taught me more about patience and budgeting than any lesson at school, and I still take better care of that laptop than of anything my parents have ever bought me.
